# The Compounding Engine

The strongest form of capability is not one-time capability. It is capability that leaves behind assets that lower the cost or raise the quality of future action.

## Core loop

> **Action → Result → Return → Asset → Reinvestment → Greater Future Puissance**

Possible returns include:

- knowledge;
- experience;
- code;
- tools;
- infrastructure;
- documentation;
- provenance;
- reputation;
- credibility;
- network relationships;
- data;
- institutional access;
- financial resources;
- memory;
- optionality.

## Why memory matters

Without reusable memory:

```text
Act → learn → forget → pay again
```

With reusable memory:

```text
Act → learn → preserve → reuse → start higher
```

This is why Kristal and operational memory are not passive archives. They are future capability infrastructure.

## Tools as crystallized past puissance

A tool records prior effort in a form that can be reused.

```text
past time + knowledge + experimentation
→ code / procedure / infrastructure
→ lower future effort
→ greater future capacity
```

The same applies to documentation, standards, schemas, trusted relationships, and institutional routines.

## Regenerative vs extractive loops

An extractive loop looks like:

```text
collective activity
→ value
→ narrow owner
→ more owner capacity
```

A regenerative loop looks like:

```text
collective activity
→ value
→ participants + shared assets + common infrastructure
→ more distributed capability
```

The constitutional question is therefore:

> **Who receives the returns generated by an exercise of collective puissance?**

## kOA transition

A founder can bootstrap an ecosystem through concentrated effort. Long-term resilience requires a transition from:

> **Founder-powered → System-powered ecosystem**

The test is whether accumulated assets become usable, maintainable, forkable, and improvable by others without requiring unique founder knowledge or permission.

> **Canonical repo source:** `showcase/04-compounding-engine.md`

# Constitutional Tests

Stress-test a system with adversarial questions:

- What if the maintainer becomes hostile?
- What if a credibility policy is biased?
- What if the dominant branch stops improving?
- What if ranking favors incumbents?
- What if a signing key is lost or captured?
- What if the official brand owner disagrees with a protocol fork?
- What if a provider changes terms?
- What if AI output becomes de facto policy?
- Can affected actors verify, contest, substitute, exit, and continue?

The objective is not perfect immunity but non-terminal failure modes.

> **Canonical repo source:** `assessment/constitutional-tests.md`

# Capability and Power Conversion Chains

## v6: make source and destination domains explicit

Every chain should now be read as `source domain → mechanism → destination domain → resulting leverage`, then tested for routing, counterpower, and mandate. See [Cross-Domain Power Conversion](Cross-Domain-Power-Conversion).


Power rarely remains in one domain. Resources and forms of puissance can be converted into other resources, opportunities, and relations of power.

## Common chains

```text
knowledge
→ credibility
→ visibility
→ opportunity
→ resources
→ better tools
→ more knowledge
```

```text
brand
→ attention
→ affiliation
→ sales / funding
→ infrastructure
→ reach
→ stronger brand
```

```text
network
→ collaborators
→ execution
→ results
→ reputation
→ broader network
```

```text
technical control
→ control of defaults
→ user dependence
→ agenda influence
```

```text
money
→ infrastructure
→ distribution
→ attention
→ political influence
```

## Conversion is not automatically illegitimate

Systems need conversion. Expertise should influence advice. Money should fund work. Reputation can help discovery. Technical administration is needed to maintain infrastructure.

The danger is **automatic cross-domain conversion**.

Examples:

- expertise becoming political sovereignty;
- money becoming more votes;
- brand becoming truth;
- technical maintenance becoming control of political outcomes;
- credibility becoming unquestionable authority;
- infrastructure becoming ownership of users.

That is why Power Dynamics uses **conversion firewalls** rather than trying to eliminate conversion itself.

## Power stacking

When one actor controls several conversion points, legitimate functions can stack into cross-domain leverage.

```text
finance
+ identity
+ certification
+ platform access
+ philanthropy
→ high cross-domain leverage
```

The issue is not that any one function is illegitimate. The issue is what the combined position makes possible.

> **Canonical repo source:** `showcase/05-conversion-chains.md`

# Core Ontology

The canonical ontology is:

> **Resource / Asset → Potential → Capacity → Puissance → Power Relation → Effect → Return**

This is not a claim that every real process is linear. It is a conceptual separation that prevents category errors.

- **Resource / asset** — something available or accumulated that may support action.
- **Potential** — what could become realistically possible because those resources exist.
- **Capacity** — an organized ability under defined conditions.
- **Puissance** — a capacity that is effectively mobilizable under actual conditions.
- **Power relation** — a relation in which one actor's or system's puissance changes another's effective possibilities.
- **Effect** — the realized change.
- **Return** — a reusable asset created or preserved by the effect.

### Knowledge example

`research corpus → potential understanding → organized expertise → epistemic puissance → advisory/recognition relation → changed decision → credibility + memory`

### Money example

`financial stock → potential purchasing options → liquid capacity → economic puissance → allocation/bargaining relation → funded project → infrastructure + revenue`

The ontology does not make relational power morally negative. Power may enable, coordinate, recognize, protect, allocate, constrain, or dominate.

> **Canonical repo source:** `framework/01-core-ontology.md`

# Counterpower and Optionality as Capability

The Power Show includes not only the ability to **do more**, but also the ability to **depend less**.

A system that can coordinate, rank, certify, route, or preserve memory is powerful. A participant who can verify, contest, substitute, exit, fork, or wait also possesses important capability.

## Verification puissance

Ability to independently inspect claims, provenance, signatures, rules, calculations, policies, and technical behavior.

## Contestation puissance

Ability to challenge a decision, score, category, workflow, authority, branch, or interpretation through a process capable of producing change.

## Substitution puissance

Ability to replace a provider, implementation, trust root, reader policy, workflow, branch, or institution with a viable alternative.

## Exit puissance

Ability to leave while preserving enough identity, history, knowledge, reputation, data, rights, relationships, and continuity for departure to be meaningful.

## Forking puissance

Ability to create an alternative rather than accept the existing implementation as terminal.

## Branch mobility

Ability to move beyond a symbolic fork: attract maintainers, preserve interoperability, gain discoverability, gather support, and become institutionally viable.

## Optionality puissance

Ability to preserve future choices rather than commit them prematurely.

Examples include:

- retained financial reserves;
- unpublished knowledge;
- unused deployment capacity;
- privacy and unrevealed identity;
- multiple providers;
- portable credentials;
- branchable software;
- independent infrastructure.

Optionality is important because **unexercised capability can still change future strategic position**.

## The inversion

Traditional power analysis often asks who can compel whom.

Power Dynamics also asks:

> **Who can refuse? Who can leave? Who can reproduce? Who can create an alternative? Who can preserve the option to act later?**

These are central forms of practical freedom and therefore belong inside the Power Show itself.

> **Canonical repo source:** `showcase/10-counterpower-and-optionality.md`

# Counterpowers, Legibility, Provenance, and Auditability

A minimum counterpower package is:

> **Verification + Contestation + Substitution + Exit**

Power should also be legible enough that affected actors can identify the relevant holder, mechanism, mandate, inputs, outputs, version, and appeal path.

**Provenance** preserves where claims, artifacts, decisions, and transformations came from. **Auditability** allows independent reconstruction or verification.

Transparency is not sufficient by itself. A transparent system that cannot be contested or substituted can remain dominating. Complexity is not inherently illegitimate either; the problem is complexity functioning as an unanswerable source of authority.

> **Canonical repo source:** `constitution/counterpowers-and-legibility.md`

# Credibility & Reputation Power

> **Core question:** Who can shape standing, trust signals, certification, expertise legibility, and access to opportunity?

## Generated puissance

Effective capability to **make demonstrated competence, contribution, reliability, and evidence legible under explicit criteria**.

## Relational power that can emerge

Standing power, certification power, endorsement power, opportunity power.

## Typical conversion paths

- `evidence → credibility`
- `credibility → visibility`
- `credibility → access / opportunity`
- `recognition → network growth`

## Routing / counterpower direction

- domain-bounded signals
- score ≠ person ≠ competence ≠ truth
- plural credibility models and distributions
- contestability, history, time decay, and portable evidence

## kOA touchpoints

- Koali / EkoH organize credibility and expertise signals
- SmartVote can use advisory readings without converting credibility into sovereign votes
- Koali protocolization aims to avoid one terminal owner of standing

## Analytical note

Credibility is useful precisely because it can affect attention and opportunity. That same usefulness makes credibility infrastructure a major power surface.

This is an analytical domain profile, not a claim that every listed mechanism is fully implemented. Use [Technical Evidence Map](Technical-Evidence-Map) when moving from architecture to current-state implementation claims.

> **Canonical v6 repo sources:** `POWER-SURFACE.md`, `domains/README.md`, `README.md`

# Cross-Domain Power Conversion

Power is often most consequential when capability or authority in one domain is converted into leverage in another.

## Conversion

A conversion can be represented as:

```text
source resource / puissance
→ conversion mechanism
→ destination resource / puissance / power relation
```

Examples:

```text
knowledge → evidence of competence → credibility
credibility → ranking / endorsement → visibility
visibility → audience → opportunity
money → purchase / funding → infrastructure
infrastructure → dependency → bargaining leverage
brand → affiliation → revenue
administrative control → defaults → de facto policy
```

## Cross-domain leverage

Not all conversion is problematic. Many desirable systems depend on it.

The problem begins when:

1. the conversion is hidden;
2. the destination authority exceeds the source mandate;
3. affected actors cannot contest or substitute;
4. the conversion compounds through multiple domains;
5. the resulting leverage becomes durable or terminal.

## Capture

> **Capture occurs when control over one legitimate function is converted into durable influence over functions outside its intended mandate.**

Canonical warning pattern:

> **Functional Control → Cross-Domain Power**

## Power stacking

Power stacking occurs when one actor or node combines several functions whose interactions create leverage not visible when each function is assessed separately.

For example:

```text
identity
+ credibility
+ discovery
+ funding
+ workflow
= cross-domain leverage
```

or:

```text
monetary rail
+ taxation
+ allocation
+ sanctions
+ dispute enforcement
= high terminality risk
```

## Conversion firewalls

A conversion firewall does not prohibit composition. It prevents **automatic authority inheritance**.

Examples:

- expertise → advice, not sovereignty;
- money → funding, not votes;
- brand → attention, not truth;
- credibility → discoverability, not unquestionable authority;
- infrastructure → capability, not ownership of users;
- execution → mandate implementation, not mandate redefinition.

## Analytical procedure

For each important function:

1. Name the source domain.
2. Name the source resource/capability.
3. Identify the conversion mechanism.
4. Name the destination domain.
5. Identify the resulting power relation.
6. Measure dependency and switching cost.
7. Identify returns and compounding.
8. Identify firewall, contestation, substitution, or exit.
9. Test whether the destination authority is within mandate.
10. Test whether capture of the instance would imply capture of the system.

## Relation to the three-axis model

Cross-domain conversion connects the three axes:

- **ontology** — what changes state;
- **dynamic** — conversion / compounding / capture;
- **domain** — where the source and destination operate.

This is why the domain axis must be explicit rather than a loose list of examples.


## Continue

- [Conversion Chains](Conversion-Chains)
- [Power Conversion Firewalls](Power-Conversion-Firewalls)
- [Desjardins Power Stacking](Desjardins-Power-Stacking)
- [Power Surface Audit](Power-Surface-Audit)

> **Canonical v6 repo source:** `framework/10-cross-domain-power-conversion.md`

# Desjardins and Power Stacking

The Desjardins proposal combines member governance with possible roles in banking, identity, certification, philanthropy, and Konnaxion access.

Each function may be legitimate while their combination creates cross-domain leverage. The design question is how to keep financial infrastructure strong in its mandate without silently becoming identity, epistemic, political, or platform sovereignty.

# Dynamics

The Power Show describes **what kOA can generate**. Dynamics describes **what happens to capability and power over time**.

The v6 dynamic sequence is:

> **Formation → Mobilization → Composition → Conversion → Returns / Compounding → Concentration / Capture → Counterpower → Diversification / Evolution → Decay → Transfer → Externalities / Scale → Routing / Sharing / Devolution**

The sequence is recursive rather than linear.

## 1. Formation
Resources become potential, organized capacity, and effectively mobilizable puissance.

## 2. Mobilization
Retained resources or existing puissance become active through release, flow, mediation, amplification, and throughput.

`Reservoir → Release → Flow → Mediation` is one mobilization model, not the whole theory.

## 3. Composition and generated puissance
Distinct capabilities combine into higher-order capability that existed nowhere individually.

## 4. Conversion and firewalls
One resource, capability, or relational advantage becomes another. v6 makes the **source domain → mechanism → destination domain** path explicit.

## 5. Returns, reinvestment, and compounding
Action can leave reusable assets that increase future capability.

> **Puissance → Action → Result → Return → Reinvestment → Greater Future Puissance**

## 6. Concentration, stacking, and capture
Several legitimate functions or accumulated returns can create cross-domain leverage.

> **Functional Control → Cross-Domain Power**

## 7. Counterpower, contestation, and exit
Actors can alter the terms of power through verification, challenge, substitution, refusal, exit, or fork.

## 8. Diversification and evolution
Alternatives emerge, demonstrate value, gather support, coexist, recombine, or replace dominant branches.

## 9. Decay, dissipation, and loss
Reputation, network position, knowledge relevance, infrastructure, liquidity, legitimacy, and organizational capacity can erode.

## 10. Transfer, succession, and inheritance
Resources, authority, keys, mandates, memory, and stewardship move across actors and generations.

## 11. Externalities and scale
A useful mechanism can acquire systemic power because adoption, network effects, standards, or dependency grow around it.

## 12. Routing, sharing, and devolution
v6 adds an explicit dynamic for **where generated capability, authority, returns, and optionality are sent**.

```text
generated leverage
→ concentrating default
→ routing mechanism
→ distributed / scoped destination
```

Examples include protocolization, self-hosting, plural Koali distributions, ethical self-binding, advice without sovereignty, democratic allocation, and branch mobility.

See [Power Routing & Sharing](Power-Routing-and-Sharing).

> **Canonical v6 repo hierarchy:** `dynamics/README.md` as described in `TREE.md`; v6 adds `12-routing-sharing-and-devolution.md`.

# Economic & Allocation Power

> **Core question:** Who can mobilize scarce resources and decide which people, projects, institutions, or goals receive them?

## Generated puissance

Effective capability to **mobilize money, property, labor, time, funding, procurement, infrastructure, contracts, and productive assets**.

## Relational power that can emerge

Bargaining power, property power, allocation power, funding dependence, purchasing leverage.

## Typical conversion paths

- `money → infrastructure`
- `funding → execution capacity`
- `credibility → opportunity / funding`
- `brand → revenue`
- `network → customers / collaborators / access`

## Routing / counterpower direction

- money → funding, not political standing
- democratic allocation where collective funds are governed
- returns should be audited for who accumulates reusable assets
- shared infrastructure can turn collective activity into broader future capability

## kOA touchpoints

- Zero-Skim illustrates common resource pooling with political firewalls
- brand / merchandise can convert symbolic attention into economic resources
- Koali / credibility can affect opportunity and funding access
- Orgo converts funding into accountable execution
- compounding turns results into future assets

## Analytical note

Money is a resource stock, not power by identity. It becomes economic puissance when mobilizable, then relational economic power when its use changes others’ access, bargaining position, allocation, or dependency.

This is an analytical domain profile, not a claim that every listed mechanism is fully implemented. Use [Technical Evidence Map](Technical-Evidence-Map) when moving from architecture to current-state implementation claims.

> **Canonical v6 repo sources:** `POWER-SURFACE.md`, `domains/README.md`, `README.md`

# Effective Possibility

The framework's central object is not abstract freedom but **effective possibility**.

A formal option can exist while remaining practically unusable. A person may possess a legal right but lack time, money, information, institutional recognition, language access, transportation, technical capability, or a viable appeal process. Conversely, infrastructure can expand effective possibility by reducing those barriers.

This is why dependency is central. If essential possibilities depend on an actor whose rules are opaque, unilateral, difficult to contest, or costly to exit, that dependency creates relational leverage even if the actor rarely intervenes.

Effective possibility should be assessed through access, affordability, time cost, intelligibility, technical operability, legal recognition, portability, alternatives, coordination cost, reversibility, risk, and institutional acceptance.

The objective is not perfect quantification. It is to make the relevant constraints and enabling conditions **legible**.

> **Canonical repo source:** `framework/02-effective-possibility.md`

# Epistemic & Semantic Power

> **Core question:** Who can shape what is knowable, valid, categorized, translated, comparable, or admissible?

## Generated puissance

Effective capability to **validate, compare, structure, version, translate, preserve, and reuse knowledge and meaning**.

## Relational power that can emerge

Canon power, validation power, classification power, framing power, admissibility power, trust-root power.

## Typical conversion paths

- `meaning → knowledge`
- `knowledge → credibility`
- `classification → enforcement / agenda`
- `epistemic standing → visibility / opportunity`

## Routing / counterpower direction

- portable artifacts and provenance
- competing / complementary Kristals
- versioned semantics and explicit policy identity
- local semantic governance and contestation

## kOA touchpoints

- SemantiK / SenTient structure concepts, relations, ambiguity, and translation
- Kristal preserves provenance, versioning, and reusable knowledge
- reader policies and competing representations prevent one reading from becoming final by default

## Analytical note

To govern meaning is already to govern part of the collective field of possibility. The goal is not semantic neutrality but governable, revisable mediation.

This is an analytical domain profile, not a claim that every listed mechanism is fully implemented. Use [Technical Evidence Map](Technical-Evidence-Map) when moving from architecture to current-state implementation claims.

# Ethical Fiction as Self-Binding

Fiction can create attention, recognition, affiliation, emotional salience, memory, and transmission. That is real symbolic puissance.

One distinctive kOA mechanism is to publish **ethical principles inside the fiction itself**.

The source corpus explicitly includes a living ethical code centered on clarity of intention, respect for living beings, vigilance against monopoly, domination and manipulation, public recognition of contributors, self-mockery, regular ethical reassessment, revision, criticism, and non-dogmatic transmission.

## Power redirection

This does more than state values.

```text
Narrative puissance
→ ethical constraints carried by the narrative
→ public commitments
→ critics and participants can cite them
→ symbolic authority becomes more contestable
```

The same material also redirects symbolic capital:

- recognize contributions;
- publicly thank allies;
- share merit;
- never sanctify the Klown;
- reject dogma;
- document and transmit;
- pass the baton;
- treat reinvention rather than fidelity as legitimate succession.

A related analytical text describes the public persona as a **diffusion instrument** distinct from the architect identity and oriented toward reducing founder-worship.

## What this does not do

Published ethics are not enforcement. They do not prove compliance.

They become politically stronger when combined with auditability, attribution, contestation, portability, exit, forkability, and independent operation.

> **Narrative may mobilize, but it should also carry instructions for resisting its own capture.**

> **Canonical repo source:** `cases/ethical-fiction-self-binding.md`

# Evolutionary & Counterpower

> **Core question:** Who can challenge, substitute, fork, recombine, improve, displace, or replace an incumbent implementation or authority?

## Generated puissance

Effective capability to **verify, contest, substitute, exit, fork, recombine, demonstrate alternatives, and gather support**.

## Relational power that can emerge

Incumbent displacement power, branch mobility, substitution leverage, exit power.

## Typical conversion paths

- `criticism → variation`
- `variation → demonstration`
- `demonstration → support`
- `support → adoption / dominance`
- `fork → institutional replacement`

## Routing / counterpower direction

- branch mobility and lineage
- interoperability and data / provenance portability
- effective exit and substitution
- stable kernel with evolving branches

## kOA touchpoints

- competing Kristals preserve plurality
- federation and self-hosting create alternative operating paths
- branch ecology turns imperfection into ongoing institutional experimentation

## Analytical note

Forkability is insufficient if alternatives cannot attract users, preserve history, interoperate, or gather institutional support.

This is an analytical domain profile, not a claim that every listed mechanism is fully implemented. Use [Technical Evidence Map](Technical-Evidence-Map) when moving from architecture to current-state implementation claims.

# Exit, Portability and Federation

Exit is not merely a legal permission. It is an effective capability that depends on portability, substitution, deployability, interoperability, and the practical survival of alternatives. Federation lets capability scale without requiring all sovereignty to scale with it.


## Revocability, exit and portability

Revocation removes future authority. Reversal addresses prior effects. They are different.

A ranking rule can be changed while earlier reputational effects persist. A decision-maker can be removed while contracts, debts, or standards remain.

Exit and fork are structural counterpowers only when effective. Portability can include data, knowledge artifacts, identity, credentials, reputation evidence, decision history, operational records, schemas, protocols, and deployment tooling.

A technically forkable project can remain practically monopolistic if forks lose recognition, network access, compatibility, or essential assets.

Constitutional analysis should therefore ask both whether authority can be revoked and whether affected actors can actually migrate, recover, or continue.


## Federation and subsidiarity

Federation coordinates independently governed nodes through shared contracts rather than requiring a flat or centerless architecture.

Subsidiarity places decisions at the lowest level capable of handling them effectively while preserving necessary coordination.

The repo rejects `federation = no center`. Strong local hubs, shared standards, and central reference services can coexist with local sovereignty if substitution and independent operation remain meaningful.


> **Canonical repo source:** `constitution/revocability-exit-portability.md`, `constitution/federation-subsidiarity.md`

# FAQ

## Is Power Dynamics a theory of domination?

No. It studies constructive capability as seriously as constraint. The project begins with the kOA Power Show: how people and collectives become more capable. Domination enters when consequential power becomes arbitrary, cross-domain, difficult to verify, impossible to contest, or effectively inescapable.

## Why use the French word *puissance* in an English framework?

Because it prevents a useful distinction from collapsing. `Puissance` means effectively mobilizable capacity. `Power` is reserved for relational effects on another actor’s effective possibilities.

## Is money power?

Not automatically. Money is a resource stock. Under real conditions it can become economic puissance, which can then create bargaining, allocation, access, ownership, or influence relations.

## Is expertise power?

Expertise is first an epistemic capacity. It becomes relational power when it changes standing, access, decisions, ranking, or authority. The framework therefore accepts `expertise → advice` while resisting `expertise → automatic sovereignty`.

## Why is discoverability governance?

Because what becomes visible is more likely to be read, cited, funded, reused, adopted, or acted upon. Ranking and discovery systems can therefore shape effective possibility before formal decisions occur.

## Does kOA eliminate capture?

No. It tries to make capture **non-terminal** through legibility, scoped authority, substitution, portability, exit, federation, and branch mobility.

## Why allow strong hubs at all?

Because useful centrality can lower coordination cost. The target is not flatness; it is `StrongHub + DistributedCapability + ReplicableWhole`. Functional centrality should not silently become total sovereignty.

## Is the dominant branch the true branch?

No. `dominance ≠ truth ≠ quality ≠ perfection`. Dominance may reflect network effects, timing, brand, compatibility, or accumulated support. Better or complementary branches need practical mobility, not only a formal right to fork.

## What is the main kOA capability loop?

> **Know → Choose → Act → Remember → Know better**

The Power Show interprets the same loop as capability generation, composition, action, return, reinvestment, and compounding.

## Where should I start?

Start with [Power Show](Power-Show). If you want the rigorous vocabulary immediately afterward, read [Core Ontology](Core-Ontology) and the [Glossary](Glossary).

> **Canonical v6 repo sources:** `README.md`, `POWER-SHOW.md`, `POWER-SURFACE.md`, `POWER-ROUTING.md`, `GLOSSARY.md`, `PRINCIPLES.md`

---

[Home](Home) · [Power Show](Power-Show) · [Glossary](Glossary)


## Does kOA try to keep all the power it generates?

No. A major design theme is **power routing**: protocolization, plural distributions, self-hosting, advisory separation, branch mobility, public ethical self-binding, and democratic allocation redirect capability and leverage toward participants, communities, protocols, and alternatives rather than treating terminal platform ownership as the objective.

See [Power Routing & Sharing](Power-Routing-and-Sharing).


## What is new in v6?

v6 makes the **Power Surface** explicit. It separates political, economic, and monetary power; maps cross-domain conversions; adds routing / sharing / devolution as a first-class dynamic; and makes the Power Surface Audit the natural entry point for system analysis.

## Is political power only voting power?

No. Political power can be produced before, during, and after a formal vote:

> **Meaning → Knowledge → Visibility → Credibility → Agenda → Deliberation → Decision → Execution → Memory**

That is why semantic, discoverability, credibility, administrative, and mnemonic mechanisms can all have political consequences.

## What is the difference between economic and monetary power?

Economic power concerns mobilization and allocation of scarce resources such as money, property, labor, funding, and infrastructure. Monetary power concerns the infrastructure and conditions of exchange itself: issuance, payment rails, settlement, reserves, liquidity, credit, convertibility, and transaction access.

## Why is Power Routing part of the Power Show?

Because distributing the capability to operate, reproduce, contest, or leave a system is itself a form of generated puissance. Self-hosting, plural Koali distributions, portable Kristals, branch mobility, and public ethical self-binding are not only defensive constraints; they create new capability for others.

# Forms of Puissance

This page is the capability side of the Power Show: the forms of effective capacity that actors and collectives can mobilize before asking what relational power those capacities create.

This directory names the **positive capability side of the Power Show**.

**Puissance** is effective mobilizable capacity. Before asking who controls whom, Power Dynamics asks what an actor, group, institution, or infrastructure can actually do.

## Agency and cognitive puissance

Choose, refuse, learn, reason, synthesize, model, plan, create, evaluate, and use governed AI assistance.

## Epistemic and semantic puissance

Produce, structure, validate, compare, translate, preserve, contest, and evolve knowledge and meaning.

## Collective, network, and coordination puissance

Find people, combine distributed expertise, form groups, coordinate roles, synchronize action, and create capability no participant possessed alone.

## Deliberative and decision puissance

Compare alternatives, preserve reasons, incorporate advisory expertise, and choose through explicit procedures.

## Operational and administrative puissance

Route work, assign responsibility, manage timing, escalate, execute, review, and close loops.

## Mnemonic puissance

Preserve provenance, decisions, actions, failures, outcomes, and lineage so the next cycle starts from reusable memory.

## Economic and allocation puissance

Mobilize material resources, funding, productive assets, and allocation mechanisms.

## Technical and infrastructural puissance

Build, inspect, modify, test, reproduce, repair, deploy, self-host, and maintain systems that lower future coordination costs.

## Distribution, brand, and affiliation puissance

Transmit knowledge, make people or projects discoverable, create public recognition, affiliation, and reach across communities and languages.

## Federation, exit, and evolutionary puissance

Operate independently, substitute dependencies, fork, gather support, preserve interoperability, recombine, and replace dominant branches.

These forms can compose. They become **power relations** only when their exercise materially changes another actor's access, options, standing, visibility, allocation, authority, dependency, interpretation, or ability to act.

See [Capability Spectrum](Capability-Spectrum) for the kOA-facing presentation.

> **Canonical repo source:** `puissance/README.md`

# Founder Decentering

Founder power can arise from authorship, vocabulary, repositories, domains, brand, relationships, keys, architecture knowledge, and interpretive authority.

The goal is not necessarily founder removal. It is independent reproducibility:

> **Founder → Architecture → Distributed Mastery**

rather than:

> **Founder → Permanent Interpretive Dependency**

Useful phases include founder sovereignty, constitutional constraints, maintainer pluralism, institutional federation, and protocol-level continuity.

> **Canonical repo source:** `constitution/founder-decentering.md`

---

[Home](Home) · [Power Show](Power-Show) · [Glossary](Glossary)


## From founder power to ecosystem capability

The target is not merely less founder power. It is conversion:

> **Founder-powered → Architecture-powered → Protocol-powered → Ecosystem-powered**

Documentation, reproducibility, self-hosting, Koali plurality, branch mobility, and public ethical self-binding redirect founder-specific assets into capability others can use independently.

# From Individual to Federation

kOA's power show is also a **scale show**. The same architecture can increase capability at different social scales without assuming that every scale requires the same center.

## Individual

Potential gains include:

- better access to structured knowledge;
- stronger personal memory and provenance;
- AI-assisted cognition;
- better discovery of expertise and opportunities;
- easier publication and reuse;
- more informed choice;
- greater exit and optionality.

## Group

A group can gain capabilities no member has alone:

- shared context;
- distributed expertise;
- collective comparison;
- task routing;
- explicit ownership;
- coordination;
- reusable memory;
- stronger network reach.

This is **generated collective puissance**.

## Organization

At organizational scale, the architecture adds:

- roles and mandates;
- governed workflows;
- escalation;
- decision records;
- institutional memory;
- auditability;
- repeatable operating patterns;
- succession and continuity.

## Federation

A federation can add:

- local sovereignty over local operation;
- shared interoperability contracts;
- portable knowledge;
- distributed deployment;
- branchable governance;
- independent continuity;
- cross-instance learning;
- substitution of providers or implementations.

The target architecture is:

> **StrongHub + DistributedCapability + ReplicableWhole**

This permits useful centrality without making the center the sole source of truth, continuity, identity, or capability.

## Scaling without scaling sovereignty

The important design challenge is:

> **Can capability scale faster than dependency on any single sovereign node?**

That is why portability, federation, explicit authority scope, provenance, branch mobility, and local operation belong inside the power show rather than being afterthoughts.

> **Canonical repo source:** `showcase/02-individual-to-federation.md`

FILE: Power-Dynamics.wiki/Glossary.md

# Canonical Glossary

This glossary is normative for repository terminology. It keeps the **kOA Power Show** expressive without allowing broad language such as “powerful” to collapse distinct analytical states. When a term is used differently in a source document, preserve the source meaning and explicitly distinguish it from the repository definition.

## Canonical distinctions

| Do not conflate | Canonical distinction |
|---|---|
| resource / power | a resource supports action; power is relational |
| potential / capacity | potential is unrealized possibility; capacity is organized ability |
| capacity / puissance | capacity can exist without being effectively mobilizable; puissance is effective under real conditions |
| puissance / power | puissance produces effects; power shapes another actor's effective possibilities |
| stock / flow | stock is accumulated; flow is movement or throughput |
| reservoir / power | a reservoir retains resources or options; it does not store relational power as a substance |
| score / competence | a score is a governed reading of evidence, not competence itself |
| credibility / truth | credibility affects trust; it does not establish truth by itself |
| support / evidence | support is social endorsement; evidence supports a claim or evaluation |
| dominance / quality | dominance describes position or adoption; quality is multidimensional and contestable |
| forkability / branch mobility | a fork may be possible while remaining unable to attract users, evidence, interoperability, or institutional support |
| centrality / sovereignty | a hub may be functionally central without possessing total or terminal authority |

### French / English convention

The repository deliberately keeps **puissance** in French even inside English prose when precision matters. English *power* collapses several meanings that this framework separates.

- **potential / potentiel** — unrealized possibility;
- **capacity / capacité** — organized ability;
- **puissance** — effectively mobilizable capacity;
- **power / pouvoir relationnel** — relational shaping of another actor's effective possibilities.

## Power Show terms

**kOA Power Show** — the presentation layer of Power Dynamics that maps the forms of effective capability kOA can generate, compose, preserve, distribute, and evolve before analyzing the power relations created by successful capability.

**Capability spectrum** — the set of distinct forms of potential, capacity, and puissance available to an actor or system across domains. In kOA this includes epistemic, semantic, cognitive, collective, network, credibility, decision, operational, mnemonic, technical, infrastructural, federative, exit, and evolutionary capability.

**Capability loop** — a sequence in which one capability feeds the next. The canonical kOA loop is `Know → Choose → Act → Remember → Know better`.

**Closed capability loop** — a capability loop in which outputs are sufficiently preserved and fed back as inputs that the system can learn from prior action rather than repeatedly restarting from near-zero institutional memory. “Closed” does not mean perfectly self-contained.

**Composition engine** — shorthand for the architecture through which distinct capabilities become interoperable inputs to one another, creating higher-order or generated puissance.

**Composition gain** — additional effective capability created because separate functions interoperate. It is the difference between possessing useful components and possessing a coherent capability system.

**Compounding engine** — shorthand for feedback loops in which action produces reusable returns that lower the cost, increase the quality, or expand the scope of future action.

**Capability infrastructure** — technical, semantic, institutional, or procedural infrastructure whose primary effect is to make future action easier, more reliable, more composable, or more reusable.

**Capability surface** — a function or interface through which a system increases what actors can effectively do. A capability surface can later become a power surface if control over it changes other actors’ effective possibilities.

**Power surface** — a function, interface, registry, role, default, gate, workflow, standard, or infrastructure boundary through which control can shape other actors’ access, standing, visibility, allocation, timing, interpretation, or ability to act.

**Power surface map** — cross-domain inventory of the capability surfaces, relational power surfaces, controllers, conversion paths, counterpowers, and routing mechanisms of a system.

**Cross-domain power conversion** — transformation through which a resource, capability, or relational advantage in one domain becomes leverage in another domain.

**Conversion path** — explicit sequence connecting a source domain to a destination domain through a mechanism such as funding, ranking, certification, standardization, ownership, or dependency.

**Capability-to-power transition** — the point at which successful puissance becomes relationally consequential for other actors. This transition is central to the repository: the same function can be empowering as capability and politically consequential as a controlled gate.

## Ontology

**Resource** — anything available to an actor or system that can support future action: money, time, knowledge, code, rights, relationships, reputation, data, infrastructure, identity, property, attention, memory, or other conditions.

**Asset** — a resource that has been accumulated, stabilized, owned, controlled, recognized, or made reusable enough to support future action.

**Stock** — an accumulated quantity or body of resources/assets at a point in time. A stock is descriptive; it is not automatically puissance or power.

**Potential** — unrealized effective possibility preserved or created by resources and conditions.

**Capacity** — an organized ability to perform a class of actions under specified conditions.

**Puissance** — capacity that is effectively mobilizable under real conditions to produce effects. Puissance is the preferred term for what English often calls *power to*, *power with*, or *power through* when no relational control over others is implied.

**Power** — a relational capacity, grounded in puissance, through which one actor, group, institution, or infrastructure shapes another's effective possibilities.

**Power relation** — a relation in which another actor's access, options, standing, visibility, allocation, authority, incentives, categories, timing, dependencies, or ability to act are materially changed.

**Effect** — a realized change produced by action, mediation, or a power relation.

**Return** — a reusable asset created or preserved by an action or effect and available for future cycles.

**Effective possibility** — what an actor can realistically understand, choose, refuse, obtain, build, coordinate, contest, preserve, or leave under actual material, institutional, temporal, informational, and social conditions.

## Modes of capability and relation

**Puissance d'agir / power to** — effective ability to accomplish, understand, build, decide, refuse, learn, create, or otherwise act.

**Collective puissance / power with** — effective capability generated through coordination among multiple actors.

**Infrastructure-mediated puissance / power through** — effective capability enabled, stored, routed, amplified, or made reusable through infrastructure.

**Power over** — an asymmetric family of power relations in which one actor can condition, constrain, block, allocate, define, authorize, or otherwise shape another's possibilities. It is not the definition of all power.

**Mutual power** — a reciprocal power relation in which actors alter one another's possibilities in ways that can expand joint capacity.

**Generated puissance** — new effective capacity created by composition that did not exist in any participant separately.

**Latent puissance** — capacity that could be mobilized but is currently inactive because of timing, coordination, information, access, or other constraints.

## Dynamics

**Formation** — the process through which resources become organized capacity and then effective puissance.

**Mobilization** — the activation of available resources or puissance into action, exposure, coordination, or effects.

**Reservoir** — a configuration in which accumulated resources or reusable assets are retained for future activation. A reservoir is not stored relational power.

**Retention** — preserving a resource, asset, option, or capability rather than immediately committing or releasing it.

**Release** — activation, disclosure, deployment, spending, publication, delegation, or other transition from retained resource to active use.

**Release puissance** — effective ability to control the timing, sequencing, form, and channels through which a retained resource is activated.

**Flow** — movement of mobilized resources, signals, actions, or effects through a system.

**Throughput** — the practical rate at which a system can process, deploy, distribute, or convert resources and puissance into effects.

**Mediation** — selection, framing, routing, translation, ranking, interpretation, packaging, contextualization, or transformation between source and affected actors.

**Amplification** — an increase in scale or intensity of an effect relative to the initiating input because of a network, platform, institution, technology, or channel.

**Bottleneck** — a constrained passage that limits throughput or access to a necessary transformation.

**Bottleneck power** — relational power created when an actor controls a passage that others must use to convert resources or puissance into effects.

**Composition** — combining distinct resources, capacities, or forms of puissance to create a higher-order capability.

**Conversion** — transformation of a resource, asset, capacity, puissance, or relational position into another form.

**Conversion pathway** — an identifiable sequence through which one form becomes another, e.g. `knowledge → credibility → visibility → opportunity`.

**Conversion firewall** — a rule or architecture that permits legitimate composition while blocking automatic conversion into unrelated authority.

**Reinvestment** — using returns to create or amplify future capacity and puissance.

**Compounding puissance** — growth in future effective capacity caused by reinvestment and reuse across cycles.

**Power yield** — the set of returns, effects, and relational positions generated by a given exercise of puissance or power. It should not be reduced to a single scalar metric.

**Concentration** — increasing share of relevant resources, puissance, or relational control accumulating in one actor, node, branch, or institution.

**Stacking** — accumulation of several distinct functions in the same actor or node, producing cross-domain leverage not visible in any function separately.

**Capture** — durable conversion of legitimate functional control into influence or control outside its intended mandate.

**Cross-domain conversion** — movement of advantage from one domain into another, e.g. economic assets becoming political influence or technical administration becoming agenda control.

**Diversification** — distribution of capability or governance across multiple actors, implementations, branches, domains, or institutions.

**Decay** — reduction in usefulness, credibility, adoption, capability, legitimacy, liquidity, network position, or other components of puissance over time.

**Dissipation** — loss of potential returns because knowledge, experience, attention, coordination, or other value is not preserved or reused.

**Transfer** — movement of resources, capacity, authority, relational position, or responsibility between actors or institutions.

**Succession** — governed transfer of roles, stewardship, keys, mandates, or interpretive responsibility across time.

## Dependence, domination, and counterpower

**Dependency** — reliance on an actor, system, resource, or infrastructure whose substitution or exit is materially costly, slow, unavailable, or illegible.

**Dependency gradient** — degree of dependency, determined by alternatives, switching cost, time, portability, recognition, rights, knowledge, network effects, and other factors.

**Domination** — exposure to arbitrary or insufficiently contestable relational power, especially where dependency is high and counterpowers are weak.

**Counterpower** — capacity that can verify, contest, substitute, exit, revoke, reverse, fork, or otherwise alter the terms of a power relation.

**Verification** — ability to independently inspect claims, artifacts, processes, identities, signatures, calculations, or compliance.

**Contestation** — ability to challenge a claim, score, decision, rule, ranking, authority, or implementation through a meaningful process.

**Substitution** — ability to replace a provider, branch, implementation, authority root, workflow, or institution with a viable alternative.

**Exit** — ability to leave a relationship or system while preserving enough assets, rights, data, identity, reputation, and continuity for exit to be meaningful.

**Formal exit** — nominal legal or technical ability to leave.

**Effective exit** — practical ability to leave at acceptable cost without disproportionate loss of essential capability.

**Forkability** — ability to create a divergent implementation, policy, ontology, or branch from an existing lineage.

**Effective forkability** — forkability plus the practical ability to operate, interoperate, attract support, preserve provenance, migrate assets, and become viable.

**Revocability** — ability to withdraw authority or permission.

**Reversibility** — ability to undo or repair the effects of an earlier action or authority. Revocability does not guarantee reversibility.

**Terminal power** — relational power whose capture, failure, or abuse cannot be effectively substituted, reversed, contested, or exited.

**Instance capture** — capture of one deployment, organization, branch, or institution.

**System capture** — capture of the broader architecture such that meaningful alternatives cannot continue, interoperate, or emerge.

## Branch ecology and evolutionary governance

**Branch** — a distinct version, implementation, policy, ontology, or interpretation with enough lineage and provenance to be compared with alternatives.

**Lineage** — traceable relationship among versions, forks, sources, transformations, and derived artifacts.

**Branch mobility** — practical ability of a branch to become discoverable, credible, supported, interoperable, adopted, and capable of influencing or replacing dominant branches.

**Dominance** — a branch's current advantage in adoption, support, visibility, compatibility, institutional recognition, network effects, or infrastructure. Dominance does not imply truth or optimality.

**Complementarity** — additional value produced when distinct branches, perspectives, or implementations cover different evidence, contexts, scales, or functions without requiring a universal winner.

**Redundant plurality** — multiple branches that add little new evidence, context, method, function, or interoperability value.

**Meaningful variation** — branch difference that creates identifiable added value or tests a consequential alternative.

**Evolutionary governance** — governance in which consequential rules, policies, interpretations, and implementations remain open to explicit variation, demonstration, comparison, adoption, recombination, amendment, and replacement.

**Stable kernel** — shared minimal contracts, formats, provenance rules, or interoperability requirements that permit divergence without fragmentation.

**Evolving branch** — a branch whose policies, readings, implementations, or governance may change while preserving lineage and interoperability where feasible.

**Recombination** — integration of useful elements from multiple branches into a new branch or later version.

**Replacement** — transition in which an alternative becomes more adopted, authoritative, useful, or institutionally supported than an earlier dominant branch.

## Epistemic, semantic, and credibility terms

**Epistemic puissance** — effective ability to produce, organize, validate, compare, preserve, or reuse knowledge.

**Semantic puissance** — effective ability to create, maintain, translate, contest, and evolve categories, ontologies, definitions, and relations of meaning.

**Semantic power** — relational power created when control over categories, definitions, ontologies, thresholds, or classifications shapes how others can understand, qualify, or act.

**Semantic sovereignty** — practical capacity of a person or community to govern the terms, categories, ontologies, and knowledge structures through which it generates and shares meaning without arbitrary dependence on external authorities.

**Credibility asset** — accumulated evidence or recognition that makes a person, branch, claim, or institution more likely to be trusted in a defined context.

**Credibility signal** — governed reading of evidence about expertise, contribution, conduct, reliability, or other criteria. It is not the person or competence itself.

**Domain-bounded expertise** — expertise whose relevance and influence are limited to explicit domains rather than generalized into universal authority.

**Discoverability puissance** — effective ability to make relevant content, people, or branches findable.

**Discoverability power** — relational power created when control of discovery materially shapes what others see, reuse, fund, cite, learn, adopt, or build.

**Pre-political power** — power exercised through meaning, knowledge, visibility, credibility, agenda, and framing before a formal decision is made.

**Mnemonic puissance** — effective ability to preserve and reuse knowledge, reasons, decisions, actions, failures, and outcomes across time.

**Mnemonic power** — relational power created when control of collective memory shapes future interpretation, precedent, options, or institutional learning.

## Political and constitutional terms

**Political puissance** — effective ability to participate in, shape, organize, or carry collective judgment, agenda formation, decision, amendment, representation, or public action.

**Political power** — relational power to shape political standing, agenda, mandates, decision rights, representation, legitimacy, veto, amendment, or binding collective outcomes.

**Constitutional power** — power to define, amend, interpret, or enforce the rules by which other powers are authorized, limited, contested, or transferred.

**Decision power** — relational power to determine or materially condition which collective option becomes binding or operative.

## Economic, monetary, and allocation terms

**Economic resource** — money, property, goods, productive assets, contractual rights, or other material means.

**Economic puissance** — effective ability to mobilize economic resources for action.

**Economic power** — relational power created when control or mobilization of scarce economic resources materially changes others’ access, bargaining position, dependency, allocation, or effective options.

**Liquidity** — ease and speed with which an asset can be converted into usable resources without excessive loss.

**Liquidity puissance** — effective ability to make resources rapidly usable.

**Allocation power** — relational power to determine which people, projects, institutions, or goals receive resources.

**Monetary puissance** — effective ability to create, access, move, settle, convert, intermediate, or finance value through monetary and credit infrastructure.

**Monetary power** — relational power associated with control over units, rails, issuance, settlement, reserves, access, or conditions of exchange.

**Custodial power** — relational power arising from control over assets held on behalf of others.

**Market-making puissance** — capacity to enable exchange by providing liquidity, matching, standards, custody, or transaction infrastructure.

**Property power** — relational power arising from enforceable control of scarce assets, land, infrastructure, intellectual property, or access rights.

## Information, media, network, and narrative terms

**Information resource** — data, documents, signals, archives, observations, or other transmissible representations.

**Distribution puissance** — effective ability to deliver information, resources, signals, or instructions to relevant actors at scale.

**Reach** — number and relevance of actors that can practically be reached through available channels.

**Reach puissance** — effective capacity to cross geographic, linguistic, network, or institutional boundaries.

**Attention power** — relational power to influence what receives scarce attention.

**Visibility power** — relational power to affect whether and how actors, claims, branches, or events become publicly visible.

**Agenda power** — relational power to influence what enters collective consideration as a problem, priority, or option.

**Framing power** — relational power to shape interpretation by selecting descriptions, categories, narratives, baselines, or causal frames.

**Media puissance** — effective ability to gather, package, distribute, synchronize, and amplify information to an audience.

**Network resource** — relationships, affiliations, pathways, memberships, trust links, or structural positions that can support future coordination.

**Network puissance** — effective ability to mobilize relationships for access, coordination, diffusion, recruitment, or action.

**Affiliation power** — relational effects produced by membership, association, endorsement, exclusion, or visible identification with a network or symbol.

**Narrative puissance** — effective ability to make complex systems understandable, memorable, transmissible, and emotionally or culturally legible.

**Narrative power** — relational power created when narratives shape interpretation, legitimacy, attention, identity, or mobilization.

**Brand asset** — accumulated recognition, associations, expectations, reputation, symbols, and identity attached to a name or mark.

**Brand puissance** — effective ability to mobilize brand recognition into attention, affiliation, trust, distribution, commerce, or coordination.

**Brand power** — relational effects created when brand-mediated trust, attention, certification, or affiliation changes others' opportunities, choices, or standing.

## Technical, infrastructural, administrative, and cognitive terms

**Technical resource** — code, tools, protocols, hardware, schemas, standards, credentials, deployment knowledge, or other technical assets.

**Technical puissance** — effective ability to build, modify, operate, verify, deploy, repair, or reproduce technical systems.

**Infrastructural puissance** — effective ability created by reliable shared systems, protocols, deployment paths, identity rails, data layers, or other infrastructure.

**Infrastructural power** — relational power arising when control over infrastructure materially conditions others' access, options, interoperability, or continuity.

**Administrative puissance** — effective ability to route, assign, schedule, validate, escalate, record, close, and coordinate work.

**Administrative power** — relational power produced when administrative control changes others' timing, workload, access, status, or ability to complete an action.

**Execution puissance** — effective ability to transform decisions into coordinated, traceable action.

**Cognitive puissance** — effective ability to perceive, reason, synthesize, model, plan, generate, or evaluate.

**AI-mediated cognitive puissance** — cognitive capacity amplified through AI tools under explicit governance and human/institutional constraints.

**Hidden sovereignty** — effective decision or policy control exercised by a system that is formally described as merely advisory, technical, or assistive.

## Constitutional and institutional terms

**Authority** — recognized permission or mandate to perform defined actions within a scope.

**Scoped authority** — authority limited by domain, function, duration, jurisdiction, conditions, and review.

**Mandate** — explicit basis, purpose, and boundary of legitimate authority.

**Separation of functions** — design principle preventing one role or component from silently inheriting unrelated functions.

**Functional centrality** — importance of a node to coordination or operation without necessarily implying ownership or sovereignty over the whole system.

**Sovereignty** — ultimate or non-subordinate authority within a defined domain. In this repo, technical centrality and practical dependence can create quasi-sovereign effects without formal sovereign status.

**Subsidiarity** — placing decisions and authority at the lowest level capable of handling them effectively while preserving necessary coordination.

**Federation** — coordination among independently governed nodes or institutions through shared contracts, protocols, or interoperability arrangements.

**Local sovereignty** — practical ability of a local deployment or community to govern critical operations and meanings without requiring permanent permission from a remote authority.

**Protocol commons** — shared technical or semantic substrate whose reuse and interoperability do not require submission to one commercial or political owner.

**Brand stewardship** — governance of official names, marks, certification, and identity separately from protocol ownership or operation.

## Founder and temporal terms

**Founder power** — relational leverage derived from authorship, code control, brand ownership, infrastructure, credentials, narrative authority, interpretation, relationships, or unique knowledge.

**Founder decentering** — progressive reduction of unique founder dependency through documentation, distributed mastery, independent operation, maintainer pluralism, federation, succession, and reproducibility.

**Optionality** — preserved ability to choose among future actions because resources, rights, identity, timing, or commitments have not been irreversibly fixed.

**Temporal optionality** — optionality specifically created by retaining control over timing or disclosure.

**Identity optionality** — preserved ability to reveal, separate, associate, or withhold public identity under different future conditions.

**Release optionality** — preserved ability to decide when and how a retained resource, corpus, asset, or announcement becomes active.

## kOA-specific terms

**kOA** — the broader conceptual, cultural, strategic, governance, and narrative framework.

**kOA Digital Ecosystem** — the operable digital system-of-systems supporting the cycle `Know → Choose → Act → Remember`.

**Kristal** — structured, portable, provenance-preserving knowledge artifact or family of artifacts used to make knowledge auditable, reusable, versioned, and capable of controlled transformation.

**Konnaxion** — public collaboration, circulation, coordination, and commons-facing platform layer.

**Koali** — credibility/reputation infrastructure envisioned as plural distributions around interoperable structures rather than one universal social-credit authority.

**EkoH** — domain-bounded credibility, expertise, ethics, contribution, or trust signal layer, depending on implementation context.

**SmartVote** — decision/discoverability mechanism preserving baseline and advisory/weighted readings separately rather than silently replacing political equality.

**Orgo** — operational workflow and case-management layer connecting decisions to roles, tasks, routing, escalation, closure, and operational memory.

**Capsule** — signed portable deployment/package unit intended to support local operation, verification, rollback, self-hosting, and federation.

**SemantiK Architect** — semantic-architecture function concerned with concepts, categories, relations, contexts, and multilingual structures.

**SenTient** — semantic reconciliation function concerned with ambiguity, entity linking, uncertain propositions, and structured interpretation.

## Compatibility with earlier Power Dynamics shorthand

Earlier drafts used several useful but broader expressions. They remain meaningful if interpreted through the stricter ontology.

**Power stock** — legacy shorthand for accumulated resources/assets plus durable relational positions. Prefer specifying the actual stock rather than treating power as a substance stored in one pool.

**Power flow** — broad shorthand for movement, mobilization, conversion, mediation, or transfer across the system. Prefer identifying what flows: resources, signals, attention, money, authority, puissance, effects, or returns.

**Power return** — shorthand for reusable returns produced by action or power relations. A return may become a resource, asset, relational advantage, or future puissance; it is not necessarily relational power at the moment it returns.

**Power reinvestment** — shorthand for using returns to increase future resources, capacity, puissance, or relational leverage.

**Power regeneration** — shorthand for recurring cycles in which effects are preserved and reused instead of dissipating, creating renewed or expanded puissance.

**Compounding power** — shorthand for cumulative dynamics in which returns increase later relational leverage. Prefer **compounding puissance** when the effect is increased capability without a specified relation to others.

**Power reservoir** — avoid as a strict term. Use **resource reservoir**, **epistemic reservoir**, **financial reserve**, or another domain-specific reservoir. Relational power is not stored like a physical substance.

## Repository shorthand

**Strong distributed puissance, weak sovereignty** — maximize useful effective capability while limiting non-contestable or terminal control.

**Plurality without fragmentation** — allow meaningful divergence while preserving enough shared lineage, contracts, provenance, and interoperability for comparison and recombination.

**Instance capture ≠ system capture** — failure or capture of one node should not automatically make alternatives non-viable.

**Architecture is governance before governance is written** — schemas, defaults, registries, identity systems, workflows, interfaces, and deployment topology already assign and constrain possibilities before formal policy text is applied.


## Power routing and sharing

**Power routing** — explicit design of where generated capability, authority, returns, optionality, visibility, and interpretive leverage are allowed to accumulate.

**Power redirection** — deliberate change in the default destination or conversion path of leverage, such as expertise → advice rather than sovereignty or infrastructure → local autonomy rather than operator dependency.

**Power sharing** — creation of conditions under which multiple actors can effectively use a capability or participate in a right without requiring exclusive ownership by one actor.

**Power devolution** — durable transfer of effective control from a central locus toward local, plural, or lower-level loci.

**Self-binding** — public, procedural, contractual, or technical constraints adopted by an actor or institution that make later exercises of power more legible, contestable, or limited.

**Narrative self-binding** — use of the same narrative or symbolic channel that creates attention and affiliation to publish ethical limits, anti-domination principles, attribution rules, revision norms, or other constraints on symbolic power.

**Protocolization** — conversion of tacit or founder-centric capability into explicit contracts, schemas, specifications, artifacts, tests, and reproducible behavior that others can implement independently.

**Ownership decoupling** — separation of a useful function from exclusive ownership of the implementation, brand, registry, or operator that first instantiated it.

**Capability commons** — a body of reusable capability that multiple actors can access, reproduce, improve, or instantiate without requiring one terminal owner of the function.

**Architect-confirmed design** — a current design intention confirmed in the working process but not yet supported by a canonical source document or direct implementation evidence. It must be labeled separately from source-derived or deployed claims.

# Governance After Capability

Power Dynamics does not treat governance as the mechanism that prevents kOA from becoming powerful.

It treats governance as the mechanism that allows kOA to become **very capable without making successful capability terminal**.

## The sequence

```text
Generate puissance
→ compose puissance
→ amplify and preserve it
→ observe emerging power relations
→ constrain arbitrary conversion
→ preserve counterpower
→ keep alternatives viable
→ let governance itself evolve
```

## Governance can redirect power before constraining it

The constitutional architecture has two jobs:

1. **redirect useful leverage toward distributed capability where possible;**
2. **preserve counterpower where concentration still occurs.**

Examples include protocolizing founder knowledge, making Koali plural and reproducible, publishing ethical constraints in narrative, separating expertise from sovereignty, and turning central infrastructure into self-hostable local capability.

Read [Power Routing & Sharing](Power-Routing-and-Sharing).

## Four core counterpowers

> **Verification + Contestation + Substitution + Exit**

### Verification
Can affected actors independently inspect the claim, rule, calculation, provenance, signature, workflow, or authority?

### Contestation
Can they challenge it through a meaningful process?

### Substitution
Can another provider, branch, reader, model, implementation, institution, or workflow perform the function?

### Exit
Can participants leave without disproportionate loss of identity, history, knowledge, reputation, data, rights, or continuity?

## Non-terminal architecture

No architecture can guarantee that capture never occurs.

The more realistic objective is:

> **Instance Capture ≠ System Capture**

A captured organization, deployment, branch, registry, or brand should not automatically capture the whole capability ecology.

## Conversion firewalls

Useful functional control should not silently become unrelated sovereignty.

```text
expertise → advice                 yes
expertise → permanent rule         no automatic path

money → contribution               yes
money → more political votes       no automatic path

technical administration → upkeep yes
technical administration → policy no automatic path
```

## Evolutionary governance

Even the constitution is an approximation.

Forks and branches are not only emergency escape valves. They are normal mechanisms of institutional experimentation.

The final safeguard is therefore not a perfect center. It is the preservation of a system in which alternatives can remain viable enough to challenge, recombine with, or replace dominant implementations.

> **Canonical repo source:** `showcase/08-governance-after-capability.md`

# Power Dynamics

**The kOA Power Show — mapped as a cross-domain power surface.**

Power Dynamics starts from an affirmative question:

> **How much effective capability can kOA generate for people, groups, organizations, institutions, and federations?**

Then it asks the constitutional question:

> **When those capabilities become socially consequential, where does the resulting power go?**

## Why is kOA immensely powerful?

> **Because kOA connects almost the entire lifecycle by which distributed resources become collective agency, reusable assets, and greater future capability.**

The v6.2 shorthand is:

> **Breadth × Composition × Closure × Memory × Compounding × Replication × Routing**

```text
People + knowledge + AI + networks + credibility + money + tools
                         ↓
                STRUCTURE + CONNECT
                         ↓
Meaning → Knowledge → Discovery → Credibility → Judgment
                         ↓
              Decision → Allocation
                         ↓
            Coordination → Execution
                         ↓
            Results → Memory → Returns
                         ↓
                  Reinvestment
                         ↓
            Greater future puissance
                         ↓
             Replication / federation
                         ↓
        Sharing / contestation / evolution
```

The core loop remains:

> **Know → Choose → Act → Remember → Know better**

A collective that can close that loop does not merely solve one problem. It can become more capable **because it has acted before**.

## The major evolution in v6: see the whole power surface

kOA can generate or amplify capability across many domains at once:

| Surface | Examples of generated capability | Relational power that can emerge |
|---|---|---|
| Epistemic / semantic | validate, structure, compare, translate, preserve | canon, classification, framing, admissibility |
| Cognitive / AI | reason, synthesize, model, translate | prioritization, hidden framing, dependency |
| Information / attention | publish, discover, rank, amplify | visibility, agenda, moderation |
| Credibility / reputation | make competence and contribution legible | standing, certification, opportunity |
| Network / affiliation | connect, recruit, broker, mobilize | inclusion, exclusion, access |
| Political / constitutional | deliberate, decide, authorize, amend | mandate, veto, legitimacy, decision |
| Economic | mobilize money, assets, labor, infrastructure | bargaining, property, allocation |
| Monetary | organize liquidity, payment, settlement, credit | issuance, rail, reserve, access |
| Administrative / operational | assign, route, escalate, execute, close | timing, workload, approval, closure |
| Technical / infrastructural | build, host, update, verify, reproduce | compatibility, standards, dependency |
| Narrative / symbolic / brand | transmit, affiliate, mobilize, remember | framing, legitimacy-by-association, gatekeeping |
| Mnemonic / temporal | preserve precedent, reasons, timing, lineage | memory, delay, succession |
| Evolutionary / counterpower | contest, substitute, fork, recombine | incumbent displacement, branch mobility, exit |

Read the [kOA Power Surface](Power-Surface).

## Political, economic, and monetary power are distinct

v6 makes this separation explicit:

```text
POLITICAL
standing / agenda / mandate / legitimacy / decision rights

ECONOMIC
money / property / labor / assets / funding / allocation

MONETARY
issuance / payment rails / settlement / reserves / liquidity / credit
```

They interact strongly, but they are not interchangeable. The most consequential mechanisms are often **cross-domain conversions**:

```text
knowledge → credibility → visibility → agenda → political influence
money → infrastructure → reach → attention → political leverage
technical control → defaults → dependency → de facto governance
brand → affiliation → revenue → infrastructure → greater capability
```

Read [Political, Economic & Monetary Power](Political-Economic-and-Monetary-Power) and [Cross-Domain Power Conversion](Cross-Domain-Power-Conversion).

## The second major evolution: power routing

The project does not only ask how to generate leverage. It asks where four things go:

```text
CAPABILITY → who can do more?
AUTHORITY  → who can decide or control more?
RETURNS    → who receives reusable value?
OPTIONALITY→ who can refuse, leave, reproduce, fork, or wait?
```

Examples of deliberate routing include:

- expertise → advice, not sovereignty;
- money → funding, not more political standing;
- ethical fiction → symbolic puissance plus explicit self-binding;
- Koali → plural, duplicable distributions rather than terminal personal ownership;
- Kristal → portable knowledge and competing representations;
- infrastructure → self-hosting, federation, rollback, substitution;
- dominant branches → branch mobility rather than final authority.

Read [Power Routing, Sharing & Devolution](Power-Routing-and-Sharing).


## Propagation: when returns activate capability elsewhere

Power Dynamics now distinguishes local compounding from propagation.

```text
compounding → past action makes the same system more capable
propagation → a reusable return lowers the activation barrier elsewhere
coupled propagation → several loops exchange returns
```

For kOA this includes evidence → credibility → access, revenue → development, training → operators, integration → wider capability, language support → new communities, and documentation → lower review cost.

Read [Propagation Engine](Propagation-Engine) and [kOA Propagation & Adoption](kOA-Propagation-and-Adoption).

## The mature Power Show

```text
GENERATE immense puissance
        ↓
COMPOSE capabilities
        ↓
CLOSE the knowledge-to-action loop
        ↓
PRESERVE memory and returns
        ↓
COMPOUND future capability
        ↓
EXPOSE cross-domain conversions
        ↓
ROUTE capability / authority / returns / optionality
        ↓
BLOCK automatic sovereignty conversion
        ↓
VERIFY / CONTEST / SUBSTITUTE / EXIT / FORK
        ↓
EVOLVE
```

> **Strong distributed puissance. Weak sovereignty.**

## Reading paths

**Fastest explanation:** [Why kOA Is Immensely Powerful](Why-kOA-Is-Immensely-Powerful) → [Power Surface](Power-Surface) → [Power Show](Power-Show).

**Cross-domain power:** [Power Domains](Power-Domains) → [Political, Economic & Monetary Power](Political-Economic-and-Monetary-Power) → [Cross-Domain Power Conversion](Cross-Domain-Power-Conversion).

**Governance:** [Power Routing & Sharing](Power-Routing-and-Sharing) → [Power Constitution](Power-Constitution) → [Conversion Firewalls](Power-Conversion-Firewalls) → [Counterpowers & Legibility](Counterpowers-and-Legibility).

**kOA architecture:** [kOA System Map](kOA-System-Map) → [Component Power Matrix](Component-Power-Matrix) → [Know → Choose → Act → Remember](Know-Choose-Act-Remember).

**Audit:** [Power Surface Audit](Power-Surface-Audit) → [Power Routing Audit](Power-Routing-Audit) → [Power Analysis Method](Power-Analysis-Method) → [Constitutional Tests](Constitutional-Tests).

## Canonical vocabulary

> **Resource / Asset → Potential → Capacity → Puissance → Power Relation → Effect → Return**

`Puissance` is effective, mobilizable capacity. `Power` is relational: it appears when that puissance changes another actor's effective possibilities.

See the [Glossary](Glossary).
