# Propagation Engine

Power Dynamics distinguishes **compounding** from **propagation**.

```text
COMPOUNDING
A acts → reusable return → A becomes more capable

PROPAGATION
A acts → reusable return → activation barrier for B falls → B becomes capable

COUPLED PROPAGATION
A strengthens B → B strengthens C → C strengthens A
```

For kOA, propagation can occur through reusable money, evidence, credibility, code, tools, operators, integrations, language resources, documentation, content, relationships, and institutional access.

## Example coupled loop

```text
technical capability
→ World / demonstration
→ institutional pilot
→ evidence
→ credibility
→ more institutional access
→ deployment / managed service
→ revenue
→ development
→ greater technical capability
```

Additional loops can attach through SmartVote, Kintsugi, UCKK, multilingual expansion, machine-readable documentation, federation, and founder decentering.

The constitutional condition is:

> **Healthy propagation increases distributed capability faster than terminal dependency.**

Positive feedback therefore needs governors: domain-bounded expertise, baseline/advisory separation, authority boundaries, self-hosting, exit, forkability, optional federation, provenance, publication gates, founder opposition, and `money ≠ votes / brand ≠ truth / credibility ≠ sovereignty` firewalls.

See the repository canonical page `PROPAGATION.md` for the full analysis.

# Property & Territorial Power

> **Core question:** Who controls scarce physical or legal assets and the enforceable right to exclude others from them?

## Generated puissance

Effective capability to **own, use, lease, sell, collateralize, restrict access to, or allocate scarce property and territory**.

## Relational power that can emerge

Exclusion power, access power, ownership leverage, bargaining power, territorial control.

## Typical conversion paths

- `property → collateral / finance`
- `ownership → access conditions`
- `territory → infrastructure control`
- `IP / trademark → commercial or symbolic leverage`

## Routing / counterpower direction

- distinguish protocol commons from scarce brand or property stewardship
- audit ownership dependencies
- preserve substitution where feasible
- avoid treating ownership of one asset as sovereignty over unrelated functions

## kOA touchpoints

- brand / trademark governance can create property-like choke points
- infrastructure ownership matters when local operation depends on one host or asset
- Power Dynamics treats property as one domain among several rather than the definition of all power

## Analytical note

Property power is strongest where the controlled asset is scarce and substitutes are weak.

This is an analytical domain profile, not a claim that every listed mechanism is fully implemented. Use [Technical Evidence Map](Technical-Evidence-Map) when moving from architecture to current-state implementation claims.

> **Canonical v6 repo sources:** `POWER-SURFACE.md`, `domains/README.md`, `README.md`

# GitHub Wiki Setup — v6.2

This directory is ready to be used as the separate Git repository behind the `Power-Dynamics` GitHub Wiki.

## Publication flow

1. Enable Wiki in the main repository.
2. Clone the wiki repository created by GitHub, normally `Power-Dynamics.wiki.git`.
3. Replace its contents with this directory.
4. Commit and push.

GitHub Wiki uses `Home.md` as the landing page and `_Sidebar.md` / `_Footer.md` as navigation chrome.

## GitHub Wiki link rule

Keep files as `.md`, but internal wiki links **must not include `.md`**:

```md
[Power Surface](Power-Surface)
[Political Power](Political-and-Constitutional-Power)
```

Rendered URL:

```text
https://github.com/Rejean-McCormick/Power-Dynamics/wiki/Power-Surface
```

Do not link rendered navigation to `Power-Surface.md`, and do not use `raw.githubusercontent.com` when you want formatted Markdown.

## Canonical-source rule

The main Power Dynamics repository remains canonical. The wiki is the reader-facing synthesis layer. Update canonical definitions in the repo first, then propagate them here.

## v6.2 front door

The recommended public entry sequence is:

> **Why kOA Is Immensely Powerful → Power Surface → Power Show → Power Routing → Constitution**


## v6.2 academic layer

The wiki includes a reader-facing [Academic Lineage](Academic-Lineage) synchronized from the canonical repo `Academic-Lineage/` directory. Keep canonical research claims in the main repository first, then propagate changes here.

# Recognition Loop and Incumbency

A healthy recognition loop:

`Contribution → Evidence → Recognition → Opportunity → More Contribution`

can become:

`Recognition → Visibility → Opportunity → Recognition`

The second loop lets incumbency reproduce itself even when new evidence is weak. Branch mobility, portable evidence, domain-bounded credibility, time decay, and alternative discovery policies can counterbalance this dynamic.

> **Canonical repo source:** `cases/recognition-loop.md`

# Recombination and Replacement

Evolution is not always replacement.

Branches may coexist, specialize, merge, import specific improvements, recombine into a new branch, decline, or be replaced.

A healthy ecosystem preserves enough lineage to explain how later branches inherit or reject earlier decisions. Replacement should not erase history.

> **Canonical repo source:** `branch-ecology/recombination-replacement.md`

# Research and Literature

Power Dynamics sits across political theory, sociology of power, political economy, information systems, science and technology studies, network science, cybernetics, organizational theory, social epistemology, social choice, expertise research, and software architecture.

The external literature is a **constraint and comparison layer**, not a substitute for the project's own corpus and not a claim that cited scholars endorse kOA.

## Academic Lineage

The v6.2 wiki now includes a structured [Academic Lineage](Academic-Lineage) that maps established academic findings and problem formulations to Power Dynamics concepts and selected kOA mechanisms.

Each connection is documented using four questions:

1. **Academic finding or problem** — what the literature actually established or argued.
2. **Power Dynamics correspondence** — which concept, audit surface, or kOA mechanism is structurally related.
3. **Design implication** — what should be tested, measured, separated, or protected.
4. **Limit of correspondence** — what the citation does **not** prove.

This distinction matters. The lineage documents **convergence of problems, concepts, and mechanisms**; it does not retroactively claim historical influence where none is documented.

## Main research lineages

- [Power, Dependence, Domination & Conversion](Academic-Lineage-01-Power-Dependence-and-Domination) — Dahl, Bachrach & Baratz, Lukes, Emerson, Cook et al., Pfeffer & Salancik, French & Raven, Pettit, Morriss, Foucault, Crozier & Friedberg.
- [Polycentric Governance & Institutions](Academic-Lineage-02-Polycentric-Governance-and-Institutions) — Ostrom, Crawford & Ostrom, rules-about-rules, federation, subsidiarity and constitutional levels.
- [Modularity, Standards & Path Dependence](Academic-Lineage-03-Modularity-Standards-and-Path-Dependence) — Simon, Baldwin & Clark, Farrell & Saloner, Katz & Shapiro, Pierson; including interface power, installed-base power and ossification.
- [Open Source, Exit, Fork & Peer Production](Academic-Lineage-04-Open-Source-Exit-Fork-and-Peer-Production) — Hirschman, Nyman & Lindman, O'Mahony & Ferraro, Benkler.
- [Network Power & Social Capital](Academic-Lineage-05-Network-Power-and-Social-Capital) — Freeman, Bonacich, Granovetter, Burt, Coleman.
- [Expertise, SmartVote & Collective Judgment](Academic-Lineage-06-Expertise-SmartVote-and-Collective-Judgment) — Chi, Ericsson & Lehmann, Goldman, Cooke, Budescu & Chen, Hong & Page, Mannes et al., Arrow, Gibbard, Satterthwaite and Tetlock.
- [Metrics, Reputation & Gaming](Academic-Lineage-07-Metrics-Reputation-and-Gaming) — Campbell, Merton and related metric/reputation failure modes.
- [Classification, Infrastructure & Code](Academic-Lineage-08-Classification-Infrastructure-and-Code) — Winner, Lessig, Bowker & Star, Star & Ruhleder.
- [Epistemic Power, Provenance & Plurality](Academic-Lineage-09-Epistemic-Power-Provenance-and-Plurality) — Fricker, Goldman, AGM belief revision, Dung argumentation.
- [Systems, Cybernetics & Adaptation](Academic-Lineage-10-Systems-Cybernetics-and-Adaptation) — Ashby, Beer, Meadows.
- [Collective Action, Mobilization & Diffusion](Academic-Lineage-11-Collective-Action-Mobilization-and-Diffusion) — Olson, McCarthy & Zald, Granovetter thresholds, Rogers, Centola.
- [Capabilities, Concerted Power & Legibility](Academic-Lineage-12-Capabilities-Concerted-Power-and-Legibility) — Sen, Arendt, James C. Scott.

The consolidated source list is in the [Academic Lineage Bibliography](Academic-Lineage-Bibliography).

## Why this matters for Power Dynamics

The literature does not merely provide citations for claims already made. It supplies **failure modes and adversarial tests**. Examples include:

- dependency can survive decentralization if meaningful alternatives do not exist;
- compatibility can create installed-base power and protocol lock-in;
- modularity can shift sovereignty into interface control;
- expertise can be domain-bounded while still creating prestige spillovers;
- voting and aggregation mechanisms face manipulation and impossibility constraints;
- reputation metrics can become targets and self-reinforcing prestige loops;
- infrastructure and classification can govern invisibly through defaults and standards;
- legibility can empower coordination while flattening local knowledge;
- exit is only credible when assets, knowledge and operating capacity are portable.

The strongest use of academic lineage is therefore not ceremonial citation. It is **better names for failure modes, sharper tests, and clearer limits on what an architecture can claim to have solved**.

> **Canonical repo sources:** `Academic-Lineage/`, `research/disciplinary-map.md`, `research/literature-roadmap.md`

# Resources, Assets, Stocks and Reservoirs

Power Dynamics begins with what is available or accumulated, but it does not call every resource “power.” Resources become politically or socially consequential only through organization, mobilization, composition, and relation. This page keeps stock, potential, capacity, and puissance distinct.


## Canonical resource model

Resources are inputs, not power by definition. A stock becomes politically significant only through the capacities, conversions, dependencies, and relations it supports.

### Material, financial, and time resources

Money, property, energy, equipment, housing, physical infrastructure, and time are foundational because many other capacities depend on them. Time is especially political: formal rights can be unusable when work, debt, bureaucracy, care burdens, or procedure consume the time needed to exercise them. Financial resources are unusually convertible because they can purchase labor, infrastructure, attention, expertise, legal support, mobility, or time.

### Knowledge, expertise, and memory

Knowledge can be shared without consuming the original resource, which makes it strongly generative. Expertise is not identical to credentials or reputation; it includes accumulated practice, methods, understanding, and evidence. Memory includes decisions, reasons, failures, outcomes, and operational traces. When made reusable, memory lowers the cost of future action.

### Networks, reputation, and brand

Relationships, trust, reputation, affiliations, and brand recognition can lower coordination cost and open pathways to attention, collaboration, funding, and institutional access. They are not automatically authority. These resources are especially prone to positive feedback and incumbency effects.

### Technical, data, and infrastructure

Code, schemas, hardware, APIs, data, keys, registries, protocols, and operational knowledge can be small artifacts with large systemic effects. Their significance depends on custody, reproducibility, substitution, recovery, and whether others must pass through them.

### Identity, rights, and optionality

Identity, privacy, credentials, legal rights, licenses, and preserved choices condition future action. Optionality is valuable because action has not yet been irreversibly committed. A private identity or unpublished corpus can preserve future timing and strategy. Optionality is potential until it becomes effectively usable.


## Stock, capacity, flow and return

An earlier useful distinction is preserved in refined form.

- **Stock** — what has accumulated.
- **Capacity** — what the actor or system is organized to do.
- **Puissance** — what it can effectively mobilize under current conditions.
- **Flow** — the practical movement or throughput of resources, action, information, or effects.
- **Return** — what comes back into the system and can be reused.

The combined model is:

> **Stock → Potential → Capacity → Puissance → Flow / Relation → Effect → Return → New Stock**

This is especially useful for compounding analysis. A large stock can remain politically weak if it cannot be mobilized or converted; a small initial stock can compound through code, experience, methods, reputation, and network assets.


> **Canonical repo source:** `resources/README.md`, `framework/06-stock-capacity-flow-return.md`

# Semantic Layer — SemantiK, SenTient and Multilingualism

Semantic puissance concerns the ability to structure categories, relations, ambiguity, and meaning. In kOA this is not a cosmetic layer: meaning precedes knowledge, discoverability, credibility, and decision. Multilingual participation is part of the same capability because language can otherwise become an access boundary.


## SemantiK and SenTient

SemantiK Architect concerns the architecture of meaning: concepts, categories, relations, contexts, and multilingual mappings. SenTient concerns reconciliation of ambiguous or uncertain propositions, entity relations, and semantic interpretation.

These functions create semantic puissance but can also create **constitutive power** if their categories become mandatory or opaque.

Semantic sovereignty therefore requires versioned ontologies, explicit uncertainty, local control where appropriate, traceable transformations, and meaningful alternatives.


## Multilingualism

Multilingualism creates two distinct capacities.

**Reach puissance** expands geographic and linguistic participation.

**Semantic participation puissance** lets communities participate without abandoning their language, categories, and local meaning structures.

Translation is therefore political. A system can claim global reach while imposing one dominant ontology through translation defaults. Traceability, uncertainty, local revision, and plural mappings are important safeguards.


> **Canonical repo source:** `koa/semantik-sentient.md`, `koa/multilingualism.md`

# SmartVote and Discoverability Power

SmartVote demonstrates that discovery is governance.

What rises is more likely to be reused, cited, learned, funded, adopted, or built. Even when final political equality is preserved, ranking criteria can shape the agenda before the final decision.

The case separates baseline support, credibility signals, weighted advisory readings, and discoverability effects rather than collapsing them into one score.

> **Canonical repo source:** `cases/smartvote-discoverability.md`

AUTHORITY: reference
CONTENT_ROLE: knowledge

CONTENT_BYTES: 2809

# SmartVote

SmartVote creates **comparative judgment and discoverability puissance** by preserving multiple readings of support rather than hiding evaluation inside one opaque score.

A critical separation is between baseline/raw democratic readings and expertise/ethics or other advisory readings.

The political baseline is not silently superseded by advisory weight. **Domain-bounded credibility informs rather than automatically governs.**

Power relations arise through lens selection, defaults, criteria, discoverability, ranking, and institutional interpretation of weighted results. Those elements require explicit registries, versioning, auditability, and branch alternatives.

## Academic constraints

The external-research layer documents several constraints directly relevant to SmartVote:

- **Expert performance is domain- and task-bounded.** Expertise in one field should not silently become generalized epistemic authority. Work by Chi, Feltovich & Glaser and by Ericsson & Lehmann supports treating expertise as structured by domain and task.
- **Nonexperts still face an expert-selection problem.** Goldman frames the problem of deciding which experts to trust when the evaluator is not already an expert in the same domain.
- **Performance-sensitive aggregation can help in some settings.** Cooke, Budescu & Chen, and related work show that calibration or measured contribution can sometimes improve expert aggregation.
- **Diversity can add independent problem-solving value.** Hong & Page provide an important counterweight to simply selecting the highest-scoring individuals.
- **Selection itself changes the crowd.** Mannes, Soll & Larrick show that selected subsets can sometimes outperform undifferentiated aggregation, but the selection rule becomes a new governance surface.
- **No voting rule escapes all structural tradeoffs.** Arrow, Gibbard and Satterthwaite establish deep limits and manipulation constraints for broad classes of social-choice mechanisms.
- **Metrics become gaming surfaces when consequential.** Campbell's law and cumulative-advantage dynamics associated with Merton matter whenever EkoH, credibility, ranking or reputation affects visibility or opportunity.

These constraints support the architecture's separation of **baseline political standing**, **domain-scoped expertise**, and **advisory lenses**. They do **not** validate a particular SmartVote formula, weight, threshold or deployment.

See [Expertise, SmartVote & Collective Judgment](Academic-Lineage-06-Expertise-SmartVote-and-Collective-Judgment) and [Metrics, Reputation & Gaming](Academic-Lineage-07-Metrics-Reputation-and-Gaming).

> **Canonical repo source:** `koa/smartvote.md`

# Sources, Provenance and Epistemic Status

Power Dynamics separates where a claim came from, how mature it is, and what kind of claim it is. The framework should never silently turn a legacy proposal into current doctrine or an analytical reconstruction into a source quotation.


## Source layer

The supplied corpus is the repository's primary evidence base.

Source IDs are stable references to the 28 supplied files. They identify provenance, not endorsement or current status.

The repo distinguishes:

- **FOUNDATIONAL / CURRENT-CORE** source material;
- **CURRENT-EXPERIMENTAL** designs;
- **ANALYTICAL** thesis drafts and reconstructions;
- **LEGACY-PROPOSAL** material preserved for genealogy and stress testing.

The source corpus contains real internal tensions. These should not be silently reconciled.


## Epistemic rules

1. Preserve source terminology when summarizing a source.
2. Label reconstructions and new concepts explicitly.
3. Do not silently convert legacy proposals into current principles.
4. Preserve disagreements among current documents.
5. Treat technical claims about current implementation as provisional unless supported by direct specifications or code.
6. Avoid treating analytical thesis drafts as canonical technical specifications.
7. Do not infer perfect measurement of competence, ethics, credibility, legitimacy, or truth from the presence of a scoring mechanism.
8. Use source IDs for provenance and technical evidence for implementation claims.


## Source status

`CURRENT-CORE` and `CURRENT-EXPERIMENTAL` describe recent design direction. `LEGACY-PROPOSAL` identifies earlier proposals retained for historical and stress-test value. `ANALYTICAL-RECONSTRUCTION` identifies synthesis rather than primary architecture.

Status is not truth ranking. A legacy proposal can still expose a useful mechanism, and a recent analytical document is not automatically a canonical technical specification.

See [Corpus Map](Corpus-Map) for per-source classification.


> **Canonical repo source:** `sources/README.md`, `sources/epistemic-rules.md`, `sources/source-status.md`

---

Some current design intentions are explicitly confirmed during repository development but are not yet represented by a canonical specification in the supplied corpus. - The **Power Surface**, cross-domain conversion model, and routing model are analytical reconstructions built from the corpus and current design discussion.
- **KingCoin** remains `LEGACY-PROPOSAL`: analytically useful for monetary / political / administrative stacking, but not current kOA doctrine.
- **Koali ownership decoupling / plural distributions** remain architect-confirmed design direction until current technical specifications demonstrate the intended portability, policy identity, interoperability, and independent operation.
- Domain profiles in this wiki are reader-facing analytical syntheses. They should not be read as implementation claims.

# System Power Profile

A System Power Profile turns the theory into an audit surface. It identifies resource stocks, capability surfaces, domains, conversion paths, dependencies, power relations, returns, counterpowers, and changes over time.


## System profile

A system profile should summarize major resource stocks, main forms of puissance, major power relations, mediators and bottlenecks, conversion pathways, return loops, concentration, stacking, dependency gradients, counterpowers, branch ecology, founder/maintainer dependencies, temporal risks, and technical-evidence confidence.

This is the preferred top-level audit output.


## Flow, conversion and capture audit

This audit combines the stock/flow, conversion, and stacking views.

### Stock → Capacity → Flow

For each major resource, trace:

`Stock → Potential → Capacity → Puissance → Flow → Relation → Return`

### Conversion map

List all material conversions and intermediate steps. Example:

`expertise evidence → credibility signal → ranking weight → discoverability → opportunity`

Ask who controls each step, whether the conversion crosses domains, whether it is contestable, and whether an alternative branch can implement a different policy.

### Stacking

Inventory every function held by the same actor or node. Ask what leverage appears only because several legitimate functions are combined.

### Capture

Test whether functional control has become durable cross-domain power:

`Functional Control → Cross-Domain Power`

Document mandate drift, default expansion, dependency growth, recognition loops, reduced branch mobility, and rising exit costs.


## Temporal evolution

Power relations change over time.

### Formation phase
Who supplies scarce initial capability?

### Scaling phase
Which feedback loops, network effects, standards, and dependencies emerge?

### Succession / maturity phase
Can authority, knowledge, keys, brand stewardship, and maintenance transfer without collapse?

Also inspect decay: credibility can expire, technical knowledge can become obsolete, and branches can lose fitness. A static snapshot is insufficient.


> **Canonical repo source:** `assessment/system-profile.md`, `assessment/flow-conversion-capture-audit.md`, `assessment/temporal-evolution.md`

# Technical & Infrastructural Power

> **Core question:** Who can build, host, sign, update, standardize, interoperate, withdraw, verify, or reproduce critical infrastructure?

## Generated puissance

Effective capability to **build, deploy, verify, operate, repair, self-host, reproduce, federate, rollback, and substitute systems**.

## Relational power that can emerge

Compatibility power, standards power, update power, signing power, access power, dependency power.

## Typical conversion paths

- `technical control → defaults`
- `defaults → dependency`
- `infrastructure → standards`
- `hosting / signing → access / continuity`

## Routing / counterpower direction

- self-hosting and capsules
- federation and substitution
- rollback and independent verification
- explicit technical boundaries and multiple operators

## kOA touchpoints

- Capsules support portability, verification, local operation, and rollback
- federation aims to scale capability without one mandatory operator
- technical evidence remains necessary for keys, registries, update paths, and current topology

## Analytical note

Infrastructure can be empowering as a capability surface and dominating as a gate. The distinction depends on dependency and counterpower.

This is an analytical domain profile, not a claim that every listed mechanism is fully implemented. Use [Technical Evidence Map](Technical-Evidence-Map) when moving from architecture to current-state implementation claims.

> **Canonical v6 repo sources:** `POWER-SURFACE.md`, `domains/README.md`, `README.md`

# Technical Evidence Map

This page distinguishes conceptual power analysis from the technical artifacts needed to verify where power is actually encoded: policies, defaults, roles, registries, keys, routing, schemas, and deployment controls.

The 28-file corpus is strong on philosophy, architecture summaries, institutional proposals, and analytical reconstructions. A rigorous next-stage audit should add direct technical specifications for the exact loci where **capacity becomes puissance and puissance becomes relational power**.

## Highest-priority evidence to import

1. **Civilizational Coherence — power/capture chapters**: capture, lock-in, verification, contestation, substitution, exit.
2. **kOA Digital Ecosystem Layer Model / Layer 0**: separation of functions and mandate boundaries.
3. **Kristal authority registry, trust roots, reader policies, federation/versioning**: epistemic puissance, authority, branch lineage.
4. **EkoH ScoreConfiguration, ScoreHistory, visibility/access policies**: who defines credibility readings and how those readings affect opportunity.
5. **SmartVote lens registry, defaults, raw/weighted storage, version governance**: decision puissance, discoverability, advisory power.
6. **Orgo RBAC, routing, escalation, closure, override rules**: operational puissance and execution power.
7. **Konnaxion moderation, discovery, identity, ranking policies**: collective puissance, attention, agenda power.
8. **Capsule Manager signing, registry, update, rollback, deployment profiles**: deployment puissance, infrastructural dependency, choke points.
9. **Koali distribution specification**: credibility portability, alternative distributions, interoperability.
10. **Brand / trademark governance and founder-control inventory**: symbolic assets, domains, repos, release keys, registries, marks, current relational control.

## Audit rule

A normative statement such as “EkoH is non-sovereign” is not sufficient technical evidence. The audit must identify the configuration, permission, key, role, registry, default, or protocol boundary that explains:

1. what resource and potential exist;
2. what becomes usable capacity;
3. what becomes effective puissance;
4. where that puissance creates relational power;
5. which counterpowers constrain that relation.

## Mobilization / mediation evidence

Also inspect release permissions, publication gates, queueing, rate limits, ranking, recommendation, translation, summarization, distribution dependencies, analytics feedback, alternative channels, moderation controls, update keys, and bottlenecks.

> **Canonical repo source:** `sources/technical-evidence-map.md`

---

[Home](Home) · [Power Show](Power-Show) · [Glossary](Glossary)

## Koali protocolization / ownership decoupling

To verify the current design intention, the repo still needs direct technical evidence for the common kernel, evidence portability, distribution identity, policy versioning, fork procedure, discovery/registry design, signing governance, and the relationship between official branding and alternative implementations.

# Three-Axis Model

Every Power Dynamics analysis should locate a phenomenon on three axes.

## Axis 1 — Ontological state

> **Resource → Potential → Capacity → Puissance → Power Relation → Effect → Return**

This axis asks **what state the phenomenon is in**.

## Axis 2 — Dynamic

Formation, mobilization, composition, conversion, return, reinvestment, compounding, concentration, stacking, capture, counterpower, diversification, evolution, decay, transfer, succession, externalities, scale, routing, sharing, and devolution.

This axis asks **what is happening to it over time**.

## Axis 3 — Domain

v6 makes the domain axis explicit rather than leaving it as a loose list:

- political / constitutional;
- economic / allocation;
- monetary / credit / liquidity;
- epistemic / semantic;
- information / attention / media;
- credibility / reputation;
- network / affiliation;
- administrative / operational;
- technical / infrastructural;
- narrative / symbolic / brand;
- cognitive / AI;
- mnemonic / temporal;
- evolutionary / counterpower;
- property / territorial.

This axis asks **where the resource, capability, or relation operates**.

A media organization can be stronger in attention power while a state is stronger in legal enforcement. A payment rail can be stronger in monetary access while a platform is stronger in discoverability. “More powerful” without naming the domain and mechanism is incomplete.

## Cross-domain movement

The domain axis also allows explicit conversion analysis:

```text
source domain
→ conversion mechanism
→ destination domain
→ resulting leverage
```

See [Power Domains](Power-Domains) and [Cross-Domain Power Conversion](Cross-Domain-Power-Conversion).

> **Canonical v6 repo sources:** `framework/03-three-axis-model.md` in the v6 tree, `domains/README.md`, `framework/10-cross-domain-power-conversion.md`.

---

# Unpublished Corpus as an Epistemic Reservoir

A large unpublished corpus is best modeled as an **epistemic resource reservoir**, not stored power.

It can preserve knowledge stock, narrative options, release timing, future publication paths, synthesis possibilities, and documentation value.

External influence depends on later transformations:

`organization → release → distribution → comprehension → credibility → reuse → adoption`

The case demonstrates why reservoir size, puissance, reach, influence, and authority are distinct.

> **Canonical repo source:** `cases/unpublished-corpus-reservoir.md`

# Why kOA Is Immensely Powerful

> **kOA is immensely powerful because it connects the full lifecycle of collective capability and makes the outputs of one cycle usable as inputs to the next.**

## Seven multipliers

### 1. Breadth

kOA touches many domains at once: meaning, knowledge, cognition, discovery, credibility, networks, politics, economics, coordination, execution, memory, infrastructure, narrative, federation, and evolution.

### 2. Composition

Those capabilities can strengthen one another.

```text
structured knowledge
+ AI
+ credibility
+ discoverability
+ decision
+ workflow
+ memory
= generated collective puissance
```

### 3. Closure

kOA attempts to close the loop:

> **Know → Choose → Act → Remember → Know better**

Information can become judgment; judgment can become action; action can become reusable memory.

### 4. Memory

The system can preserve reasons, evidence, provenance, decisions, failures, outcomes, and implementation history instead of forcing every cycle to restart.

### 5. Compounding

Action can leave assets behind:

> **Action → Return → Asset → Reinvestment → Greater Future Puissance**

### 6. Replication

Portability, self-hosting, capsules, federation, and branchability can transform one functioning capability into many independently operated capabilities.

### 7. Routing

kOA does not only ask how to generate leverage. It also creates mechanisms to redirect it:

- expertise → advice rather than sovereignty;
- money → funding rather than more votes;
- narrative → mobilization plus ethical self-binding rather than unquestionable legitimacy;
- Koali → plural distributions rather than one terminal credibility owner;
- infrastructure → self-hosting and substitution rather than captivity;
- dominance → branch mobility rather than final authority.

## The master loop

```text
Distributed resources
→ meaning
→ structured knowledge
→ discovery
→ credibility
→ deliberation
→ decision / allocation
→ coordination
→ execution
→ results
→ memory / returns
→ reinvestment
→ greater capability
→ federation / replication
→ contestation / evolution
```

This is why the system can become more capable **because it has already acted**.

## The central warning

The same architecture that increases collective capability can also create:

- semantic power;
- agenda power;
- political power;
- economic and monetary leverage;
- administrative power;
- infrastructural dependency;
- narrative capture;
- founder power;
- standards and registry power.

Therefore:

> **The Power Show and the Power Constitution are the same project viewed from opposite directions.**

The first asks how much capability can be generated. The second asks whether those capabilities remain governable.

- [Power Surface](Power-Surface)
- [Composition Engine](Composition-Engine)
- [Compounding Engine](Compounding-Engine)
- [Power Routing & Sharing](Power-Routing-and-Sharing)

> **Canonical v6 repo source:** `showcase/00-why-koa-is-immensely-powerful.md`

# Why kOA Is Not Just Software

Software is one layer of kOA's capability architecture.

The broader system combines:

- knowledge structures;
- semantic conventions;
- provenance;
- credibility interpretations;
- coordination rules;
- decision procedures;
- workflows;
- institutional memory;
- identity and affiliation;
- deployment infrastructure;
- governance;
- branching and federation;
- human and AI cognition.

That means kOA can affect not only computation but **what a collective can know, notice, compare, decide, coordinate, preserve, and reproduce**.

## Capability infrastructure

A useful definition is:

> **kOA is capability infrastructure for converting knowledge into governed collective action and action back into reusable knowledge.**

Its deeper loop is:

```text
knowledge
→ orientation
→ choice
→ execution
→ experience
→ memory
→ better knowledge
```

## Why infrastructure matters politically

Infrastructure disappears into normality when it works. Once widely adopted, technical defaults and interfaces can become de facto political boundaries.

A powerful infrastructure may therefore create:

- standard-setting power;
- compatibility power;
- identity gatekeeping;
- discoverability power;
- administrative power;
- mnemonic power;
- dependency and lock-in.

The objective is not to avoid infrastructure. It is to make infrastructural puissance **governable, portable, inspectable, and non-terminal**.

> **Canonical repo source:** `showcase/09-why-this-is-not-just-software.md`

# Zero-Skim Philanthropy

The Zero-Skim model separates economic contribution from democratic allocation.

`Money → donation resources` does not automatically become `Money → more votes`.

A cooperative rail can provide financial and compliance puissance while members retain allocation authority through one-member-one-vote mechanisms.

> **Canonical repo source:** `cases/zero-skim-philanthropy.md`

---
maturity: "OPEN-QUESTION"
claim_type: "EXTERNAL-RESEARCH"
scope: "GENERAL"
version: "6.2"
title: "Power, Dependence, Domination, and Conversion"
source_basis:
---
# Power, Dependence, Domination, and Conversion

This lineage is the closest academic neighbor to the repo's distinction between **capability** and **relational power**.

## Robert A. Dahl — relational power

Dahl's 1957 formulation treats power as a relation in which A can affect B's behavior. This is a useful baseline for Power Dynamics because it rejects `power = possession` and directs attention toward **relations, scope, and effects**.

**Correspondence:** `Resource → Potential → Capacity → Puissance → Power Relation → Effect`.

**Limit:** Dahl's behavioral formulation is narrower than Power Dynamics' interest in agenda formation, infrastructures, dependency, and latent possibility.

Reference: Robert A. Dahl, “The Concept of Power,” *Behavioral Science* 2(3), 1957. https://doi.org/10.1002/bs.3830020303

## Bachrach & Baratz; Steven Lukes — visible decisions are not the whole power surface

Bachrach and Baratz's “two faces” argument expands power analysis beyond observable decisions to **nondecision and agenda control**. Lukes' later three-dimensional account adds preference-shaping and forms of domination that may not present as open conflict.

**Correspondence:** the **pre-political chain** in Power Dynamics: classification, discoverability, ranking, agenda formation, defaults, and legitimacy can shape outcomes before a formal vote occurs.

**Design implication:** an audit that only inspects formal decision rights will miss important upstream power.

References:
- Peter Bachrach and Morton S. Baratz, “Two Faces of Power,” *American Political Science Review* 56(4), 1962. https://doi.org/10.2307/1952796
- Steven Lukes, *Power: A Radical View*, first published 1974; later editions expand the argument. https://www.bloomsbury.com/ca/power-9781350544758/

## Richard M. Emerson — power-dependence relations

Emerson's 1962 theory is exceptionally close to the repo's dependency logic: power emerges from a relation in which one actor depends on resources, services, or opportunities controlled by another, and dependence is reduced when meaningful alternatives exist.

**Correspondence:**

- `Strong capability. Weak sovereignty.`
- `credible exit`;
- substitutability;
- alternative providers / branches;
- dependency-exit audits;
- founder decentering through reproducibility.

**Design implication:** for each critical capability, ask not only *who can provide it?* but also *who becomes dependent on that provider, and what alternatives remain viable?*

Reference: Richard M. Emerson, “Power-Dependence Relations,” *American Sociological Review* 27(1), 1962, pp. 31–41. https://www.jstor.org/stable/2089716

## Cook, Emerson, Gillmore & Yamagishi — network structure changes power

Cook et al. experimentally studied power in exchange networks and found that simple centrality measures can fail where dependency structure predicts power more accurately.

**Correspondence:** Power Dynamics should not infer `most connected = most powerful`. Network power depends on **alternatives, scarcity, exchange structure, and dependency topology**.

**Design implication:** network audits should combine centrality with dependency / substitutability measures.

Reference: Karen S. Cook, Richard M. Emerson, Mary R. Gillmore, and Toshio Yamagishi, “The Distribution of Power in Exchange Networks,” *American Journal of Sociology* 89(2), 1983. https://doi.org/10.1086/227866

## Pfeffer & Salancik — resource dependence

Resource Dependence Theory studies how organizations become constrained by actors controlling critical external resources.

**Correspondence:** DNS, hosting, registries, funding, compute, signing keys, maintainer knowledge, payment rails, distribution, trademarks, and scarce operators can all create hidden sovereignty even when governance documents appear decentralized.

**Design implication:** extend the power audit from formal authority to **critical-resource dependency**.

Reference: Jeffrey Pfeffer and Gerald R. Salancik, *The External Control of Organizations: A Resource Dependence Perspective*, 1978. https://www.gsb.stanford.edu/faculty-research/books/external-control-organizations-resource-dependence-perspective

## French & Raven — distinct bases of social power

French and Raven distinguished bases of social influence such as legitimate, reward, coercive, expert, and referent power.

**Correspondence:** Power Dynamics' separation of domains and conversion firewalls. Expertise, money, brand, technical administration, and political standing should not be treated as interchangeable permissions.

**Design implication:** identify the **source** of leverage before deciding the legitimate **scope** of that leverage.

Reference: John R. P. French Jr. and Bertram Raven, “The Bases of Social Power,” in *Studies in Social Power*, 1959. https://cdn.isr.umich.edu/pubFiles/historicPublications/studiesinsocialpower_1413_.pdf

## Pierre Bourdieu — multiple forms of capital and convertibility

Bourdieu distinguishes economic, cultural, and social forms of capital and emphasizes that resources can be accumulated and converted across social fields. His broader work on symbolic power is especially relevant to cases where recognition and classification become consequential without direct coercion.

**Correspondence:** Power Dynamics' cross-domain conversion model: money, network position, knowledge, credentials, reputation, and symbolic standing can become leverage in other domains.

**Design implication:** conversion pathways need explicit firewalls because different resources do not remain in the domains where they were originally earned.

Reference: Pierre Bourdieu, “The Forms of Capital,” in John G. Richardson (ed.), *Handbook of Theory and Research for the Sociology of Education*, Greenwood, 1986, pp. 241–258.

## Philip Pettit — non-domination

Republican non-domination focuses not merely on actual interference, but on exposure to **arbitrary power**: another actor may possess uncontrolled capacity to interfere even if it is not presently exercised.

**Correspondence:** `non-domination-terminality`, revocability, contestability, exit, and constitutional limits on successful functions becoming terminal sovereigns.

**Design implication:** “the administrator is benevolent” is not a sufficient safeguard. The architecture should reduce arbitrary dependency itself.

Reference: Philip Pettit, *Republicanism: A Theory of Freedom and Government*. Oxford University Press. https://doi.org/10.1093/0198296428.001.0001

## Peter Morriss — power as ability

Morriss' philosophical analysis is especially relevant to the repo's use of **puissance**. He argues that power is usefully understood as a form of **ability / capacity**, not only as successful influence over another actor.

**Correspondence:** this is a close conceptual neighbor to the repo's insistence that `capacity / puissance` must be distinguished from an exercised `power relation`.

**Limit:** similar vocabulary does not imply identity between Morriss' analysis and the Power Dynamics ontology.

Reference: Peter Morriss, *Power: A Philosophical Analysis*, Manchester University Press, 1987.

## Michel Foucault — power relations revealed through resistance

Foucault's “The Subject and Power” emphasizes studying concrete relations of power and using forms of resistance to locate their points of application.

**Correspondence:** counterpower, contestation, founder-opposition rehearsals, and adversarial audits can be used diagnostically: resistance is not merely a threat to the system; it is evidence about where power actually sits.

Reference: Michel Foucault, “The Subject and Power,” *Critical Inquiry* 8(4), 1982. https://doi.org/10.1086/448181

## Crozier & Friedberg — zones of uncertainty

Crozier and Friedberg's strategic analysis of organizations emphasizes how actors gain leverage through organizational **zones of uncertainty** — areas where rules do not fully determine outcomes and where some actors control information, expertise, external relations, or operational contingencies.

**Correspondence:** hidden maintainer knowledge, undocumented exceptions, manual override paths, scarce operational expertise, and discretionary queues can create power even when formal permissions are symmetrical.

**Design implication:** audit uncertainty control, not only explicit roles.

Reference: Michel Crozier and Erhard Friedberg, *L'acteur et le système: les contraintes de l'action collective*, Seuil, 1977.

## Power conversion as the synthesis opportunity

The literature above generally studies **particular relations or domains**. Power Dynamics adds a cross-domain question:

> **How does legitimate capability in one domain convert into leverage in another?**

Examples include:

```text
expertise → credibility → agenda leverage
brand → attention → affiliation → revenue
money → infrastructure → dependency
technical administration → defaults → governance leverage
network brokerage → opportunity allocation
```

The academic lineage strengthens the need for **Power Conversion Firewalls**. It does not by itself establish that the current firewalls are sufficient.

# Polycentric Governance and Institutional Grammar

## Elinor Ostrom — polycentric governance

Ostrom's work rejects the assumption that complex collective-action problems must be solved either by a single state hierarchy or by simple privatization. Polycentric systems can contain multiple, partially autonomous centers of decision-making that interact, experiment, monitor, and learn.

**Correspondence:**

- federation and subsidiarity;
- local autonomy;
- independently governed branches;
- multiple implementations sharing protocols;
- `Founder-powered → Architecture-powered → Protocol-powered → Ecosystem-powered`.

**Design implication:** decentralization is not merely “no center.” A viable polycentric ecology still needs rules for coordination, conflict, monitoring, nested levels, and cross-scale learning.

References:
- Elinor Ostrom, *Governing the Commons*, Cambridge University Press, 1990.
- Elinor Ostrom, “Beyond Markets and States: Polycentric Governance of Complex Economic Systems,” Nobel Prize Lecture, 2009/2010. https://www.nobelprize.org/prizes/economic-sciences/2009/ostrom/lecture/

## Crawford & Ostrom — a grammar of institutions

Crawford and Ostrom proposed a structured grammar for analyzing institutional statements — rules, norms, and shared strategies.

**Correspondence:** kOA's ambition to represent institutional structures as explicit, inspectable, versionable artifacts rather than leaving them as tacit custom or inaccessible legal prose.

**Design implication:** common syntax can increase legibility without requiring identical institutions. This is a strong academic precedent for **shared grammar + local institutional variation**.

Reference: Sue E. S. Crawford and Elinor Ostrom, “A Grammar of Institutions,” *American Political Science Review* 89(3), 1995. https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/american-political-science-review/article/grammar-of-institutions/7D37CD3BC5ED2D9FD57D2EE292958F47

## Institutional levels: operational, collective-choice, constitutional

The Institutional Analysis and Development tradition distinguishes rules that govern day-to-day action from rules governing collective choice and higher-level constitutional rules that determine who can alter the system itself.

**Correspondence:** Power Dynamics' authority architecture and the recurring question:

> **Who can change the rules that determine who can change the rules?**

**Design implication:** audits should classify authority by level, not merely by feature. A system can be democratic operationally while constitutional amendment remains concentrated.

## Polycentricity is not automatically non-dominating

A federation can still contain:

- a dominant reference implementation;
- a registry everyone depends on;
- an interface controlled by one maintainer group;
- a financially dominant node;
- a credibility system that becomes de facto mandatory.

Therefore:

> **Polycentric topology ≠ non-domination.**

Power Dynamics adds dependency, conversion, and exit analysis to the polycentric frame.

---
maturity: "OPEN-QUESTION"
claim_type: "EXTERNAL-RESEARCH"
scope: "GENERAL"
version: "6.2"
title: "Modularity, Standards, Compatibility, and Path Dependence"
source_basis:
---
# Modularity, Standards, Compatibility, and Path Dependence

This lineage is central to the repo's attempt to combine **local freedom** with a **common compatible substrate**.

## Herbert Simon — near decomposability

Simon argued that complex systems often become tractable and evolvable when organized into subsystems with relatively strong internal interaction and weaker interaction across subsystem boundaries.

**Correspondence:** capsules, replaceable services, bounded modules, and the ambition to let parts of kOA evolve without requiring total-system redesign.

Reference: Herbert A. Simon, “The Architecture of Complexity,” *Proceedings of the American Philosophical Society* 106(6), 1962.

## Baldwin & Clark — modularity and visible design rules

Baldwin and Clark formalize modular systems in which components can change independently while obeying shared **design rules** / interfaces.

**Correspondence:**

> **Freeze only what is necessary for interoperability; allow everything else to mutate.**

This provides a strong academic parallel to kOA's intended combination of independent implementations, shared artifact contracts, and branch evolution.

Reference: Carliss Y. Baldwin and Kim B. Clark, *Design Rules, Volume 1: The Power of Modularity*, MIT Press, 2000. https://doi.org/10.7551/mitpress/2366.001.0001

## Interface power

The same modularity that creates freedom can create a new concentration point: **the interface itself**.

A party that controls the shared interface can shape all modules while appearing not to control them internally.

Power Dynamics should therefore treat shared interfaces as **governance surfaces**:

- Who can change the interface?
- Who bears migration cost?
- Can an older version remain viable?
- Can adapters bridge divergent versions?
- Can competing standards coexist?
- Who certifies compatibility?

## Farrell & Saloner — excess inertia

Farrell and Saloner analyze how the benefits of standardization can trap an industry in an inferior or obsolete standard under incomplete information — **excess inertia**.

**Correspondence:** a successful kOA compatibility layer could become difficult to change precisely because compatibility is valuable.

Reference: Joseph Farrell and Garth Saloner, “Standardization, Compatibility, and Innovation,” *RAND Journal of Economics* 16(1), 1985, pp. 70–83. https://doi.org/10.2307/2555589

## Katz & Shapiro — network externalities and installed-base power

Network goods become more valuable as more compatible users / complements exist. Adoption itself can therefore create structural advantage.

**New Power Dynamics concept proposed:** **Installed-Base Power** — the ability of an adopted protocol, implementation, or standard to constrain future choice because participants depend on continued interoperability.

Reference: Michael L. Katz and Carl Shapiro, “Network Externalities, Competition, and Compatibility,” *American Economic Review* 75(3), 1985, pp. 424–440. https://www.jstor.org/stable/1814809

## Paul Pierson — path dependence and increasing returns

Pierson describes how increasing returns can make timing and sequence matter, amplify initially small differences, and make established paths difficult to reverse.

**Correspondence:** early defaults, registries, naming conventions, scoring systems, reference implementations, and trust roots can become constitutionally significant long after they were introduced as convenience choices.

Reference: Paul Pierson, “Increasing Returns, Path Dependence, and the Study of Politics,” *American Political Science Review* 94(2), 2000, pp. 251–267. https://doi.org/10.2307/2586011

## Proposed constitutional invariant

> **Compatibility must not imply constitutional subordination.**

Related audit propositions:

- `Shared interface → explicit governance surface`;
- `Installed base → migration obligation`;
- `Compatibility benefit → ossification risk`;
- `Forkability without bridges → fragmentation risk`;
- `Version pluralism + adapters → evolutionary optionality`.

The academic literature establishes the failure modes. It does not prove that a particular kOA versioning or federation scheme solves them.


================================================================================================
FILE: power_dynamics/Academic-Lineage/04-open-source-exit-fork-and-peer-production.md
AUTHORITY: reference
CONTENT_ROLE: knowledge
CONTENT_SHA256: 2eae3ea0c2b2f51c8dcec9f3aa0136ccf7e1a48c0850430c53d16ee02961f80a

title: "Exit, Voice, Forking, and Peer Production"
source_basis:
---
# Exit, Voice, Forking, and Peer Production

## Albert O. Hirschman — exit and voice

Hirschman's classic distinction asks how members respond when an organization deteriorates: they may **exit** or use **voice** to attempt repair.

**Correspondence:** Power Dynamics deliberately combines contestation and credible exit rather than treating either as sufficient alone.

**Design implication:**

- voice without exit can leave participants captive;
- exit without voice can cause unnecessary fragmentation;
- formal exit without portable assets may be practically meaningless.

Reference: Albert O. Hirschman, *Exit, Voice, and Loyalty*, Harvard University Press, 1970.

## Nyman & Lindman — forking as governance

Nyman and Lindman explicitly analyze code forking as a governance and sustainability mechanism across software, community, and ecosystem levels.

**Correspondence:** forkability, branch mobility, incumbent replacement, and the idea that the **possibility** of a fork can constrain maintainers before a fork occurs.

Reference: Linus Nyman and Juho Lindman, “Code Forking, Governance, and Sustainability in Open Source Software,” *Technology Innovation Management Review*, January 2013. https://www.timreview.ca/article/644

## O'Mahony & Ferraro — authority can emerge without conventional ownership

Their study of an open-source community shows the development of formal authority combined with democratic mechanisms and evolving conceptions of legitimate leadership.

**Correspondence:** a commons still needs governance; `open` does not mean `authority-free`. The relevant question is whether authority remains bounded, contestable, and revisable.

Reference: Siobhán O'Mahony and Fabrizio Ferraro, “The Emergence of Governance in an Open Source Community,” *Academy of Management Journal* 50(5), 2007. https://doi.org/10.5465/amj.2007.27169153

## Yochai Benkler — commons-based peer production

Benkler's analysis of Linux and peer production demonstrates a mode of large-scale production that is neither simply firm hierarchy nor price-mediated market organization.

**Correspondence:** independent contributors can take responsibility for branches, modules, documentation, institutions, or deployments without becoming employees of a central owner.

Reference: Yochai Benkler, “Coase's Penguin, or, Linux and The Nature of the Firm,” *Yale Law Journal* 112, 2002. https://yalelawjournal.org/article/coases-penguin-or-linux-and-the-nature-of-the-firm

## Founder decentering as protocolization

Power Dynamics goes beyond “the founder promises not to interfere.” It seeks conversion of founder-specific capability into:

- explicit schemas;
- reproducible builds;
- documentation;
- public interfaces;
- branch rights;
- independent maintenance capacity;
- portable data and artifacts.

The academic lineage above supports the importance of these mechanisms while also warning that informal reputation, scarce expertise, release keys, brand ownership, and installed-base dominance can preserve founder power after formal control has been removed.

# Network Power, Brokerage, Diffusion, and Social Capital

## Linton Freeman — centrality is multidimensional

Freeman clarified distinct concepts of centrality rather than treating “central” as one thing.

**Correspondence:** network / affiliation power should be measured through several structural lenses rather than a single universal score.

Reference: Linton C. Freeman, “Centrality in Social Networks: Conceptual Clarification,” *Social Networks* 1(3), 1978–79. https://doi.org/10.1016/0378-8733(78)90021-7

## Phillip Bonacich — centrality is not automatically power

Bonacich explicitly addresses cases in which network centrality and power diverge.

**Correspondence:** Power Dynamics should resist `many connections = sovereignty`. Dependency structure and the positions of one's neighbors matter.

Reference: Phillip Bonacich, “Power and Centrality: A Family of Measures,” *American Journal of Sociology* 92(5), 1987. https://doi.org/10.1086/228631

## Mark Granovetter — weak ties and cross-cluster diffusion

Weak ties can connect otherwise separated social clusters and play an outsized role in information diffusion, mobility, and community organization.

**Correspondence:** mobilization capacity is not only a function of audience size or strong affiliation. **Bridging ties** can matter disproportionately for spreading initiatives across communities.

Reference: Mark S. Granovetter, “The Strength of Weak Ties,” *American Journal of Sociology* 78(6), 1973. https://doi.org/10.1086/225469

## Ronald Burt — structural holes and brokerage

Burt's structural-hole theory treats gaps between otherwise disconnected actors as opportunities for brokerage, information access, and strategic advantage.

**Correspondence:** identify actors or services whose brokerage position becomes a hidden allocation or gatekeeping surface.

Reference: Ronald S. Burt, *Structural Holes: The Social Structure of Competition*, Harvard University Press, 1992.

## James Coleman — social capital as a resource for action

Coleman describes obligations, expectations, information channels, and norms as forms of social capital that enable action.

**Correspondence:** Power Dynamics' broad resource → capacity model can explicitly recognize network relations and trust structures as **resources that alter effective possibility**.

Reference: James S. Coleman, “Social Capital in the Creation of Human Capital,” *American Journal of Sociology* 94, 1988. https://doi.org/10.1086/228943

## Audit consequence

A network power audit should inspect at least:

- centrality;
- dependency / substitutability;
- brokerage;
- bridge positions;
- control of discovery / introductions;
- access to high-trust clusters;
- concentration of communication channels;
- ability to route opportunities to others.

Network maps show topology. They do **not** by themselves show legitimacy, authority, or domination.

title: "Expertise, SmartVote, and Collective Judgment"
source_basis:

# Expertise, SmartVote, and Collective Judgment

This is the most direct academic lineage for the SmartVote principle:

> **Expertise should be bounded by domain and used as an advisory reading rather than silently converted into universal political sovereignty.**

## Chi, Feltovich & Glaser — experts structure problems differently within a domain

Their classic physics study found systematic differences between expert and novice representations: experts tended to organize problems around deeper domain principles rather than surface features.

**Correspondence:** expert standing should be attached to a **domain / problem class**, not treated as a universal personal property.

Reference: Michelene T. H. Chi, Paul J. Feltovich, and Robert Glaser, “Categorization and Representation of Physics Problems by Experts and Novices,” *Cognitive Science* 5(2), 1981. https://doi.org/10.1207/s15516709cog0502_2

## Ericsson & Lehmann — expert performance is adapted to domain-specific constraints

Their broad review emphasizes domain-specific cognitive, perceptual-motor, and physiological adaptations behind expert performance.

**Correspondence:** a universal “expert score” would collapse heterogeneous competence into an invalid abstraction. EkoH / SmartVote credibility should be scoped to demonstrable domains and tasks.

Reference: K. Anders Ericsson and Andreas C. Lehmann, “Expert and Exceptional Performance: Evidence of Maximal Adaptation to Task Constraints,” *Annual Review of Psychology* 47, 1996. https://doi.org/10.1146/annurev.psych.47.1.273

## Alvin Goldman — the layperson's expert-selection problem

Goldman analyzes how nonexperts can rationally decide which experts to trust when they cannot directly evaluate the technical subject matter.

**Correspondence:** SmartVote should not merely expose an `expert` badge. It should make evidence of expertise, track record, relevant credentials / contribution, disagreement, and source relations legible.

Reference: Alvin I. Goldman, “Experts: Which Ones Should You Trust?”, *Philosophy and Phenomenological Research* 63(1), 2001. https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1933-1592.2001.tb00093.x

## Roger Cooke — calibrated expert judgment

Cooke develops methods for eliciting, evaluating, and weighting expert judgments, including performance-based calibration rather than reliance on status alone.

**Correspondence:** `expertise → weighted advisory reading` can be grounded in observable performance and calibration rather than title-based authority.

Reference: Roger M. Cooke, *Experts in Uncertainty: Opinion and Subjective Probability in Science*, Oxford University Press, 1991. https://doi.org/10.1093/oso/9780195064650.001.0001

## Budescu & Chen — identify contributors who improve the crowd

Budescu and Chen show that aggregation can be improved by identifying judges whose forecasts consistently make positive contributions relative to the group.

**Correspondence:** reputation may be **performance-sensitive** and task-sensitive rather than merely social prestige.

Reference: David V. Budescu and Eva Chen, “Identifying Expertise to Extract the Wisdom of Crowds,” *Management Science* 61(2), 2015. https://doi.org/10.1287/mnsc.2014.1909

## Mannes, Soll & Larrick — select crowds

Their work shows that in some estimation settings, averaging judgments from a carefully selected small group can be robust and accurate.

**Correspondence:** Power Dynamics should allow multiple aggregation lenses rather than assuming either `all votes always equal epistemically` or `single best expert decides`.

Reference: Albert E. Mannes, Jack B. Soll, and Richard P. Larrick, “The Wisdom of Select Crowds,” *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology* 107(2), 2014. https://doi.org/10.1037/a0036677

## Hong & Page — diversity can outperform selection of individually strongest solvers

Under their model's conditions, a diverse group of problem solvers can outperform a group selected only for individual ability.

**Correspondence:** SmartVote should not turn expertise weighting into an anti-diversity filter. Diversity of representations and heuristics can itself be an epistemic resource.

Reference: Lu Hong and Scott E. Page, “Groups of Diverse Problem Solvers Can Outperform Groups of High-Ability Problem Solvers,” *PNAS* 101(46), 2004. https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.0403723101

## Social choice: Arrow, Gibbard, and Satterthwaite

Social choice theory supplies an important warning: there is no universal voting mechanism that simultaneously satisfies every attractive property under unrestricted conditions, and broad classes of voting systems are vulnerable to strategic manipulation.

**Correspondence:** SmartVote should present itself as a **plural, inspectable set of readings and decision aids**, not as a mathematically perfect replacement for politics.

References:
- Kenneth J. Arrow, *Social Choice and Individual Values*, 1951/1963.
- Allan Gibbard, “Manipulation of Voting Schemes: A General Result,” *Econometrica* 41(4), 1973. https://doi.org/10.2307/1914083
- Mark A. Satterthwaite, “Strategy-proofness and Arrow's Conditions,” *Journal of Economic Theory* 10(2), 1975. https://doi.org/10.1016/0022-0531(75)90050-2

## SmartVote design implications

The literature supports several strong constraints:

1. **Domain-bounded expertise** — no universal expert authority.
2. **Performance evidence** — title or prestige is not enough.
3. **Plural aggregation** — different lenses can answer different questions.
4. **Diversity preservation** — selecting only high-status experts can destroy useful heterogeneity.
5. **Political firewall** — epistemic weighting does not automatically purchase political sovereignty.
6. **Manipulation awareness** — every ranking / voting mechanism exposes gaming surfaces.
7. **Baseline visibility** — raw democratic readings should remain visible when advisory lenses are added.

None of these references validates a particular SmartVote weighting formula. They provide **design constraints and testable failure modes**.

title: "Metrics, Reputation, Prestige, and Gaming"
source_basis:
---
# Metrics, Reputation, Prestige, and Gaming

Any system that makes expertise, contribution, trust, or credibility legible creates a new power surface.

## Donald Campbell — indicators become corruption targets

Campbell's work on social indicators is associated with a recurring warning: when quantitative indicators become important targets for decision-making, actors have incentives to corrupt or optimize the indicator rather than the underlying goal.

**Correspondence:** EkoH, reputation, contribution metrics, moderation metrics, and SmartVote-derived standings must be treated as **adversarially optimizable surfaces**.

Reference: Donald T. Campbell, “Assessing the Impact of Planned Social Change,” *Evaluation and Program Planning* 2(1), 1979. https://doi.org/10.1016/0149-7189(79)90048-X

## Robert K. Merton — the Matthew effect

Merton analyzed cumulative advantage in science: recognition and resources can disproportionately accrue to already prominent actors.

**Correspondence:** credibility systems can produce a positive feedback loop:

```text
initial recognition
→ more visibility
→ more opportunities
→ more visible contribution
→ more recognition
```

**Design implication:** distinguish demonstrated contribution from **recognition compounding**, and monitor whether discoverability causes prestige to become self-validating.

Reference: Robert K. Merton, “The Matthew Effect in Science,” *Science* 159(3810), 1968. https://doi.org/10.1126/science.159.3810.56

## Prestige, reputation, and authority are not identical

Power Dynamics should retain separate concepts for:

- observed performance;
- domain expertise;
- social prestige;
- contribution history;
- institutional credential;
- political standing;
- moral / ethical trust;
- brand affiliation.

A reputation system becomes dangerous when one of these silently converts into all the others.

## Proposed audit questions

For every score or ranking:

1. What exactly is being measured?
2. Is the construct domain-specific?
3. Can participants optimize the metric without improving the underlying objective?
4. Does early visibility create cumulative advantage?
5. Are negative evidence and uncertainty preserved?
6. Is the score portable, contestable, and versioned?
7. Can users inspect the weighting rule?
8. Does the score grant authority outside the domain it measures?
9. Can alternative scoring systems coexist?
10. What happens to newcomers with no historical data?

## Proposed invariant

> **Legibility should increase informed choice without turning measurement into universal sovereignty.**

The academic literature makes the gaming and cumulative-advantage risks difficult to dismiss. It does not determine the correct scoring architecture; that remains an engineering and governance question.

# Classification, Infrastructure, Code, and Hidden Governance

This lineage supports one of Power Dynamics' most important claims:

> **Power can be embedded in technical and semantic architecture before any formal political decision occurs.**

## Langdon Winner — artifacts can embody politics

Winner argues that technical artifacts and systems can embody or require particular distributions of power and authority.

**Correspondence:** technical design choices belong on the Power Surface; they are not merely neutral implementation details.

Reference: Langdon Winner, “Do Artifacts Have Politics?”, *Daedalus* 109(1), 1980. https://www.jstor.org/stable/20024652

## Lawrence Lessig — code regulates

Lessig's work on cyberspace argues that software and hardware architecture regulate behavior alongside law, norms, and markets.

**Correspondence:** permissions, defaults, data models, ranking, reader policies, trust roots, update mechanisms, and interoperability rules are governance mechanisms.

Reference: Lawrence Lessig, *Code and Other Laws of Cyberspace*, 1999; *Code: Version 2.0*, 2006. https://hls.harvard.edu/bibliography/code-and-other-laws-of-cyberspace-version-2-0/

## Bowker & Star — classification has consequences

Bowker and Star show that classification systems and standards organize worlds, distribute visibility, and can encode consequential social assumptions.

**Correspondence:** SemantiK, Kristal schemas, ontologies, category systems, admissibility criteria, and metadata are forms of **semantic / classificatory power**.

Reference: Geoffrey C. Bowker and Susan Leigh Star, *Sorting Things Out: Classification and Its Consequences*, MIT Press, 1999. https://doi.org/10.7551/mitpress/6352.001.0001

## Star & Ruhleder — infrastructure is relational and becomes visible on breakdown

Their infrastructure studies emphasize that infrastructure is not merely equipment; it is embedded in practices, communities, standards, learned conventions, and installed bases.

**Correspondence:** kOA's technical layer should be audited in relation to actual users and institutions, not only against internal architectural elegance.

Reference: Susan Leigh Star and Karen Ruhleder, “Steps Toward an Ecology of Infrastructure,” *Information Systems Research* 7(1), 1996. https://doi.org/10.1287/isre.7.1.111

## Hidden-sovereignty audit

A technically decentralized system may still concentrate power through:

- default schemas;
- canonical registries;
- ranking algorithms;
- update keys;
- API compatibility requirements;
- moderation queues;
- model prompts / AI system instructions;
- search and discovery;
- identity providers;
- documentation that defines the “normal” implementation.

This literature strongly supports treating those mechanisms as constitutional surfaces rather than implementation trivia.

# Epistemic Power, Provenance, Disagreement, and Plurality

Kristal and Konnaxion operate in a space that social epistemology, argumentation theory, and belief-revision research have studied from different angles.

## Miranda Fricker — epistemic injustice

Fricker distinguishes forms of injustice in which a speaker is given an unfair credibility deficit or lacks shared interpretive resources for making an experience intelligible.

**Correspondence:** credibility systems, semantic categories, moderation, and expert-weighted discovery can either reduce or amplify epistemic exclusion.

**Design implication:** a credibility system should inspect not only false positive authority, but also **systematic credibility deficits** and missing conceptual categories.

Reference: Miranda Fricker, *Epistemic Injustice: Power and the Ethics of Knowing*, Oxford University Press, 2007. https://doi.org/10.1093/acprof:oso/9780198237907.001.0001

## Alvin Goldman — social epistemology and expert trust

Goldman's work treats knowledge practices as social systems that can be evaluated partly by how well they support truth-seeking and rational trust.

**Correspondence:** provenance, source comparison, expert-selection evidence, and visible disagreement should support users' own epistemic judgment rather than simply replace it with authority.

Reference: Alvin I. Goldman, “Experts: Which Ones Should You Trust?”, 2001. https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1933-1592.2001.tb00093.x

## AGM belief revision — changing a knowledge state should be explicit

Alchourrón, Gärdenfors, and Makinson formalized belief revision as a problem of changing a knowledge / belief set while preserving rational structure.

**Correspondence:** versioned Kristals, explicit revisions, lineage, conflict, supersession, and rollback are related to the general problem that **new information should alter a knowledge state without erasing how the change occurred**.

Reference: Carlos E. Alchourrón, Peter Gärdenfors, and David Makinson, “On the Logic of Theory Change: Partial Meet Contraction and Revision Functions,” *Journal of Symbolic Logic* 50(2), 1985. https://doi.org/10.2307/2274239

## Dung — argumentation frameworks and conflicting claims

Dung's abstract argumentation framework models arguments and attack relations without requiring all participants to share a single consistent knowledge base.

**Correspondence:** competing claims / Kristals can remain explicitly related through support, challenge, provenance, and evaluation rather than being collapsed into false consensus.

Reference: Phan Minh Dung, “On the Acceptability of Arguments and Its Fundamental Role in Nonmonotonic Reasoning, Logic Programming and n-Person Games,” *Artificial Intelligence* 77(2), 1995. https://doi.org/10.1016/0004-3702(94)00041-X

## Epistemic design implications

1. **Cryptographic integrity ≠ truth.**
2. **Provenance ≠ authority.**
3. **Source multiplicity ≠ source independence.**
4. **Forkability ≠ epistemic equivalence.**
5. **Consensus ≠ absence of dissent.**
6. **Credibility ≠ infallibility.**
7. **Revision should preserve lineage.**
8. **Competing worldviews should remain comparable where possible.**

The academic lineage reinforces the repo's adversarial epistemic tests. It also supplies a warning: making epistemic structure explicit does not eliminate disagreement; it makes disagreement more inspectable.

# Systems, Cybernetics, Feedback, and Adaptation

Power Dynamics is not only a theory of authority. It is also a theory of **capability formation, feedback, memory, and evolutionary adaptation**.

## W. Ross Ashby — requisite variety

Ashby's cybernetics is associated with the principle that effective regulation requires sufficient variety in the regulator to respond to the variety of disturbances in the system.

**Correspondence:** centralized simplification can fail when local contexts vary more than the center can represent. Federation, local autonomy, plural branches, and specialized modules can preserve response variety.

Reference: W. Ross Ashby, *An Introduction to Cybernetics*, 1956.

## Stafford Beer — viable systems and recursive autonomy

Beer's Viable System Model studies how viable organizations combine operational autonomy with coordination, control, intelligence, and identity across recursive levels.

**Correspondence:** kOA's local / federated architecture and the attempt to avoid both total centralization and incoherent fragmentation.

Reference: Stafford Beer, *Brain of the Firm*, 1972; later editions.

## Donella Meadows — leverage points

Meadows' systems work distinguishes interventions at different depths, from parameters to information flows, rules, system goals, and paradigms.

**Correspondence:** Power Dynamics' cross-domain approach asks whether changing a UI parameter, a ranking rule, a constitutional permission, or an underlying paradigm produces radically different leverage.

Reference: Donella H. Meadows, “Leverage Points: Places to Intervene in a System,” Sustainability Institute, 1999.

## Feedback and the kOA capability loop

The canonical kOA cycle:

> **Know → Choose → Act → Remember → Know better**

can be interpreted as a feedback architecture:

```text
state / evidence
→ interpretation
→ decision
→ action
→ result
→ memory
→ revised state / evidence
```

The key Power Dynamics addition is that **feedback channels themselves are power surfaces**. Whoever controls what gets measured, remembered, ranked, or ignored can influence future adaptation.

## Systems caution

A system can be technically adaptive while politically dominating. Cybernetic viability is not identical to legitimacy, justice, or non-domination.

Therefore the systems lineage should be paired with the constitutional lineage rather than used as a substitute for it.
