# Collective Action, Mobilization, Diffusion, and Thresholds

Power Dynamics includes a mobilization layer: resources do not become social power merely because they exist. They must be **released, routed, coordinated, and adopted**.

## Mancur Olson — collective action is not automatic

Olson's classic work shows why groups with shared interests may still fail to organize or contribute to collective goods because individual incentives and group outcomes can diverge.

**Correspondence:** kOA cannot assume that a good common objective automatically produces participation. Roles, incentives, visibility of contribution, modular tasks, local ownership, and low-friction entry all affect whether latent capacity becomes mobilized capacity.

Reference: Mancur Olson, *The Logic of Collective Action*, Harvard University Press, 1965. https://doi.org/10.4159/9780674041660

## McCarthy & Zald — resource mobilization

Resource Mobilization Theory shifts attention from grievances alone toward the resources, organizations, media relations, alliances, and structural conditions that make sustained collective action possible.

**Correspondence:** Power Dynamics' `Reservoir → Release → Flow → Mediation` model and the distinction between possessing resources and actually mobilizing them.

Reference: John D. McCarthy and Mayer N. Zald, “Resource Mobilization and Social Movements: A Partial Theory,” *American Journal of Sociology* 82(6), 1977. https://doi.org/10.1086/226464

## Granovetter — threshold models of collective behavior

Granovetter models individuals as having different participation thresholds: some act early, while others act only after enough others have acted first. Small differences in the distribution of thresholds can produce very different collective outcomes.

**Correspondence:** a network can contain substantial **latent commitment** that becomes visible only after credibility, adoption, or social proof crosses thresholds.

This directly supports analysis of:

- delayed activation of previously contacted collaborators;
- tipping dynamics;
- adoption cascades;
- why early visible participants can disproportionately affect later mobilization;
- why aggregate inactivity does not prove absence of latent support.

Reference: Mark Granovetter, “Threshold Models of Collective Behavior,” *American Journal of Sociology* 83(6), 1978. https://doi.org/10.1086/226707

## Everett Rogers — diffusion of innovations

Rogers' diffusion framework studies how innovations spread through communication channels over time and how uncertainty, social networks, and adopter categories affect uptake.

**Correspondence:** cultural / narrative corridors, demonstrations, reference implementations, peer testimony, and local adoption can be treated as parts of a diffusion system rather than generic “marketing.”

Reference: Everett M. Rogers, *Diffusion of Innovations*, 5th ed., Free Press, 2003.

## Damon Centola — complex contagion

Centola's experimental work shows that behavioral adoption can differ from simple information diffusion: some behaviors benefit from **reinforcement through multiple social contacts** rather than one weak exposure.

**Correspondence:** mobilization for costly participation may require redundant trust paths and local reinforcement, even when information itself can spread through weak ties.

Reference: Damon Centola, “The Spread of Behavior in an Online Social Network Experiment,” *Science* 329(5996), 2010. https://doi.org/10.1126/science.1185231

## Power Dynamics consequence

Mobilization should be modeled as a conversion problem:

```text
latent resource / willingness
→ visibility
→ credibility / social proof
→ threshold crossing
→ contribution
→ coordination
→ result
→ public evidence
→ lower thresholds for later participants
```

This creates a possible compounding loop, but also a capture risk: whoever controls visibility, endorsement, discovery, or narrative framing may control which latent resources ever become mobilized.

title: "Capabilities, Concerted Power, and the Risks of Legibility"
source_basis:
---
# Capabilities, Concerted Power, and the Risks of Legibility

Three academic traditions sharpen the normative stakes of Power Dynamics: **capabilities**, **collective agency**, and the dangers of making society legible through simplified administrative categories.

## Amartya Sen — capability and substantive freedom

Sen's capability approach shifts attention from resources alone toward what people are **actually able to do and be**. Resources matter partly through the substantive freedoms they make possible.

**Correspondence:** Power Dynamics' concept of **effective possibility** is structurally close to a capability-oriented question: possession is not enough; what matters is the real action space available under actual constraints.

**Design implication:** a system should not measure empowerment only through nominal access. Ask whether participants can actually use, contest, exit, reproduce, and benefit from the capability.

Reference: Amartya Sen, *Development as Freedom*, 1999/2000. https://www.penguinrandomhouse.com/books/163962/development-as-freedom-by-amartya-sen/

## Hannah Arendt — power as acting in concert

Arendt distinguishes power from simple coercion or violence and ties political power to a group's capacity to **act in concert**.

**Correspondence:** this is a useful academic neighbor to **generated collective puissance**: composition can create a capacity at group scale that no isolated participant possesses.

**Limit:** Arendt's political concept of power is normatively and philosophically distinct from the broader Power Dynamics ontology.

Reference: Hannah Arendt, *On Violence*, 1970.

## James C. Scott — legibility can become domination

Scott's *Seeing Like a State* studies failures of high-modernist schemes that simplified complex local realities into administratively legible categories and then governed through those abstractions.

**Correspondence:** kOA deliberately increases legibility — schemas, classifications, provenance, metrics, workflows, registries, institutional grammar. That is a capability **and a danger**.

**Design implication:** semantic clarity must not erase local knowledge or turn the reference model into compulsory reality.

The anti-capture answer is not “avoid legibility,” but combine legibility with:

- local override / interpretation;
- plural schemas where necessary;
- provenance and uncertainty;
- reversible mappings;
- branch alternatives;
- explicit scope;
- user-visible disagreement.

Reference: James C. Scott, *Seeing Like a State: How Certain Schemes to Improve the Human Condition Have Failed*, Yale University Press, 1998/1999. https://yalebooks.yale.edu/book/9780300078152/seeing-like-a-state/

## Synthesis

These three traditions create a useful triangle:

```text
Sen:     What can people actually do?
Arendt:  What can people do together?
Scott:   What happens when institutions simplify people in order to coordinate them?
```

Power Dynamics can use that triangle as a normative test:

> **Increase effective individual and collective capability without making the legibility infrastructure a new sovereign.**

# Academic Lineage Bibliography

This bibliography is intentionally **selective rather than exhaustive**. Entries are grouped by their strongest use in Power Dynamics. A work may belong to several groups.

## Power, dependency, domination

- Bachrach, Peter, and Morton S. Baratz. “Two Faces of Power.” *American Political Science Review* 56(4), 1962. https://doi.org/10.2307/1952796

## Candidates for later expansion

These are significant enough to deserve later treatment but are not yet integrated in detail:

- Hannah Arendt — power as acting in concert, and distinction from violence;
- Pierre Bourdieu — economic, cultural, social, and symbolic capital and conversion;
- Michel Foucault — productive / dispersed power, normalization, and conduct;
- Crozier & Friedberg — organizational power through zones of uncertainty;
- mechanism design / incentive compatibility;
- platform governance and recommender-system power;
- algorithmic accountability and AI-mediated authority;
- common-pool resource design principles in digital infrastructure;
- standards governance and Internet protocol ossification;
- epistemic democracy and deliberative systems;
- institutional diversity and comparative constitutional design.

# Academic Lineage

This directory situates **Power Dynamics** and selected kOA mechanisms beside established academic traditions that study the same classes of problems.

> **This is a lineage of problems, concepts, and convergent mechanisms — not a claim of historical influence, endorsement, or proof.**

A cited author may have identified a problem that Power Dynamics also identifies, formalized a mechanism that helps explain a Power Dynamics claim, or supplied a counterexample / impossibility result that a kOA mechanism must survive. None of that means the author endorsed kOA, and none of it validates a particular implementation.

## Why this layer exists

Power Dynamics crosses fields that are usually separated: political theory, organizational sociology, institutional economics, social choice, network science, open-source governance, systems theory, science and technology studies, social epistemology, expertise research, and software architecture.

The purpose of this directory is therefore to make four things explicit for each major connection:

1. **Academic finding or problem** — what the literature actually established or argued.
2. **Power Dynamics correspondence** — which concept, audit surface, or kOA mechanism is structurally related.
3. **Design implication** — what should be tested, measured, separated, or protected.
4. **Limit of correspondence** — what the citation does **not** prove.

## High-level map

| Academic lineage | Problem / insight | Power Dynamics correspondence |
|---|---|---|
| Dahl; Bachrach & Baratz; Lukes | power includes decisions, agenda control, and preference shaping | pre-political chain; discoverability; narrative / classification power |
| Emerson; Cook et al.; Pfeffer & Salancik | power grows from dependency and scarce alternatives | effective possibility; dependency-exit audit; strong capability / weak sovereignty |
| French & Raven; Bourdieu | power has multiple bases and convertible forms | power domains; cross-domain conversion; credibility ≠ authority |
| Pettit | freedom requires protection against arbitrary domination | non-domination terminality; contestation; credible exit |
| Ostrom; Crawford & Ostrom | polycentric governance and institutional rules can be analyzed at multiple levels | federation; subsidiarity; rules-about-rules; constitutional architecture |
| Simon; Baldwin & Clark | complex systems can remain evolvable through modularity and explicit interfaces | capsules; protocolization; branch ecology; local variation + compatibility |
| Farrell & Saloner; Katz & Shapiro; Pierson | compatibility can create lock-in, installed-base power, and path dependence | protocol power; ossification audits; migration paths; version pluralism |
| Hirschman; Nyman & Lindman; O'Mahony & Ferraro; Benkler | exit, voice, forking, peer production, and open governance constrain hierarchy | forkability; branch mobility; founder decentering; capability commons |
| Freeman; Bonacich; Granovetter; Burt; Coleman | network position, brokerage, weak ties, and social capital alter effective capacity | network / affiliation power; mobilization; brokerage and dependency audits |
| Chi; Ericsson & Lehmann; Goldman | expertise is structured, domain-bounded, and difficult for nonexperts to evaluate | SmartVote domain scopes; EkoH context; expertise → advice, not sovereignty |
| Cooke; Budescu & Chen; Mannes et al.; Hong & Page | calibrated weighting can help, but diversity and aggregation rules matter | plural lenses; measured contribution; diverse advisory readings |
| Arrow; Gibbard; Satterthwaite | collective choice mechanisms face structural impossibility and manipulation limits | SmartVote transparency; baseline vs advisory lenses; no universal perfect voting rule |
| Campbell; Merton | metrics and prestige can self-reinforce and be gamed | EkoH / reputation audits; Matthew effects; anti-Goodhart safeguards |
| Winner; Lessig; Bowker & Star; Star & Ruhleder | artifacts, code, classifications, and infrastructure embed governance | power surface; semantic power; interface power; hidden sovereignty |
| Fricker; Goldman; argumentation / belief-revision traditions | epistemic standing, testimony, disagreement, and revision are power-laden | Kristal provenance; competing Kristals; epistemic authority corruption tests |
| Ashby; Beer; Meadows | viable systems require feedback, requisite variety, adaptation, and attention to leverage points | Know → Choose → Act → Remember; local autonomy; feedback; systemic audits |
| Olson; McCarthy & Zald; Granovetter thresholds; Rogers; Centola | collective action requires mobilization, diffusion, threshold crossing, and sometimes social reinforcement | mobilization flow; latent resources; cultural corridors; adoption cascades |
| Sen; Arendt; James C. Scott | real capability, concerted action, and dangers of administrative legibility | effective possibility; generated collective puissance; plural / contestable legibility |

## Reading order

1. [`01-power-dependence-and-domination.md`](01-power-dependence-and-domination.md)
2. [`02-polycentric-governance-and-institutions.md`](02-polycentric-governance-and-institutions.md)
3. [`03-modularity-standards-and-path-dependence.md`](03-modularity-standards-and-path-dependence.md)
4. [`04-open-source-exit-fork-and-peer-production.md`](04-open-source-exit-fork-and-peer-production.md)
5. [`05-network-power-and-social-capital.md`](05-network-power-and-social-capital.md)
6. [`06-expertise-smartvote-and-collective-judgment.md`](06-expertise-smartvote-and-collective-judgment.md)
7. [`07-metrics-reputation-and-gaming.md`](07-metrics-reputation-and-gaming.md)
8. [`08-classification-infrastructure-and-code.md`](08-classification-infrastructure-and-code.md)
9. [`09-epistemic-power-provenance-and-plurality.md`](09-epistemic-power-provenance-and-plurality.md)
10. [`10-systems-cybernetics-and-adaptation.md`](10-systems-cybernetics-and-adaptation.md)
11. [`11-collective-action-mobilization-and-diffusion.md`](11-collective-action-mobilization-and-diffusion.md)
12. [`12-capabilities-concerted-power-and-legibility.md`](12-capabilities-concerted-power-and-legibility.md)
13. [`BIBLIOGRAPHY.md`](BIBLIOGRAPHY.md)

## Core caution

Academic literature can support a **problem diagnosis**, a **mechanism**, an **impossibility constraint**, or a **design principle**. It does not automatically validate:

- a specific SmartVote weighting formula;
- an EkoH scoring implementation;
- the security or decentralization of a current deployment;
- a claim that kOA is optimal;
- a claim that Power Dynamics is derived from these authors;
- or a claim that any cited scholar endorses kOA.

The strongest use of this literature is adversarial: **it gives the framework better names for failure modes and better tests for whether the architecture actually solves them.**

maturity: "CURRENT-CORE"
claim_type: "ANALYTICAL-RECONSTRUCTION"
scope: "GENERAL"
version: "6.0"
title: "Power Analysis Method"
---
# Power Analysis Method

Use this sequence for any actor, institution, subsystem, or branch.

1. **Problem / baseline** — what incapacity, dependency, or coordination problem exists?
2. **Resources** — what stocks and assets are available?
3. **Potential** — what possibilities do they create?
4. **Capacity** — what is organized enough to be used?
5. **Puissance** — what can actually be mobilized now?
6. **Mobilization** — reservoir, release, throughput, channels, mediation, amplification.
7. **Power relations** — whose possibilities change, and how?
8. **Returns** — what assets are created and who receives them?
9. **Conversion pathways** — what can become what?
10. **Stacking / concentration** — what accumulates in one node?
11. **Dependency** — what becomes hard to substitute or exit?
12. **Counterpowers** — verification, contestation, substitution, exit, fork, branch mobility.
13. **Temporal analysis** — compounding, decay, succession, lock-in.
14. **Constitutional requirements** — what should be scoped, separated, portable, or reversible?

title: "Branch Comparison"
---
# Branch Comparison

Compare branches multidimensionally rather than producing one winner score.

Suggested dimensions: evidence quality, provenance, uncertainty handling, outcomes, autonomy, auditability, privacy, interoperability, cost, complexity, accessibility, multilinguality, contestability, branch mobility, unique contribution, and complementarity.

A valid conclusion may be that no global winner is required.

---
maturity: "CURRENT-CORE"
claim_type: "ANALYTICAL-RECONSTRUCTION"
scope: "GENERAL"
version: "6.0"
title: "Capability Show Profile"
---
# Capability Show Profile

The Capability Show Profile is the preferred format for demonstrating that a system is powerful **without collapsing capability into authority**.

For each capability claim, document:

## 1. Baseline limitation

What effective possibility is currently constrained? Examples: knowledge is fragmented, expertise is hard to discover, decisions are not executable, institutional memory is weak, exit is costly.

## 2. Resource and mechanism

What resources, components, protocols, roles, or institutions are introduced?

## 3. Capacity created

What organized ability becomes available?

## 4. Puissance demonstrated

Under realistic conditions, what can the actor or collective now effectively do that it could not do before, or could only do at much higher cost?

## 5. Composition gain

Which other capabilities become more useful because this capability exists?

## 6. Returns and compounding

What reusable assets are left behind? Does the action lower future cost, increase future quality, preserve memory, expand network access, or create shared infrastructure?

## 7. Capability-to-power transition

If the capability becomes widely relied upon, which actors' access, standing, visibility, allocation, interpretation, timing, dependency, or ability to act can be affected?

## 8. Conversion pathways

What legitimate conversions are expected? What cross-domain conversions should be blocked?

## 9. Counterpowers

Can affected actors verify, contest, substitute, exit, revoke, fork, or operate through an alternative branch?

## 10. Evidence and maturity

Separate:

- source-derived architecture;
- implemented technical evidence;
- demonstrated deployment evidence;
- analytical reconstruction;
- open questions.

The goal is to make the **Power Show falsifiable and inspectable**, not merely impressive.


## Power routing

A strong capability claim should now also state:

- who gains the capability;
- what new relational leverage may arise;
- whether the mechanism redirects any leverage away from a central operator;
- who receives the returns;
- what can be duplicated, shared, self-hosted, forked, or independently maintained;
- which rights remain centralized and why.

This prevents the Power Show from confusing **a powerful ecosystem** with **a powerful owner of the ecosystem**.

---
maturity: "CURRENT-CORE"
claim_type: "ANALYTICAL-RECONSTRUCTION"
scope: "GENERAL"
version: "6.0"
title: "Constitutional Tests"
---
# Constitutional Tests

Stress-test a system with adversarial questions:

- What if the maintainer becomes hostile?
- What if a credibility policy is biased?
- What if the dominant branch stops improving?
- What if ranking favors incumbents?
- What if a signing key is lost or captured?
- What if the official brand owner disagrees with a protocol fork?
- What if a provider changes terms?
- What if AI output becomes de facto policy?
- Can affected actors verify, contest, substitute, exit, and continue?

The objective is not perfect immunity but non-terminal failure modes.

title: "Dependency and Exit Audit"
---
# Dependency and Exit Audit

Assess effective dependency through alternatives, migration time, data portability, identity portability, reputation portability, legal barriers, network loss, retraining cost, infrastructure requirements, institutional recognition, recovery, and rollback.

Report both **formal exit** and **effective exit**. Their difference is often the locus of hidden power.

---
maturity: "CURRENT-CORE"
claim_type: "ANALYTICAL-RECONSTRUCTION"
scope: "GENERAL"
version: "6.0"
title: "Flow, Conversion, Stacking, and Capture Audit"
source_basis:
  - S10
  - S15
  - S16
  - S21
  - S25
  - S28
---
# Flow, Conversion, Stacking, and Capture Audit

This audit combines the stock/flow, conversion, and stacking views.

## Stock → Capacity → Flow

For each major resource, trace:

`Stock → Potential → Capacity → Puissance → Flow → Relation → Return`

## Conversion map

List all material conversions and intermediate steps. Example:

`expertise evidence → credibility signal → ranking weight → discoverability → opportunity`

Ask who controls each step, whether the conversion crosses domains, whether it is contestable, and whether an alternative branch can implement a different policy.

## Stacking

Inventory every function held by the same actor or node. Ask what leverage appears only because several legitimate functions are combined.

## Capture

Test whether functional control has become durable cross-domain power:

`Functional Control → Cross-Domain Power`

Document mandate drift, default expansion, dependency growth, recognition loops, reduced branch mobility, and rising exit costs.

FILE: power_dynamics/assessment/power-balance-sheet.md
AUTHORITY: reference
CONTENT_ROLE: knowledge

title: "Power Balance Sheet"
---
# Power Balance Sheet

A power balance sheet maps accumulated assets and dependencies without pretending they have one common unit.

### Assets / stocks
financial, technical, epistemic, human, network, credibility, brand, institutional, operational, mnemonic, optionality.

### Dependencies / liabilities
single providers, single maintainers, remote services, legal permissions, proprietary formats, recognition monopolies, key-person knowledge, network effects, update/signing keys, funding concentration.

The purpose is to expose conversion pathways and terminal dependencies, not to produce a net “power score.”

AUTHORITY: reference

---
maturity: "CURRENT-CORE"
claim_type: "NEW-CONCEPT"
scope: "GENERAL"
version: "6.0"
title: "Power Routing Audit"
source_basis:
  - S02
  - S14
  - S15
  - S16
  - S17
  - S19
  - S21
  - S25
---
# Power Routing Audit

Use this audit after identifying a system's generated puissance.

## 1. Capability destination

- Who becomes more capable?
- Is the capability reusable outside the original operator?
- Can participants teach, reproduce, or transfer it?

## 2. Authority destination

- Who gains approval, ranking, validation, certification, moderation, routing, update, or veto rights?
- Are those rights scoped?
- Can they be revoked or substituted?

## 3. Return destination

- Who receives money, data, reputation, network effects, knowledge, code, memory, and institutional access generated by collective activity?
- Do collective returns become shared assets or private concentration?

## 4. Optionality destination

- Who can leave?
- Who can self-host?
- Who can fork?
- Who can preserve identity, history, reputation, and knowledge after exit?

## 5. Interpretive destination

- Who defines categories, credibility policies, semantic mappings, reader policies, and defaults?
- Can alternative interpretations coexist?

## 6. Self-binding

- Are ethical or constitutional commitments public?
- Can affected actors cite them against the operator or founder?
- Are they connected to procedures or only aspirational prose?

## 7. Protocolization

- Which functions remain dependent on tacit founder knowledge?
- Which have explicit specifications?
- Can another maintainer or institution reproduce the function without unique permission?

## 8. Ownership decoupling

- Is a useful function inseparable from one brand, registry, identity provider, key, or operator?
- Could a compatible alternative become socially and technically viable?

## Output

For each major capability, record:

```text
Generated puissance:
Concentration vector:
Routing mechanism:
Primary recipients:
Retained central rights:
Transferred / shared rights:
Return allocation:
Exit / substitution path:
Failure mode:
Evidence needed:
```

---
maturity: "CURRENT-CORE"

source_basis:
  - S13

  - S25
---
# Power Surface Audit

Use this audit to answer:

> **What kinds of power does this system generate, where can they convert, and where are they routed?**

## 1. Domain inventory

For each domain, record:

| Domain | Resources | Capacity | Puissance | Relational power | Main controllers |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Political | | | | | |
| Economic | | | | | |
| Monetary | | | | | |
| Epistemic | | | | | |
| Semantic | | | | | |
| Information / attention | | | | | |
| Credibility | | | | | |
| Network | | | | | |
| Administrative | | | | | |
| Technical / infrastructure | | | | | |
| Narrative / brand | | | | | |
| Cognitive / AI | | | | | |
| Mnemonic / temporal | | | | | |
| Evolutionary / counterpower | | | | | |

## 2. Conversion inventory

For every major conversion:

```text
source domain
→ mechanism
→ destination domain
→ resulting leverage
```

Record whether the conversion is:

- explicit or hidden;
- intended or emergent;
- reversible or sticky;
- scoped or cross-mandate;
- contestable or terminal.

## 3. Stacking test

Which actor or node controls multiple high-leverage surfaces?

Look especially for combinations of:

- identity;
- credibility;
- discoverability;
- allocation;
- workflow;
- infrastructure;
- signing / updates;
- brand / certification;
- monetary rails.

## 4. Routing test

For each major capability, identify who receives:

- **capability**;
- **authority**;
- **returns**;
- **optionality**.

## 5. Counterpower test

Can affected actors effectively:

- verify?
- contest?
- substitute?
- exit?
- self-host?
- fork?
- preserve identity / history / evidence?
- gather support for an alternative?

## 6. Terminality test

Ask:

> If this controller fails, captures the function, changes policy arbitrarily, or disappears, can the ecosystem continue through another viable path?

If not, the surface may be **terminal power**.

## 7. Output

A complete audit should produce:

1. a domain map;
2. a conversion graph;
3. a stacking map;
4. a routing map;
5. a counterpower map;
6. a terminality assessment;
7. a list of technical evidence still required.

FILE: power_dynamics/assessment/propagation-audit.md
AUTHORITY: reference
CONTENT_ROLE: knowledge

---
maturity: "CURRENT-CORE"
claim_type: "ANALYTICAL-RECONSTRUCTION"
scope: "GENERAL"
version: "6.3"
title: "Propagation Audit"
source_basis:
  - S21
  - S25
  - S29
---
# Propagation Audit

Use this audit to determine whether a claimed growth mechanism actually propagates capability or merely creates attention, dependency, or local compounding.

## A. Loop identity

1. What is the loop called?
2. What actor / branch / domain owns each stage?
3. What begins the loop?
4. What event counts as closure?

## B. Return

5. What reusable return is produced?
6. Is it rival or non-rival?
7. Is it portable?
8. Does it retain provenance?
9. Who receives it?

## C. Propagation

10. Which other actor / branch can consume the return?
11. What activation barrier does it lower?
12. How much does it lower that barrier?
13. Can the transfer occur without founder intervention?
14. Can the transfer occur without mandatory central service dependency?

## D. Coupling

15. Which other loop does this feed?
16. Does that loop return an asset back?
17. Is the coupling intentional or accidental?
18. What shared dependency could break both loops?

## E. Governance

19. What authority grows as the loop grows?
20. What cross-domain conversion becomes possible?
21. What prevents money, credibility, brand, expertise, or infrastructure from becoming unrelated sovereignty?
22. What exit / substitution / fork remains practical?

## F. Damping

23. What causes saturation?
24. What slows or stops the loop when quality declines?
25. What signal distinguishes healthy growth from runaway growth?
26. Can operators intentionally pause or remain local?

## G. Evidence

27. Is the loop only designed, demonstrated, used, repeated, independently replicated, or federated?
28. What metric is currently observed?
29. What metric is only projected?
30. What result would falsify the mechanism?

## H. Founder-dependency test

31. Founder minutes per cycle?
32. Founder-only credentials / keys / relationships?
33. Founder-only interpretive knowledge?
34. Can another operator close the loop?
35. Can another operator teach a third operator?

A claimed propagation mechanism should not be considered mature until at least one non-founder actor has closed the relevant loop independently.

Assessment converts Power Dynamics into repeatable audits.

## Core instruments

- [`analysis-method.md`](analysis-method.md) — general method.

- [`branch-comparison.md`](branch-comparison.md) — compare alternatives without assuming one final metric.
- [`temporal-evolution.md`](temporal-evolution.md) — inspect change, drift, succession, and compounding over time.
- [`system-profile.md`](system-profile.md) — system-level summary.

# Temporal Evolution Analysis

Power relations change over time.

### Formation phase
Who supplies scarce initial capability?

### Scaling phase
Which feedback loops, network effects, standards, and dependencies emerge?

### Succession / maturity phase
Can authority, knowledge, keys, brand stewardship, and maintenance transfer without collapse?

Also inspect decay: credibility can expire, technical knowledge can become obsolete, and branches can lose fitness. A static snapshot is insufficient.

# Branch Mobility in Practice

Branch mobility is the practical path from alternative to viable competitor or complement.

A high-mobility ecosystem lets a branch:

`form → preserve lineage → interoperate → demonstrate → be discovered → gather support → deploy → influence / coexist / replace`.

Low branch mobility can create dogma without explicit censorship: alternatives formally exist but cannot accumulate enough recognition, compatibility, or infrastructure to matter.

# Complementarity and Anti-Dogma

Complementarity exists when several branches jointly provide more value than a single universal branch.

Scientific, legal, historical, local, experiential, or cultural representations may answer different questions. Credibility systems can specialize in academic, practical, or community evidence.

Complementarity is not relativism. Branches remain comparable on explicit dimensions.

Dogma, in this framework, is repetition protected from meaningful comparison, revision, or added value. Useful plurality contributes new evidence, context, method, ontology, implementation, translation, or function.

> **No branch should become final merely because alternatives cannot be meaningfully formed, compared, supported, or adopted.**

# Improvement, Support, Adoption, and Dominance

An alternative should be able to say not only “we disagree,” but **what changed and what value was added**.

Possible demonstrations include stronger evidence, better provenance, lower error, lower cost, higher autonomy, better accessibility, improved multilinguality, better outcomes, stronger privacy, better local fit, or improved interoperability.

Support, adoption, and dominance are distinct. A branch may be intellectually supported but rarely deployed, or dominant because of history, compatibility, network effects, official status, or funding.

> **Dominance ≠ Truth ≠ Quality ≠ Legitimacy**

No universal scalar is required. Improvement can be multidimensional and context-dependent.

# Lineage and Competing Kristals

Kristals can preserve divergent claims, evidence, scopes, policies, and contexts without forcing immediate universal convergence.

Two Kristals may each claim better representation of a phenomenon. The system should preserve sources, assumptions, validation policy, scope, uncertainty, lineage, added evidence, and unresolved disagreement.

A later Kristal may synthesize, specialize, or challenge both. The objective is cumulative epistemic evolution rather than winner-take-all truth declaration.

Branch plurality does not imply epistemic equivalence. A branch can be preserved because it is culturally meaningful, institutionally adopted, experimentally useful, adversarial, or fictional while still carrying weaker evidentiary support than another branch. Lineage should make both **difference of worldview** and **difference of support** visible.

---
# Branch Ecology

Branch ecology is the **evolutionary end of the Power Show**.

A system is more capable when it can not only act, but also **improve the mechanisms through which it knows, chooses, acts, and remembers**.

Branches are therefore not merely emergency responses to capture. They are normal instruments of experimentation under permanent imperfection.

The ecology asks:

- how lineage and competing Kristals are preserved;
- how complementarity avoids dogma without collapsing into relativism;
- how alternatives demonstrate added value;
- how support, adoption, dominance, truth, and quality remain distinct;
- whether a better branch can actually become viable;
- how branches coexist, specialize, recombine, merge, or replace one another.

The key capability is **branch mobility**: not only the formal right to fork, but the practical ability to preserve provenance, interoperate, attract support, gain discoverability, and eventually influence or replace a dominant branch.

> **The core protects evolution; it does not freeze truth.**

# Recombination and Replacement

Evolution is not always replacement.

Branches may coexist, specialize, merge, import specific improvements, recombine into a new branch, decline, or be replaced.

A healthy ecosystem preserves enough lineage to explain how later branches inherit or reject earlier decisions. Replacement should not erase history.

# AI and Hidden Sovereignty

AI can be formally advisory while becoming practically sovereign if institutions cannot understand, challenge, reproduce, or override its outputs.

Signals include automatic adoption of model recommendations, inaccessible prompts or policies, no governed fallback, dependence on a remote provider, or model outputs treated as authoritative evidence by default.

The case tests the principle `AI assists under governance`.

source_basis:

---
# Competing and Complementary Kristals

Two Kristals can each claim a better representation without requiring immediate centralized adjudication.

The system should preserve lineage, provenance, evidence, policy differences, scope, uncertainty, and added value.

A third branch may synthesize or specialize. The governance capability is the ability to compare and evolve rather than repeat one official answer without added value.

## Pluralism is not epistemic equivalence

Forkability protects the right to maintain incompatible representations. It does not require the system to pretend that all branches have equal support.

A branch may encode a religious cosmology, a minority scientific interpretation, a deliberately adversarial worldview, or an explicitly fictional universe. The system should preserve the branch's own axioms and lineage while also making differences in evidence, provenance, uncertainty, contradiction, and validation policy legible.

> **Freedom to fork a knowledge representation is not ownership of truth, and it is not immunity from comparison with evidence.**

A reference or institutionally stewarded Kristal should therefore function as a visible reference branch, not as an irreversible global truth monopoly.

FILE: power_dynamics/cases/epistemic-authority-corruption.md
AUTHORITY: reference
CONTENT_ROLE: knowledge

---
maturity: "DESIGN-DIRECTION"
claim_type: "USER-CONFIRMED-DESIGN"
scope: "kOA"
version: "6.1"
title: "Epistemic Authority Corruption Drill"
source_basis:
---
# Epistemic Authority Corruption Drill

A forkable knowledge system must remain usable even when an actor deliberately tries to make a false worldview look well supported.

Current architect-confirmed design includes an adversarial Kristal centered on a known false conclusion — a flat-Earth worldview — so that the system can study **how apparent knowledge can be manufactured** without ambiguity about the ground truth.

The object of study is not the truth of the flat-Earth claim. The object is the **construction of epistemic authority around it**.

## Power conversion under test

```text
claims / documents / credentials / popularity / citations
→ apparent credibility
→ apparent source multiplicity
→ discoverability and repetition
→ perceived consensus
→ epistemic authority
```

The stress test asks where these conversions become misleading and which controls reveal the distortion.

## Threat classes

The drill is intended to expose classes of corruption such as:

- unverified or fabricated authority claims;
- circular citation;
- dependent sources presented as independent confirmation;
- selective evidence and omitted counterevidence;
- prestige transfer from institutions, titles, or formats;
- popularity treated as evidentiary weight;
- semantic reframing and definition drift;
- repetition interpreted as consensus;
- scientific material quoted or transformed beyond what it actually supports.

This repository treats these as **threat classes**, not as instructions for deceiving external institutions or audiences.

## Apparent independence vs causal independence

A central analytical distinction is:

> **Many references do not imply many independent evidentiary lineages.**

For example:

```text
100 apparent references
→ 12 intermediary summaries
→ 3 shared source chains
→ 1 unsupported origin
```

A robust epistemic system should make this compression visible where provenance allows it.

## Postmortem as the primary output

The valuable artifact is the autopsy:

```text
How did the false conclusion acquire authority?
Which signals were trusted too easily?
Which sources were not actually independent?
Where did provenance become weak or opaque?
Which counterevidence was excluded?
Which detection rules worked?
Which controls failed?
What should change before the same pattern appears in a subtler domain?
```

The result can become a reusable library of epistemic pathologies for later analysis of political propaganda, pseudoscience, commercial persuasion, institutional bias, or other contested knowledge environments.

## Constitutional objective

Forkability permits incompatible representations. It does **not** require equal evidentiary standing.

> **Freedom to maintain a worldview is distinct from immunity from provenance, comparison, contradiction, and evidentiary audit.**


================================================================================================

# Ethical Fiction as Narrative Self-Binding

Fiction can generate symbolic power: attention, recognition, emotional salience, affiliation, memory, and transmission.

One distinctive mechanism in the kOA / King Klown corpus is that ethical principles are published **inside the fictional and symbolic layer itself**.

The source material includes a living ethical code that emphasizes clarity of intention, respect for living beings, vigilance against monopoly, domination, and manipulation, public recognition of contributors, self-mockery, regular ethical reassessment, revision, criticism, and non-dogmatic transmission.

## Power Dynamics interpretation

This can be understood as **narrative self-binding**.

```text
fiction / symbol
→ attention and affiliation puissance
→ explicit ethical constraints embedded in the same channel
→ public commitments
→ criticism can cite the commitments
→ symbolic authority becomes more contestable
```

The mechanism redirects some of the symbolic power away from loyalty to a persona and toward **principles that can be carried, quoted, criticized, and reinterpreted by others**.

## Why this is unusual

Public narratives often maximize persuasive power while leaving their ethical limits external, implicit, or privately intended.

Here the ethical content is part of the transmission payload.

That creates several distinct redirection effects:

1. **Self-limitation** — the narrative states what it should not become.
2. **Public contestability** — critics can compare behavior against published principles.
3. **Symbolic-capital sharing** — recognition is explicitly redirected toward contributors through gratitude, attribution, and “share the merit” norms.
4. **Anti-sanctification** — self-mockery and explicit rejection of sanctifying the Klown reduce the claim that symbolic centrality should become unquestionable authority.
5. **Transmissibility of limits** — people can copy the ethical constraints even when they reinterpret or detach from the original symbolic figure.
6. **Succession without fidelity** — the text emphasizes documentation, transmission, reinvention, and “passing the baton,” redirecting inheritance from loyalty to a founder-model toward the capacity to transform and continue the work.

The related public-brand analysis reinforces the same direction by treating the persona as a **diffusion instrument** distinct from the architect identity and explicitly oriented toward reducing founder-worship. In Power Dynamics terms, this is an attempt to route narrative puissance toward transmission rather than personal sovereignty.

## What it does not prove

Published ethics are not enforcement.

They do not prove that a founder, community, brand, or future branch will behave ethically. Narrative self-binding is strongest when paired with institutional mechanisms such as auditability, contestation, attribution, exit, forkability, and independent operation.

The constitutional objective is therefore:

> **Narrative may mobilize, but it should also carry the instructions for resisting its own capture.**


## Trickster function as counterpower training

Narrative self-binding can go beyond publishing limits. A trickster figure can also create an adversarial but bounded situation in which readers or participants must **use those limits against the symbolic figure itself**.

In Power Dynamics terms, this converts narrative centrality into a training surface for counterpower:

```text
symbolic authority
→ deliberate contradiction or provocation
→ public scrutiny
→ organized objection
→ practiced resistance to symbolic authority
```

The important output is not obedience to the trickster's intended lesson. It is a precedent that the persona can be challenged, analyzed, rejected in part, and prevented from converting attention into unquestionable authority.

A founder whose face or identity is not broadly public preserves privacy, ordinary social mobility, observational freedom, scarcity, and future reveal timing.

---
maturity: "DESIGN-DIRECTION"
claim_type: "USER-CONFIRMED-DESIGN"
scope: "kOA"
version: "6.1"
title: "Founder Opposition Rehearsal"
source_basis:
---
# Founder Opposition Rehearsal

A system is not founder-independent merely because its code can run without the founder. It must also remain capable of **disagreeing with, organizing against, and defeating the founder's proposals** when those proposals are wrong.

Current architect-confirmed design includes a deliberately adversarial civic drill built around a politically coherent party that contains one conspicuously abusive policy: a state prohibition on tattoos combined with compulsory removal of existing tattoos.

The policy is not the point of the exercise. The intended test is whether founder or persona authority can be converted into political leverage **without becoming political immunity**.

## Power conversion under test

```text
founder authorship / symbolic capital / rhetorical skill
→ attention
→ trust / deference
→ agenda influence
→ political mobilization
→ attempted policy leverage
```

The constitutional requirement is that this chain remain interruptible.

```text
attention ≠ truth
trust ≠ immunity from criticism
founder status ≠ policy authority
movement coherence ≠ mandatory agreement
```

## Civic fire-drill logic

A fire drill creates practiced evacuation pathways before a real fire exists. This case applies the same logic to counterpower.

The intended pathway is:

```text
problematic proposal
→ recognition
→ objection
→ evidence and rights analysis
→ argument registry
→ relevant expertise
→ coalition formation
→ institutional contestation
→ amendment / rejection
→ postmortem
→ reusable civic memory
```

The desired outcome is not hostility to the founder. It is **legitimate divisible agreement**: participants can support a broader project while rejecting and organizing against a specific proposal.

## Rehearsed counterpower

Power Dynamics distinguishes **formal counterpower** from **rehearsed counterpower**.

A right to contest that has never been exercised may remain socially expensive, procedurally unclear, or symbolically taboo. A successful drill can establish a precedent that opposition to the founder is normal, legitimate, and survivable.

The resulting pattern can become a reusable reference — informally, a **Tattoo Protocol** — for later cases in which an authority makes a subtler or genuinely unintended error.

## Success criteria

The drill is successful if:

- the proposal can be isolated from the rest of the platform;
- critics can organize without being treated as enemies of the whole project;
- evidence, rights, proportionality, expertise, and consequences can be made legible;
- founder symbolic power does not silently become sovereign authority;
- opposition can succeed without requiring censorship or destruction of the surrounding movement;
- the process leaves reusable institutional memory.

## Failure modes

Important failure modes include:

- supporters rationalize the proposal primarily because of founder loyalty;
- critics must exit the whole system to oppose one proposal;
- persona prestige overrides evidence or rights;
- counterpower exists formally but is too costly to exercise;
- the exercise creates a precedent of obedience rather than a precedent of contestation.

The constitutional objective is:

> **A system should not merely permit opposition to its founder. It should know how to practice it.**

# Case Studies

Cases are where the Power Show and the analytical theory meet.

Each case should ideally answer:

1. what resources are present;
2. what capability is generated;
3. how that capability composes or compounds;
4. what relations of power emerge;
5. what returns and dependencies follow;
6. what counterpowers and alternatives remain.

The cases are not all equally current. Legacy proposals are retained when they expose useful failure modes, centralization risks, or conceptual evolution.

Notable cases include:

- kOA bootstrap and founder-to-system transition;
- unpublished corpus as epistemic reservoir;
- media vs state as a domain-specific power comparison;
- SmartVote discoverability;
- competing Kristals;
- recognition loops;
- Desjardins power stacking;
- Zero-Skim allocation governance;
- brand, merchandise, and affiliation;
- AI hidden sovereignty;
- KingCoin as a legacy stress test.

- [`ethical-fiction-self-binding.md`](ethical-fiction-self-binding.md) — publishing ethical limits inside a narrative as a form of symbolic self-binding;
- [`founder-opposition-rehearsal.md`](founder-opposition-rehearsal.md) — civic fire-drill logic for practicing legitimate opposition to founder authority;
- [`epistemic-authority-corruption.md`](epistemic-authority-corruption.md) — adversarial Kristal stress test for apparent authority, source dependence, and false consensus.

# Recognition Loop and Incumbency

A healthy recognition loop:

`Contribution → Evidence → Recognition → Opportunity → More Contribution`

can become:

`Recognition → Visibility → Opportunity → Recognition`

The second loop lets incumbency reproduce itself even when new evidence is weak. Branch mobility, portable evidence, domain-bounded credibility, time decay, and alternative discovery policies can counterbalance this dynamic.

# Unpublished Corpus as an Epistemic Reservoir

A large unpublished corpus is best modeled as an **epistemic resource reservoir**, not stored power.

It can preserve knowledge stock, narrative options, release timing, future publication paths, synthesis possibilities, and documentation value.

External influence depends on later transformations:

`organization → release → distribution → comprehension → credibility → reuse → adoption`

The case demonstrates why reservoir size, puissance, reach, influence, and authority are distinct.

maturity: "CURRENT-CORE"

title: "Authority Architecture: Scope, Separation, and Centrality"
source_basis:
  - S15

  - S17

  - S21
  - S25
  - S28
---
# Authority Architecture: Scope, Separation, and Centrality

Legitimate authority should specify domain, function, jurisdiction, duration, basis, conditions, review, delegation, and revocation.

Authority in one domain should not automatically travel to another. A technical maintainer may authorize releases without deciding political outcomes. An expert may provide domain-bounded advice without receiving universal civic superiority.

Separation of functions limits conversion by assigning distinct responsibilities to distinct components or roles. Examples include separating credibility from vote results, narrative from legitimacy, execution from mandate creation, protocol commons from brand stewardship, and AI assistance from final authority.

Healthy systems also need not be centerless. A useful pattern is:

> **StrongHub + DistributedCapability + ReplicableWhole**

Important distinctions:

- Functional centrality ≠ domination
- Centralized access ≠ centralized truth
- Strong center ≠ total sovereignty
- Federation ≠ flatness
- Local sovereignty ≠ absence of a local center
- Composition does not require ownership
- Creator-owned channel ≠ mandatory dependency
- Independent reproducibility ≠ founder removal

The constitutional question is whether actors can verify, substitute, reproduce, branch, and continue when the center changes or fails.

title: "Constitutional Branch Evolution"
source_basis:
  - S13

  - S25
---
# Constitutional Branch Evolution

A stable kernel protects the ability to diverge without losing all comparability.

The stable layer may include provenance formats, identifiers, versioning, signatures, interoperability contracts, or minimum audit requirements. Branches may diverge in policies, criteria, ontologies, interfaces, rankings, governance, and implementation.

**Branch mobility** measures whether an alternative can become consequential. A branch may technically exist but remain irrelevant if it cannot be found, imported, supported, recognized, funded, deployed, or integrated.

The goal is:

> **stability at the level of evolutionary capacity, diversity at the level of implementation**

AUTHORITY: reference

maturity: "CURRENT-CORE"

title: "Power-Conversion Firewalls"
source_basis:
  - S15
  - S17
  - S21
  - S22
  - S28
---
# Power-Conversion Firewalls

A conversion firewall permits useful composition while blocking automatic cross-domain authority.

Canonical examples:

- `Expertise → Advice`, not `Expertise → Sovereignty`.
- `Money → Funding`, not `Money → More Votes`.
- `Brand → Attention`, not `Brand → Truth`.
- `Technical Administration → Maintenance`, not `Administration → Political Outcome Control`.
- `Credibility → Discoverability`, not `Credibility → Unquestionable Authority`.
- `Founder / Persona → Attention`, not `Founder / Persona → Truth or Policy Immunity`.
- `Reference Stewardship → Maintained Reference`, not `Reference Stewardship → Monopoly on Reality`.
- `Many Citations → Apparent Support`, not `Many Citations → Independent Evidence` unless lineage supports independence.

Firewalls should be auditable and testable at implementation level, not only stated as principles.

---
# Counterpowers, Legibility, Provenance, and Auditability

A minimum counterpower package is:

> **Verification + Contestation + Substitution + Exit**

Power should also be legible enough that affected actors can identify the relevant holder, mechanism, mandate, inputs, outputs, version, and appeal path.

**Provenance** preserves where claims, artifacts, decisions, and transformations came from. **Auditability** allows independent reconstruction or verification.

Transparency is not sufficient by itself. A transparent system that cannot be contested or substituted can remain dominating. Complexity is not inherently illegitimate either; the problem is complexity functioning as an unanswerable source of authority.


## Rehearsed counterpower

Counterpower can be formally available yet socially unusable. A community may possess appeal, fork, exit, or objection mechanisms that no one knows how to activate against a prestigious internal authority.

Power Dynamics therefore distinguishes **available counterpower** from **rehearsed counterpower**. Deliberate drills can create procedural memory, lower the symbolic cost of dissent, expose coordination failures, and establish legitimate opposition as normal system behavior.

```text
formal right
→ practiced procedure
→ remembered precedent
→ lower activation cost
→ stronger effective counterpower
```

# Federation and Subsidiarity

Federation coordinates independently governed nodes through shared contracts rather than requiring a flat or centerless architecture.

Subsidiarity places decisions at the lowest level capable of handling them effectively while preserving necessary coordination.

The repo rejects `federation = no center`. Strong local hubs, shared standards, and central reference services can coexist with local sovereignty if substitution and independent operation remain meaningful.

scope: "GENERAL"
version: "6.1"
title: "Founder Decentering"
source_basis:
  - S18
  - S25
---
# Founder Decentering

Founder power can arise from authorship, vocabulary, repositories, domains, brand, relationships, keys, architecture knowledge, and interpretive authority.

The goal is not necessarily founder removal. It is independent reproducibility:

> **Founder → Architecture → Distributed Mastery**

rather than:

> **Founder → Permanent Interpretive Dependency**

Useful phases include founder sovereignty, constitutional constraints, maintainer pluralism, institutional federation, and protocol-level continuity.


## Power redirection, not only founder limitation

Founder decentering can be designed as a productive conversion rather than simple loss of control:

```text
founder knowledge
→ documentation / schemas / protocols
→ shared implementation capacity

founder infrastructure
→ portable deployments / federation
→ local operational autonomy

founder-created credibility mechanism
→ interoperable Koali distributions
→ plural credibility capacity

founder narrative
→ published ethical constraints
→ public self-binding and reinterpretability
```

This is **power redirection**: leverage that could remain attached to the founder is converted into capability that other actors can reuse independently.


## Founder fallibility and opposition rehearsal

Independent reproducibility is necessary but not sufficient. A community can reproduce the system technically while remaining socially dependent on the founder's interpretive or symbolic authority.

The stronger criterion is:

> **The system can continue when the founder is absent, and it can correct the founder when the founder is present and wrong.**

This adds a distinct transition:

```text
founder centrality
→ published limits
→ practical contestation
→ rehearsed opposition
→ precedent of legitimate disagreement
→ lower symbolic dependency
```

Founder error should be treated as an expected long-run condition, not an exceptional edge case. A durable system should therefore make disagreement, amendment, branch formation, and opposition procedurally and culturally usable before they become necessary in a high-stakes crisis.

# Non-Domination and Non-Terminality

Non-domination means more than absence of current interference. A relationship is problematic when an actor remains exposed to discretionary power it cannot meaningfully verify, contest, substitute, or exit.

The repository extends this into **non-terminality**: capture of one institution, branch, deployment, or maintainer should not automatically make the broader ecosystem unrecoverable.

`Instance Capture ≠ System Capture` is therefore a design objective, not a guarantee.

# Power Redirection and Sharing

The Power Constitution is not only defensive.

It does not wait for a powerful center to emerge and then attempt to constrain it. It also asks how architecture can **redirect leverage before it becomes terminal**.

## Constitutional objective

> **Generate strong distributed puissance, route its returns and control deliberately, and prevent useful coordination from becoming exclusive sovereignty.**

## Six constitutional mechanisms

### 1. Conversion firewalls

Prevent one legitimate form of capability from silently becoming unrelated authority.

### 2. Power routing

Specify where capability, authority, returns, and optionality are intended to accumulate.

### 3. Self-binding

Make principles, procedures, limits, and revision rules public enough that later behavior can be compared against them.

### 4. Protocolization

Translate unique personal or institutional mastery into reusable, testable, and independently implementable forms.

### 5. Devolution and plurality

Permit local operation, multiple distributions, branches, reader policies, or implementations rather than one mandatory operator.

### 6. Counterpower

Preserve verification, contestation, substitution, exit, forkability, and branch mobility when routing fails or capture occurs.

## Public ethics as a power mechanism

Publishing ethical principles in a symbolic or fictional layer is politically relevant when the symbolic layer itself creates influence.

The mechanism is:

```text
narrative puissance
→ explicit public commitments
→ interpretable limits
→ expectations and criticism
→ self-binding of symbolic authority
```

This is stronger than private intention because the commitment becomes externalized and reusable by others.

It remains weaker than enforceable institutional governance unless tied to procedures, audit, recourse, or material control.

## Protocol commons and founder power

A founder may legitimately create, coordinate, steward, or brand a system while still redirecting much of the system's practical capability outward.

The desired transition is:

> **Founder-powered → Architecture-powered → Protocol-powered → Ecosystem-powered**

Independent reproducibility is the key test.

## Koali as a power-sharing test

Credibility infrastructure is especially sensitive because it can affect opportunity, standing, discoverability, and access.

A non-terminal design should make it possible for multiple Koali distributions or compatible implementations to exist without one founder or operator owning the only meaningful credibility layer.

The audit questions are:

- Can another group instantiate the function?
- Can evidence and history move?
- Can policy differ while identity and provenance remain interpretable?
- Can alternative distributions become discoverable and viable?
- Can one operator deny interoperability to all competitors?
- Does the official brand control the protocol, or only a particular distribution?

## Failure modes

Power-sharing language can hide concentration if:

- the code is open but the registry is not;
- forks exist but network access does not;
- principles are public but behavior is not auditable;
- data is portable but identity or reputation is not;
- local hosting exists but updates require one signing authority;
- multiple credibility models exist but one platform controls discoverability;
- democratic allocation exists but capital controls eligibility upstream.

For that reason, power redirection must be assessed as an **effective system property**, not a slogan.


================================================================================================

source_basis:

  - S25
---
# Power Constitution

The constitution exists **because the Power Show succeeds**.

The objective is not to make kOA weak. It is to make strong capability compatible with weak, scoped, and non-terminal sovereignty.

> **Strong puissance. Weak sovereignty.**

A power constitution governs how useful capability may be:

- exercised;
- composed;
- converted;
- concentrated;
- challenged;
- revoked;
- transferred;
- substituted;
- forked;
- replaced.

## Constitutional problem

A successful capability layer can become a gate:

```text
discovery → ranking power
credibility → standing power
workflow → administrative power
identity → access power
standards → compatibility power
memory → precedent power
AI → interpretive power
infrastructure → dependency power
```

The architecture must therefore make emerging power legible before it becomes terminal.

## Constitutional core

- **non-domination and non-terminality**;
- **scoped authority and separation of functions**;
- **power-conversion firewalls**;
- **Verification + Contestation + Substitution + Exit**;
- **revocability, reversibility, portability, and effective exit**;
- **federation and subsidiarity**;
- **stable kernels with evolving branches**;
- **founder decentering without requiring founder erasure**.

Useful centers are compatible with this model. The issue is not centrality itself but whether the rest of the system remains practically capable without permanent submission to that center.

- [`power-redirection-sharing.md`](power-redirection-sharing.md) — redirecting leverage before it becomes terminal and sharing capability through protocolization, plurality, and devolution.

---
maturity: "CURRENT-CORE"
claim_type: "ANALYTICAL-RECONSTRUCTION"
scope: "GENERAL"
version: "6.0"
title: "Revocability, Reversibility, Exit, Fork, and Portability"
source_basis:
  - S16
  - S19
  - S21
  - S25
---
# Revocability, Reversibility, Exit, Fork, and Portability

Revocation removes future authority. Reversal addresses prior effects. They are different.

A ranking rule can be changed while earlier reputational effects persist. A decision-maker can be removed while contracts, debts, or standards remain.

Exit and fork are structural counterpowers only when effective. Portability can include data, knowledge artifacts, identity, credentials, reputation evidence, decision history, operational records, schemas, protocols, and deployment tooling.

A technically forkable project can remain practically monopolistic if forks lose recognition, network access, compatibility, or essential assets.

Constitutional analysis should therefore ask both whether authority can be revoked and whether affected actors can actually migrate, recover, or continue.

scope: "GENERAL"
version: "6.0"
title: "Contributing"
---
# Contributing

Contributions should preserve both faces of the repository:

1. **Power Show clarity** — make concrete what a capability enables;
2. **Power Dynamics rigor** — distinguish capability from the relations of power created around it.

## Before adding a concept

Ask six questions:

1. **What becomes possible?** What practical capability does the mechanism create or amplify?
2. **What is its ontological state?** Resource, potential, capacity, puissance, power relation, effect, or return?
3. **What dynamic is occurring?** Formation, mobilization, composition, conversion, reinvestment, capture, diversification, decay, transfer, etc.?
4. **In which domain does it occur?** Keep political, economic, and monetary domains distinct where relevant.
5. **What can it convert into?** Name important cross-domain conversion paths and firewalls.
6. **Where does the power go?** Identify the destinations of capability, authority, returns, and optionality.

Avoid shorthand such as `money = power`, `expertise = authority`, or `brand = legitimacy`. Trace the mechanism.

## Power Show additions

A new showcase claim should state:

- the baseline limitation;
- what kOA component or mechanism changes it;
- what new puissance becomes available;
- how it composes with other capabilities;
- what power relations can emerge if the capability becomes consequential;
- how the capability or leverage is routed;
- what cross-domain conversions are possible;
- what counterpowers or scope limits apply.

Do not turn aspirational capability into an unsupported deployment claim. Use the repository's maturity and claim-type labels.

## Evidence

A claim should identify at least one of:

- source IDs from [`sources/corpus-map.md`](sources/corpus-map.md);
- direct technical evidence;
- explicit analytical reconstruction;
- external research, clearly labeled.

## Branches and disagreement

Do not rewrite disagreement into artificial consensus. Preserve divergence and state the scope, evidence, trade-offs, and conditions of each claim.

## Metrics

Avoid false precision. Prefer multidimensional assessment to universal scores when constructs are not directly measurable.


## Architect-confirmed design claims

When a current design intention has been confirmed by the project architect but is not yet documented in the canonical corpus or implementation evidence, label it `USER-CONFIRMED-DESIGN` and state what technical evidence is still required.

# Political and Constitutional Power

Political / constitutional power concerns standing, legitimacy, mandate, representation, agenda, decision rights, veto, amendment, jurisdiction, and the authority to bind collective action.

## kOA power surface

kOA can increase political puissance by making publics better able to frame issues, discover evidence, compare proposals, expose advisory readings, make decisions, route implementation, and preserve institutional memory.

The canonical chain is:

> **Meaning → Knowledge → Visibility → Credibility → Agenda → Deliberation → Decision → Execution → Memory**

This makes political power partly **pre-political**: control of categories, discovery, ranking, and credibility can shape what reaches formal decision.

## Main relational powers

- agenda power;
- decision power;
- mandate power;
- veto / amendment power;
- legitimacy power;
- administrative implementation power;
- precedent power.

## kOA safeguards

- political equality as baseline;
- expertise as bounded advice rather than sovereignty;
- explicit lenses and multiple readings;
- scoped authority;
- auditability and recourse;
- branch mobility and institutional alternatives.

## Key conversion risks

```text
credibility → political authority
money → political standing
technical administration → policy control
narrative popularity → legitimacy
```

These are precisely the kinds of conversions Power Conversion Firewalls are meant to expose.

# Economic and Allocation Power

Economic puissance is the effective ability to mobilize scarce material resources for action. Economic power appears when control of resources changes others' access, bargaining position, options, or dependency.

## Resources

- money;
- property;
- productive assets;
- labor and time;
- contracts;
- funding;
- procurement;
- infrastructure;
- philanthropy;
- market access.

## kOA pathways

```text
knowledge → credibility → opportunity → income
network → collaborators → execution → output → funding
brand → attention → affiliation → revenue
money → infrastructure → capability
collective activity → returns → shared assets → greater future capability
```

## Allocation power

Allocation power is the relational power to determine which people, projects, institutions, or goals receive resources.

Zero-Skim is important because it attempts to route:

> **Economic contribution → common resource pool → democratic allocation**

rather than:

> **Economic contribution → proportional political authority**

## Main risks

- donor / funder capture;
- capital → governance conversion;
- infrastructure dependency;
- property bottlenecks;
- returns flowing narrowly to an operator instead of participants.

# Monetary, Credit, and Liquidity Power

Monetary power is not identical to economic power. It concerns control over the infrastructure and conditions of exchange.

## Monetary surfaces

- currency issuance;
- units of account;
- liquidity;
- settlement;
- payment rails;
- reserves;
- convertibility;
- credit access;
- debt terms;
- transaction permissions.

A monetary operator can alter effective possibility without owning all underlying economic assets because other actors depend on the rail itself.

## Why it is high leverage

```text
rail access
→ ability to transact
→ business continuity
→ economic dependency
→ bargaining / political leverage
```

## Legacy stress test

KingCoin legacy proposals combine monetary governance, taxation, redistribution, sanctions, public investment, and dispute enforcement. In current doctrine this is not a recommendation; it is a useful demonstration of **power stacking and terminality risk**.

## Current constitutional lesson

> Powerful monetary functions require especially strong separation, substitution, transparency, and limits on cross-domain conversion.

# Epistemic and Semantic Power

Epistemic puissance is the ability to produce, validate, compare, preserve, and reuse knowledge. Semantic puissance is the ability to structure concepts, categories, relations, translation, and ambiguity.

## kOA mechanisms

- Kristal;
- provenance and lineage;
- trust roots / reader policies;
- SemantiK Architect;
- SenTient;
- multilingual structures;
- competing and complementary Kristals.

## Power relations

- validation power;
- canonization power;
- classification power;
- framing power;
- admissibility power;
- trust-root power;
- apparent-consensus power;
- provenance-obscuration power.

## Core insight

> **To govern meaning is already to govern the collective.**

Definitions affect what can count as harm, evidence, competence, fairness, or action-worthy knowledge.

## Counterpower

Versioning, provenance, alternative readers, competing representations, local semantic governance, portability, and contestation keep epistemic capability from becoming ownership of truth.

A further requirement is **causal-lineage legibility**: source count, citation count, credentials, popularity, and institutional presentation should not be treated as independent evidence when they derive from the same origin or cannot be verified.

# Information, Attention, and Media Power

Information and media puissance includes the ability to publish, package, search, rank, recommend, moderate, synchronize, and amplify signals.

## Relational powers

- visibility power;
- discoverability power;
- agenda power;
- moderation power;
- framing power;
- attention power.

Konnaxion matters because discoverability is not merely convenience. What becomes findable can become reusable, fundable, discussable, and politically salient.

## Risk

A system can preserve formal freedom of speech while controlling practical visibility.

## Counterpower

Explicit discovery policies, alternative ranking/discovery layers, portability, federation, transparency, and branch discoverability reduce terminal control of attention.

source_basis:

  - S25
  - S28
---
# Credibility, Reputation, and Certification Power

Credibility infrastructure makes evidence about expertise, contribution, conduct, or reliability legible. That is useful capability, but it can become standing power.

## kOA mechanisms

- Koali;
- EkoH;
- evidence histories;
- domain-bounded policies;
- SmartVote advisory readings;
- certification / attestation structures.

## Main distinction

> **Score ≠ person ≠ competence ≠ ethics ≠ truth.**
>
> **Credential claim ≠ verified credential. Popularity ≠ evidence. Citation count ≠ independent confirmation.**

A system should produce governed readings of evidence, not claim perfect measurement of human worth.

## Conversion risks

```text
credibility → visibility
credibility → opportunity
credibility → funding
credibility → political influence
```

These can be useful, but should not silently become universal authority.

## Routing direction

Koali is intended to move toward plural distributions, portable evidence, explicit policy identity, interoperability, and independent operation rather than terminal founder ownership.


## Authority-signal corruption

Credibility systems are vulnerable when surface signals can be converted into standing without tracing their basis. Titles, publication formats, follower counts, endorsements, and citation volume can all create useful heuristics, but they can also be manufactured, inherited, or recursively amplified.

The constitutional objective is not to eliminate heuristics. It is to prevent **prestige signals from silently becoming truth claims** and to preserve enough provenance for independent evaluation.

Network puissance is the ability to mobilize relationships for access, diffusion, recruitment, coalition, collaboration, or action.

- relationships;

- brokerage positions;
- audiences;
- institutional access;
- affiliation with brands or communities.

```text
knowledge → contribution → recognition → network → opportunity → execution
```

## Relational powers

- brokerage power;
- inclusion / exclusion power;
- affiliation power;
- recruitment power;
- access power.

Network power compounds because every successful collaboration can create new future pathways.

# Administrative and Operational Power

Administrative puissance is the ability to route, assign, schedule, validate, escalate, record, and close work. Operational puissance is the ability to turn decisions into coordinated execution.

## Orgo surface

```text
decision
→ task
→ owner
→ routing
→ deadline
→ escalation
→ action
→ closure
→ memory
```

## Relational powers

- assignment power;
- timing power;
- approval power;
- escalation power;
- closure power;
- workload power.

## Political significance

Administration can change outcomes without formally changing policy. Therefore:

> **Execution ≠ mandate redefinition.**

Scoped roles, audit trails, appeal, revocability, and explicit decision-to-work boundaries are constitutional requirements.

# Technical and Infrastructural Power

Technical puissance is the ability to build, modify, verify, deploy, repair, and reproduce systems. Infrastructural puissance is capability created by reliable shared systems and protocols.

## Power surfaces

- code repositories;
- signing keys;
- identity rails;
- registries;
- APIs;

- deployment topology;
- hosting;
- update channels;
- compatibility rules.

## Relational powers

- access power;
- compatibility power;
- update power;
- dependency power;
- standards power.

## kOA routing direction

Self-hosting, offline capability, capsules, verification, rollback, federation, portability, and substitution seek to convert infrastructure into **local capability rather than terminal operator dependency**.

> **Instance capture ≠ system capture.**

scope: "GENERAL"

title: "Narrative, Symbolic, and Brand Power"
source_basis:
  - S02
  - S04
  - S23
---
# Narrative, Symbolic, and Brand Power

Narrative puissance makes complex systems memorable, transmissible, culturally legible, and capable of affiliation. Brand puissance can convert recognition into attention, trust, distribution, commerce, or coordination.

## Conversion chains

```text
narrative → attention → affiliation → mobilization
brand → recognition → merchandise / funding → infrastructure
symbol → memory → identity → network
```

## Relational risks

- charisma replacing procedure;
- brand becoming truth;
- symbolic centrality becoming founder worship;
- trademark control becoming protocol control.

## Ethical self-binding

The source fiction explicitly publishes a living ethical code, vigilance against monopoly and domination, recognition and shared merit, self-mockery, criticism, revision, and non-dogmatic transmission.

This is treated as **narrative self-binding**: the channel that creates symbolic power also carries public constraints on that power.

Narrative is legitimate as **function, not sovereign**.

---
maturity: "CURRENT-CORE"

title: "Cognitive and AI-Mediated Power"
source_basis:
  - S13
  - S16
  - S25
---
# Cognitive and AI-Mediated Power

Cognitive puissance is the effective ability to reason, synthesize, model, plan, generate, compare, interpret, and learn.

AI can multiply cognitive throughput, especially when combined with structured knowledge, provenance, memory, and workflow.

```text
AI + Kristal + policy + human judgment
→ higher cognitive throughput
```

## Risks

- hidden framing;
- silent prioritization;
- opaque synthesis;
- model dependency;
- AI-generated defaults becoming policy;
- cognitive convenience becoming hidden sovereignty.

## Principle

> **AI assists under governance; it does not silently acquire governance.**

The key question is not whether AI is powerful. It is whether its cognitive amplification remains subordinate to explicit authority and reconstructible evidence paths.

---
# Mnemonic and Temporal Power

Mnemonic puissance is the ability to preserve and reuse reasons, knowledge, decisions, actions, failures, provenance, and outcomes across time.

## Compounding role

```text
action
→ experience
→ memory
→ lower future coordination cost
→ better action
```

Memory is therefore a production factor, not merely an archive.

## Relational power

Control of memory can shape:

- precedent;
- institutional interpretation;
- which failures are remembered;
- which actors receive recognition;
- what counts as historical continuity.

Temporal power also includes control over timing, delay, deadlines, release, expiry, succession, and disclosure.

## Counterpower

Provenance, versioning, portable history, alternative representations, succession planning, and release optionality make temporal power more governable.

---
# Evolutionary Power and Counterpower

Evolutionary puissance is the practical ability to improve, fork, substitute, recombine, or replace systems and institutions.

This is not merely emergency resistance to corruption. It is a normal mechanism of institutional experimentation.

```text
imperfection / opportunity
→ criticism
→ variation
→ demonstration
→ comparison
→ support
→ coexistence / merge / replacement
→ further variation
```

## Core counterpowers

> **Verification + Contestation + Substitution + Exit**

## Branch mobility

Formal forkability is insufficient. A better branch must be able to:

- preserve data and provenance;
- remain interoperable;
- gather maintainers and support;
- become discoverable;
- operate independently;
- influence or replace a dominant branch.

The goal is **improvement without final authority**.

source_basis:
  - S01
