---
# Property and Territorial Power

Property and territorial power arise from enforceable control over scarce physical assets, land, facilities, infrastructure locations, housing, intellectual property, or jurisdictional access.

This domain is less specific to current kOA software than other domains, but it remains necessary in a general systems theory of power because digital capability ultimately depends on material substrates.

## Conversion chains

```text
property → exclusion / access → economic leverage
physical infrastructure → service continuity → dependency
IP rights → licensing conditions → technical / economic leverage
jurisdiction → legal authority → operational constraints
```

## kOA relevance

Power Dynamics should track whether supposedly distributed systems remain materially dependent on a small set of datacenters, institutions, domains, signing credentials, trademarks, or physical operators.

Formal software openness does not eliminate material bottlenecks.

# Power Domains

Domains answer **where** resources, capability, puissance, and relational power operate.

Version 6 separates domains that were previously grouped too loosely, especially:

> **Political ≠ Economic ≠ Monetary**

The distinction matters because the mechanisms, mandates, risks, and counterpowers differ.

## Domain map

| Domain | Core question | Detailed page |
|---|---|---|
| Political / constitutional | Who can decide, bind, represent, amend, veto, or define legitimate public standing? | [`01-political-constitutional.md`](01-political-constitutional.md) |
| Economic / allocation | Who can mobilize scarce material resources and decide where they go? | [`02-economic-allocation.md`](02-economic-allocation.md) |
| Monetary / credit / liquidity | Who controls exchange rails, issuance, settlement, reserves, liquidity, or credit conditions? | [`03-monetary-credit.md`](03-monetary-credit.md) |
| Epistemic / semantic | Who can shape what is knowable, valid, categorized, translated, or admissible? | [`04-epistemic-semantic.md`](04-epistemic-semantic.md) |
| Information / attention / media | Who can make things visible, discoverable, amplified, ranked, or ignored? | [`05-information-attention-media.md`](05-information-attention-media.md) |
| Credibility / reputation | Who can shape standing, trust signals, certification, or opportunity? | [`06-credibility-reputation.md`](06-credibility-reputation.md) |
| Network / affiliation | Who can broker access, recruit, include, exclude, connect, or mobilize relationships? | [`07-network-affiliation.md`](07-network-affiliation.md) |
| Administrative / operational | Who can route, assign, delay, approve, escalate, execute, or close? | [`08-administrative-operational.md`](08-administrative-operational.md) |
| Technical / infrastructural | Who can build, update, host, sign, standardize, interoperate, or withdraw infrastructure? | [`09-technical-infrastructural.md`](09-technical-infrastructural.md) |
| Narrative / symbolic / brand | Who can shape memory, affiliation, framing, identity, or symbolic legitimacy? | [`10-narrative-symbolic-brand.md`](10-narrative-symbolic-brand.md) |
| Cognitive / AI | Who or what can shape reasoning, synthesis, modeling, and automated interpretation? | [`11-cognitive-ai.md`](11-cognitive-ai.md) |
| Mnemonic / temporal | Who controls memory, precedent, timing, release, delay, expiry, or succession? | [`12-mnemonic-temporal.md`](12-mnemonic-temporal.md) |
| Evolutionary / counterpower | Who can contest, substitute, fork, recombine, and replace? | [`13-evolutionary-counterpower.md`](13-evolutionary-counterpower.md) |
| Property / territorial | Who controls scarce physical or legal assets and the right to exclude? | [`14-property-territorial.md`](14-property-territorial.md) |

## Domain rule

Avoid statements such as “X is more powerful than Y” without specifying:

> **powerful in what domain, through what resource, capacity, relation, mechanism, duration, conversion path, and counterpower environment?**

## Cross-domain analysis

The most important questions often concern transitions between domains:

```text
knowledge → credibility
credibility → visibility
visibility → agenda
money → infrastructure
infrastructure → dependency
brand → affiliation
administration → de facto policy
```

See [`../framework/10-cross-domain-power-conversion.md`](../framework/10-cross-domain-power-conversion.md).

# Formation

Formation is the process through which resources become organized capacity and effective puissance.

Typical transitions:

`information → knowledge`

`practice → expertise`

`money → liquid capacity`

`relationships → network capacity`

`code → technical capability`

`rules → institutional capacity`

`memory → lower future learning cost`

Formation has costs: time, attention, education, infrastructure, coordination, verification, maintenance, and trust. Actors with similar resource stocks can therefore have very different effective puissance.

A central kOA ambition is to reduce the loss between dispersed resources and usable collective capacity.

maturity: "CURRENT-CORE"

source_basis:

---
# Mobilization: Reservoir, Release, Flow, and Mediation

Mobilization explains how retained resources or existing puissance become externally effective.

> **Reservoir / Retention → Release → Flow / Throughput → Mediation / Amplification → Relation or Effect**

This is **one dynamic inside Power Dynamics**, not the architecture of the whole field.

- **Reservoir** — accumulated resources retained for possible future use.
- **Release** — disclosure, deployment, spending, publication, delegation, or activation.
- **Flow / throughput** — rate at which resources and puissance become action, distribution, coordination, or effects.
- **Mediation / amplification** — selection, ranking, translation, framing, routing, synchronization, and multiplication.

A large unpublished corpus can have high epistemic stock and optionality while producing little public effect until organized, released, distributed, understood, and reused. Media demonstrate the inverse: very high distribution throughput can create large agenda effects.

# Composition and Generated Puissance

Composition combines distinct capacities to create higher-order capability.

`expertise + local knowledge + coordination → project capacity`

`knowledge + translation + distribution → multilingual reach`

`code + governance + deployment → operational infrastructure`

`money + network + technical capacity → implementation capacity`

The resulting **generated puissance** may exist nowhere individually. This is why collective intelligence can become a new pole of capability.

Composition does not require ownership. Independent systems can compose through explicit contracts. The risk appears when the orchestration layer becomes a necessary bottleneck and silently inherits authority over the resources or decisions being composed.

maturity: "CURRENT-CORE"

source_basis:
  - S15
  - S17
  - S21
  - S22

---
# Conversion and Power-Conversion Firewalls

Conversion is one of the most important dynamics in Power Dynamics.

Examples:

`knowledge → credibility → visibility → opportunity`

`money → infrastructure → distribution → influence`

`brand → attention → affiliation → revenue`

`technical administration → defaults → agenda leverage`

`adoption → dependency → standard-setting power`

The question is not whether conversion exists but **which conversions are visible, legitimate, bounded, and contestable**.

Canonical firewalls:

| Useful conversion | Prohibited automatic conversion |
|---|---|
| expertise → advice | expertise → universal sovereignty |
| money → funding | money → extra votes |
| brand → attention | brand → truth |
| credibility → discoverability | credibility → unquestionable authority |
| administration → maintenance | administration → self-expanding mandate |
| narrative → mobilization | narrative → legitimacy by itself |

Different forms of puissance should remain composable without becoming silently convertible into unrelated power.

maturity: "CURRENT-CORE"

source_basis:

  - S26
---
# Returns, Reinvestment, and Compounding

Action can leave reusable assets behind.

> **Puissance → Action → Result → Return → Reinvestment → Greater Future Puissance**

Returns include money, code, tools, experience, methods, evidence, data, memory, credibility, relationships, brand recognition, standards, and optionality.

The key question is:

> **Who receives the returns generated by collective activity?**

An extractive system may channel collective activity into assets controlled by a narrow owner. An empowering system may return more value to participants, shared infrastructure, public memory, reusable knowledge, or interoperable commons.

Compounding can also entrench incumbents. A healthy loop:

`Contribution → Evidence → Recognition → Opportunity → More Contribution`

can degrade into:

`Recognition → Visibility → Opportunity → Recognition`

where incumbency reproduces itself.

maturity: "CURRENT-CORE"

title: "Concentration, Stacking, and Capture"
source_basis:
  - S10
  - S16
  - S18
  - S21
  - S25
---
# Concentration, Stacking, and Capture

Concentration is multidimensional. An actor can be decentralized economically while centralized technically, or plural in formal governance while dependent on one trust root, identity provider, brand, maintainer, or deployment key.

## Stacking

Stacking occurs when multiple legitimate functions accumulate in one node.

`banking + identity + certification + philanthropy + platform access → cross-domain leverage`

No individual function must be abusive for the stack to create new dependency.

## Capture

Capture occurs when control over one legitimate function is converted into durable influence outside its intended mandate:

> **Functional Control → Cross-Domain Power**

Capture is a gradient, not merely a binary state. Signals include mandate drift, default expansion, dependency growth, weakened substitution, recognition loops, reduced branch mobility, and increased exit cost.

The constitutional aim is not impossible-to-capture systems. It is **non-terminal capture**.

CONTENT_ROLE: knowledge

---
maturity: "CURRENT-CORE"
claim_type: "ANALYTICAL-RECONSTRUCTION"
scope: "GENERAL"
version: "6.1"
title: "Counterpower: Verification, Contestation, Substitution, Exit"
source_basis:
  - S01
  - S16
  - S19
  - S21
  - S25
---
# Counterpower: Verification, Contestation, Substitution, Exit

Counterpower is practical capacity to alter the terms of a power relation.

> **Verification + Contestation + Substitution + Exit**

- **Verification** — inspect what happened and why.
- **Contestation** — meaningfully challenge the claim, decision, ranking, score, rule, or authority.
- **Substitution** — replace the provider, branch, trust root, policy, or infrastructure.
- **Exit** — leave without disproportionate loss of identity, data, reputation, continuity, rights, or capability.

These are gradients, not checkboxes.

Branch systems add **branch mobility**: an alternative must be able not only to exist but to become discoverable, credible, interoperable, supported, and viable.


## Counterpower can be trained

Effective counterpower depends not only on available mechanisms but on practiced activation. Drills can reveal whether participants know how to identify a problem, assemble evidence, coordinate objection, invoke procedures, form alternatives, and preserve the result as institutional memory.

This creates a compounding loop:

```text
contestable event
→ exercised counterpower
→ procedural memory
→ lower future coordination cost
→ stronger future counterpower
```

A system that repeatedly practices legitimate disagreement can become more resistant to later capture even when the later failure is subtler than the original exercise.

source_basis:

---
# Diversification and Evolution

Diversification distributes implementation, interpretation, governance, or capability across multiple actors or branches.

`imperfection / new context / new evidence → variation → lineage → demonstration → comparison → support/adoption → coexistence/specialization/recombination/replacement → further variation`

The goal is not permanent fragmentation. Stable lineage, provenance, shared interfaces, and interoperability can permit divergence while retaining a shared ecosystem.

Distinguish:

- **competition** — branches seek the same role;
- **complementarity** — branches add different useful views or functions;
- **recombination** — later branches integrate useful elements from earlier ones.

Evolutionary governance constitutionalizes the ability to improve.

# Decay, Dissipation, and Loss

Power Dynamics must study loss as seriously as accumulation.

Resources and puissance can decay through obsolescence, forgotten knowledge, declining credibility, network fragmentation, depleted finance, technical debt, unmaintained infrastructure, loss of recognition, legal change, language drift, or increasing coordination cost.

**Dissipation** occurs when useful returns are not captured in reusable form. Institutions may repeatedly solve the same problem because experience remains trapped in staff memory, inaccessible archives, or transient conversation.

Decay can also be constitutionally useful. Time decay, sunset clauses, and periodic revalidation can prevent delegated authority or reputation from becoming permanent.

# Transfer, Succession, and Inheritance

Power and puissance persist across time through transfer mechanisms.

Transfer may involve money, property, credentials, code, repositories, signing keys, administrative roles, authority registries, institutional mandates, brands, knowledge, relationships, and interpretive authority.

Succession is especially important in founder-dependent systems. A system is not independently reproducible if operation, legitimacy, keys, vocabulary, or interpretation remain dependent on one person's unique knowledge or permission.

Distinguish founder authorship from permanent interpretive sovereignty; brand stewardship from protocol sovereignty; maintainer authority from political authority; technical custody from ownership of collective meaning.

FILE: power_dynamics/dynamics/11-externalities-and-scale.md
AUTHORITY: reference
CONTENT_ROLE: knowledge
CONTENT_SHA256: ab8e38798b3fe58b6afb1ccc37c728eebee4236027dd3937567b62d723f1c8d2

---
maturity: "CURRENT-CORE"
claim_type: "ANALYTICAL-RECONSTRUCTION"
scope: "GENERAL"
version: "6.0"
title: "Power Externalities and Scale"
source_basis:
  - S15
  - S16
  - S18
  - S21
  - S25
---
# Power Externalities and Scale

Success can create power even without an explicit decision to concentrate it.

A useful system can become a default. A default can become a standard. A standard can create dependency. A credibility system can become expected by employers. A platform can become the place where discoverability occurs. A brand can become a de facto certification mark.

This is a **power externality**:

`usefulness → adoption → network effects → standardization → dependency / leverage`

Scale also changes the political meaning of mechanisms. A ranking rule affecting 100 users and the same rule affecting 100 million users are not constitutionally equivalent even if the code is identical.

Analyze scale transitions:

- local tool;
- network infrastructure;
- institutional standard;
- ecosystem dependency;
- systemic infrastructure.

At each transition, reassess scope, branch mobility, substitution cost, auditability, and conversion firewalls.

FILE: power_dynamics/dynamics/12-routing-sharing-and-devolution.md
AUTHORITY: reference
CONTENT_ROLE: knowledge
CONTENT_SHA256: 5379152d1e96c65af324734bc2206646364699b2dc65d341bc4074a8ded68212

---
maturity: "CURRENT-CORE"
claim_type: "ANALYTICAL-RECONSTRUCTION"
scope: "GENERAL"
version: "6.0"
title: "Routing, Sharing, and Devolution"
source_basis:
  - S02
  - S14
  - S15
  - S16
  - S17
  - S25
---
# Routing, Sharing, and Devolution

Routing is the dynamic through which newly generated capability, authority, returns, or optionality are directed toward particular actors, levels, protocols, or communities.

## Canonical pattern

```text
new capability / leverage
→ default locus of accumulation
→ routing mechanism
→ redistributed capability / authority / returns / optionality
```

Examples:

```text
founder mastery
→ protocolization
→ distributed mastery
```

```text
expertise
→ bounded advisory channel
→ informed public judgment
```

```text
infrastructure
→ self-hosting / federation
→ local continuity and substitution
```

```text
money
→ common pool
→ democratic allocation
```

```text
symbolic attention
→ explicit ethical self-binding
→ public grounds for criticism and reinterpretation
```

## Sharing

Sharing occurs when multiple actors gain effective use of a capability or right without requiring one terminal owner of the function.

Sharing should be tested practically, not only legally:

> Can others actually understand, operate, reproduce, adapt, and continue the capability?

## Devolution

Devolution is a durable movement of effective control from a central locus toward local, plural, or lower-level loci.

It can involve:

- local semantic governance;
- self-hosted infrastructure;
- distributed maintainership;
- community-run Koali distributions;
- branch autonomy;
- local decision rights;
- portable evidence and memory.

## Failure modes

- nominal openness with impractical replication;
- distributed software but centralized identity;
- portable data but non-portable credibility/history;
- self-hosting without discoverability;
- forkability without branch mobility;
- shared capability with centralized authority;
- shared authority with centralized returns.

Routing therefore must track **capability, authority, returns, and optionality separately**.

  - S21
  - S25
  - S29
---
# Propagation and Coupled Loops

Compounding explains how returns increase future capability.

Propagation explains how those returns can activate capability **elsewhere**.

> **Compounding deepens capability. Propagation spreads or activates capability. Coupled propagation lets several loops exchange returns.**

A general sequence is:

```text
resource / capability A
→ action
→ return X
→ transfer / reuse
→ lower activation barrier for B
→ capability B
→ return Y
→ reinvestment into A or C
```

Propagation therefore extends the dynamic sequence after returns and compounding:

> **Formation → Mobilization → Composition → Conversion → Effects → Returns → Compounding → Propagation → Concentration / Contestation → Diversification / Evolution → Routing / Sharing**

The primary risks are:

- positive feedback producing capture;
- cross-loop hidden dependencies;
- prestige / credibility self-reproduction;
- standards or interface choke points;
- founder bottlenecks;
- network effects that reduce exit;
- growth in content or integration complexity faster than validation capacity.

The constitutional target is not weak capability. It is:

> **distributed compounding with bounded sovereignty and practical exit.**

See [`../PROPAGATION.md`](../PROPAGATION.md).

title: "Power Dynamics: Dynamic Processes"
source_basis:
  - S01
  - S02
  - S13
  - S18
  - S25
  - S26
---
# Dynamic Processes

The Power Show answers **what kOA can make possible**. This directory explains **how capability and power change over time**.

The main dynamic sequence is:

> **Formation → Mobilization → Composition → Conversion → Effects → Returns → Reinvestment → Compounding → Propagation → Concentration / Contestation → Diversification / Evolution → Decay / Transfer → Routing / Sharing**

## 1. Formation
How resources become potential, capacity, and eventually effectively mobilizable puissance.

## 2. Mobilization
How retained stocks are released, routed, distributed, mediated, amplified, or blocked. `Reservoir → Release → Flow → Mediation` is a mobilization model, not the whole theory.

## 3. Composition and generated puissance
How distinct capabilities combine to create higher-order individual or collective capability.

## 4. Conversion and firewalls
How one resource, capability, or relational advantage becomes another — and where conversion should be constrained.

## 5. Returns, reinvestment, and compounding
How action leaves reusable assets that increase or redistribute future capability.

## 6. Concentration, stacking, and capture
How several legitimate functions or returns accumulate into cross-domain leverage.

## 7. Counterpower, contestation, and exit
How actors alter the terms of power through verification, challenge, substitution, refusal, exit, or fork.

## 8. Diversification and evolution
How variants emerge, demonstrate value, gather support, coexist, recombine, or replace dominant branches.

## 9. Decay, dissipation, and loss
How reputation, network position, knowledge relevance, infrastructure, liquidity, legitimacy, or organizational capacity erode.

## 10. Transfer, succession, and inheritance
How resources, authority, keys, mandates, memory, and stewardship move across actors and generations.

## 11. Externalities and scale
How a useful mechanism can acquire systemic power because adoption, network effects, standards, or infrastructure dependence grow around it.

## 12. Routing, sharing, and devolution
How newly generated capability, authority, returns, and optionality are deliberately directed away from default concentration and toward participants, communities, protocols, local operators, or viable alternatives.

The sequence is recursive rather than linear. Returns from one cycle become resources for another.

## 13. Propagation and coupled loops
How returns from one actor, branch, or deployment lower activation barriers elsewhere, and how several feedback loops exchange assets. Propagation is distinct from local compounding and must be audited for hidden dependency and runaway feedback.

# What Power Dynamics Studies

Power Dynamics has a deliberate double purpose.

First, it is a **map of capability**: what can an architecture such as kOA make individuals, groups, organizations, and federations effectively able to do?

Second, it is a **systems theory of social consequence**: what happens when those capabilities alter access, visibility, standing, allocation, dependency, authority, interpretation, coordination, or future possibility for others?

The field therefore begins with two questions:

> **What can this actor or system effectively do?**

and:

> **Whose possibilities change because it can do that?**

The first concerns **puissance**. The second concerns **power**.

## Why kOA is a useful primary case

kOA intentionally spans a large portion of the knowledge-to-action chain:

> **Meaning → Knowledge → Visibility → Credibility → Deliberation → Decision → Execution → Memory**

That makes it useful for studying how many forms of capability can compose:

- semantic;
- epistemic;
- cognitive;
- network;
- reputational;
- political / constitutional;
- economic / allocation;
- monetary / credit / liquidity;
- deliberative;
- operational;
- mnemonic;
- infrastructural;
- federative;
- evolutionary.

The repo therefore places the **kOA Power Show** in front of the theory. The theory then explains the mechanisms visible in the show.

## Full lifecycle

The canonical ontology is:

> **Resources / Assets → Potential → Capacity → Puissance → Power Relation → Effect → Return**

The dynamic lifecycle is:

> **Formation → Mobilization → Composition → Conversion → Action / Relation → Return → Reinvestment → Compounding → Concentration / Contestation → Diversification / Evolution → Decay / Transfer → Routing / Sharing**

A return can be money, knowledge, code, reputation, network access, credibility, standards, infrastructure, memory, data, legitimacy, dependency, or optionality. These returns can increase or reduce future capability.

## The field is broader than domination

Power Dynamics studies:

- empowerment;
- generated collective capability;
- infrastructure-mediated capability;
- relational influence;
- domination and dependency;
- composition and conversion;
- returns and compounding;
- concentration and capture;
- counterpower;
- plurality and branch evolution;
- decay, succession, and transfer;
- routing, sharing, and devolution;
- cross-domain political, economic, and monetary conversion.

The normative question is not whether a system has power. Any sufficiently useful collective infrastructure will create consequential relations. The question is whether those relations remain **legible, scoped, contestable, substitutable, revocable, portable, and non-terminal**.

title: "Core Ontology"
source_basis:
  - S01
  - S13

  - S18
  - S21
  - S25
---
# Core Ontology

The canonical ontology is:

> **Resource / Asset → Potential → Capacity → Puissance → Power Relation → Effect → Return**

This is not a claim that every real process is linear. It is a conceptual separation that prevents category errors.

- **Resource / asset** — something available or accumulated that may support action.
- **Potential** — what could become realistically possible because those resources exist.
- **Capacity** — an organized ability under defined conditions.
- **Puissance** — a capacity that is effectively mobilizable under actual conditions.
- **Power relation** — a relation in which one actor's or system's puissance changes another's effective possibilities.
- **Effect** — the realized change.
- **Return** — a reusable asset created or preserved by the effect.

### Knowledge example

`research corpus → potential understanding → organized expertise → epistemic puissance → advisory/recognition relation → changed decision → credibility + memory`

### Money example

`financial stock → potential purchasing options → liquid capacity → economic puissance → allocation/bargaining relation → funded project → infrastructure + revenue`

The ontology does not make relational power morally negative. Power may enable, coordinate, recognize, protect, allocate, constrain, or dominate.


================================================================================================

---
maturity: "CURRENT-CORE"
claim_type: "ANALYTICAL-RECONSTRUCTION"
scope: "GENERAL"
version: "6.0"
title: "Effective Possibility"
source_basis:
  - S01
  - S16
  - S18
---
# Effective Possibility

The framework's central object is not abstract freedom but **effective possibility**.

A formal option can exist while remaining practically unusable. A person may possess a legal right but lack time, money, information, institutional recognition, language access, transportation, technical capability, or a viable appeal process. Conversely, infrastructure can expand effective possibility by reducing those barriers.

This is why dependency is central. If essential possibilities depend on an actor whose rules are opaque, unilateral, difficult to contest, or costly to exit, that dependency creates relational leverage even if the actor rarely intervenes.

Effective possibility should be assessed through access, affordability, time cost, intelligibility, technical operability, legal recognition, portability, alternatives, coordination cost, reversibility, risk, and institutional acceptance.

The objective is not perfect quantification. It is to make the relevant constraints and enabling conditions **legible**.

source_basis:
  - S18
  - S21

---
# Three-Axis Model

Every Power Dynamics analysis should locate a phenomenon on three axes.

## Axis 1 — Ontological state

`resource → potential → capacity → puissance → power relation → effect → return`

## Axis 2 — Dynamic

Formation, mobilization, composition, conversion, return, reinvestment, compounding, concentration, stacking, capture, counterpower, diversification, branch evolution, decay, transfer, succession, routing, sharing, devolution.

## Axis 3 — Domain

Economic, monetary, political, epistemic, semantic, informational, reputational, attention, network, technical, infrastructural, administrative, operational, narrative, symbolic, brand, cognitive, mnemonic, allocation, certification, property, territorial, and others.

A media organization can therefore be strong in distribution and agenda power while a state is stronger in legal enforcement. “More powerful” without a domain and mechanism is incomplete.

# Power Ecology

A **power ecology** is a system in which resources, forms of puissance, relations of power, returns, dependencies, and alternatives continuously interact.

The kOA Power Show is best understood ecologically because no single capability explains the whole effect.

## Capability ecology

Typical capability-generating chains include:

`knowledge → understanding → judgment → action`

`network → collaborators → coordination → collective puissance`

`memory → lower relearning cost → better future judgment`

`AI + provenance + human governance → higher cognitive throughput`

`portable infrastructure → substitution → stronger exit capacity`

## Conversion ecology

Typical cross-domain chains include:

`knowledge → credibility → visibility → opportunity`

`execution → results → evidence → credibility`

`brand → attention → affiliation → revenue`

`money → infrastructure → reach → influence`

`technical control → defaults → dependency → agenda leverage`

These chains can be productive, extractive, regenerative, or dangerous depending on who receives returns and whether conversions remain scoped.

## Feedback ecology

Power Dynamics therefore asks:

- what resources are accumulated;
- what puissance they support;
- how capabilities compose;
- how they are mobilized;
- what power relations emerge;
- what effects and returns are produced;
- where returns accumulate;
- which nodes become bottlenecks;
- what dependencies form;
- which counterpowers remain effective;
- whether alternatives can survive and evolve.

## kOA as a designed ecology

kOA deliberately distributes capability across semantic, epistemic, cognitive, social, political, economic, administrative, operational, mnemonic, technical, and federative layers. The design challenge is to obtain the **composition gain** without allowing one layer to silently convert its useful function into total cross-domain authority.

The constitutional aspiration is therefore:

> **Maximum composable capability; minimum terminal sovereignty.**

## Routing ecology

A power ecology is shaped not only by where capability emerges but by where it is deliberately sent. kOA therefore tracks four routing dimensions: **capability, authority, returns, and optionality**.

A regenerative ecology routes useful returns toward broader future capability while keeping high-leverage authority scoped and contestable.

# Pre-Political Power

Formal decisions are only one stage in a longer chain:

> **Meaning → Knowledge → Visibility → Credibility → Agenda → Deliberation → Decision → Execution → Memory**

Each stage is a locus of puissance and potentially of relational power.

Definitions shape what can be perceived. Validation shapes credibility. Discovery shapes visibility. Ranking shapes attention. Agenda setting shapes what enters decision. Administrative routing shapes what becomes executable. Memory shapes what later actors inherit.

A system can preserve one-person-one-vote while still concentrating major influence upstream in search, ranking, classification, moderation, recommendation, defaults, identity, or workflow routing.

Power Dynamics therefore studies the entire mediation chain rather than treating the ballot or final command as the only politically meaningful event.

# Stock, Capacity, Flow, and Return

An earlier useful distinction is preserved in refined form.

- **Stock** — what has accumulated.
- **Capacity** — what the actor or system is organized to do.
- **Puissance** — what it can effectively mobilize under current conditions.
- **Flow** — the practical movement or throughput of resources, action, information, or effects.
- **Return** — what comes back into the system and can be reused.

The combined model is:

> **Stock → Potential → Capacity → Puissance → Flow / Relation → Effect → Return → New Stock**

This is especially useful for compounding analysis. A large stock can remain politically weak if it cannot be mobilized or converted; a small initial stock can compound through code, experience, methods, reputation, and network assets.

---
# Imperfection, Readings, and Plurality

Power Dynamics assumes that consequential human and institutional judgments are imperfect.

Competence, ethics, credibility, legitimacy, truth, branch quality, and social value cannot be captured perfectly by one score, authority, or implementation. This is not a reason to avoid evaluation. It is a reason to make evaluation **explicitly governed**.

`phenomenon ≠ evidence ≠ policy interpreting evidence ≠ reading/score ≠ institutional use of that reading`

This permits multiple branches or readings without requiring relativism. A branch may be better supported on one dimension and weaker on another. Different Kristals may preserve different evidence, contexts, ontologies, or scopes.

The objective is not perfect governance but **governable imperfection**.

---
# Constitutional Thesis

The central constitutional problem is:

> **How can an infrastructure distribute enormous puissance without becoming sovereign over the puissance it distributes?**

The answer is not decentralization in one dimension. It requires scoped authority, separation of functions, conversion firewalls, provenance, verification, contestation, substitution, exit, revocability, reversibility, local operability, federation, branch mobility, founder decentering, and non-terminal dependency.

The repository's shorthand is:

> **Strong distributed puissance. Weak sovereignty.**

A system can have a strong hub, useful defaults, recognized brands, maintainers, registries, and standards. The issue is whether those centers can silently convert functional centrality into unchallengeable cross-domain control.

> **Architecture is governance before governance is written.**


## From restraint to routing

The constitution should not be understood only as a brake on successful capability.

It also governs **where the benefits and leverage of capability go**.

> **Generate strong puissance → route it deliberately → bind authority → preserve counterpower → keep alternatives viable.**

This is why protocolization, public ethical self-binding, Koali plurality, democratic allocation, portability, self-hosting, and branch mobility belong to the constitutional architecture rather than only to product design.

---

title: "Power Routing and Sharing"

# Power Routing and Sharing

Power Dynamics studies not only the **generation** and **constraint** of power, but its **routing**.

> **Power routing is the explicit design of where generated capability, authority, returns, and optionality are allowed to accumulate.**

This adds a missing question to ordinary power analysis. It is not enough to identify a source of power and ask how it is limited. A system can often redirect the same source into a different institutional destination.

## Core terms

### Power redirection

A deliberate change in the default destination or conversion path of leverage.

Examples:

- expertise → advice rather than sovereignty;
- capital → shared funding rather than extra votes;
- infrastructure → user autonomy rather than operator dependency;
- founder knowledge → protocol rather than permanent personal gatekeeping.

### Power sharing

The creation of conditions under which multiple actors can effectively use a capability or participate in a decision-right without requiring exclusive ownership by one actor.

Sharing can concern capability, authority, returns, or optionality.

### Power devolution

A durable transfer of effective control from a central locus toward local, plural, or lower-level loci.

### Self-binding

A mechanism by which a powerful actor or institution publishes, encodes, or accepts constraints that make later exercises of power more legible, contestable, or limited.

### Protocolization

The conversion of tacit, founder-centric, or institution-centric capability into explicit contracts, schemas, specifications, portable artifacts, and reproducible behavior that others can implement or operate.

### Ownership decoupling

The separation of a useful function from exclusive ownership of the implementation, brand, registry, or operator that first instantiated it.

## Routing dimensions

| Dimension | Question |
|---|---|
| Capability | Who can actually do more because the system exists? |
| Authority | Who can approve, validate, rank, route, certify, update, or decide? |
| Returns | Who receives the assets generated by successful action? |
| Optionality | Who can refuse, leave, substitute, fork, self-host, or wait? |
| Interpretation | Who can define categories, credibility policies, readings, and meanings? |
| Visibility | Who controls discoverability, ranking, attention, and agenda? |

A system is not meaningfully power-sharing merely because one dimension is open. Open code can coexist with closed identity, centralized ranking, proprietary credibility, or terminal network effects.

## Redirection matrix

| Concentration vector | Redirection pattern |
|---|---|
| founder mastery | protocolization + documentation + maintainer pluralism |
| brand / narrative | public ethical self-binding + architecture-first references |
| credibility | plural distributions + portable evidence + contestable policy |
| expertise | bounded advisory influence + preserved political baseline |
| knowledge validation | provenance + reader plurality + competing artifacts |
| infrastructure | portability + self-hosting + federation + substitution |
| capital | contribution separated from political standing |
| incumbent branch | branch mobility + compatibility + demonstrable replacement |
| AI cognition | explicit policy + provenance + human-governed authority boundaries |

## Self-binding is not self-certification

Publishing principles is not proof that they are followed.

A self-binding mechanism becomes stronger when commitments are:

- explicit;
- stable enough to cite;
- connected to procedures;
- auditable against behavior;
- revisable through a visible process;
- usable by critics and participants as grounds for contestation.

This distinction is essential for narrative ethics. Ethical principles inside fiction can constrain symbolic drift by making the narrative carry its own limits, but they do not replace institutional safeguards.

## Sharing without flattening

Power sharing does not require identical influence or identical expertise.

The target is **non-terminal asymmetry**: differences may exist, but affected actors retain meaningful routes to understand, contest, substitute, leave, learn, reproduce, or build alternatives.

## Relation to the constitution

Traditional constitutional design asks how to constrain authority after it forms.

Power routing asks an earlier question:

> **Can the architecture prevent some authority from concentrating in the first place by routing capability somewhere else?**

The full constitutional sequence becomes:

> **Generate → Route → Scope → Bind → Verify → Contest → Substitute → Exit → Fork → Evolve**


================================================================================================

---
maturity: "CURRENT-CORE"
claim_type: "ANALYTICAL-RECONSTRUCTION"
scope: "GENERAL"
version: "6.1"
title: "Cross-Domain Power Conversion"
source_basis:
  - S01
  - S04
  - S10
  - S13
  - S14
  - S15
  - S16
  - S18
  - S25
  - S26
---
# Cross-Domain Power Conversion

Power is often most consequential when capability or authority in one domain is converted into leverage in another.

## Conversion

A conversion can be represented as:

```text
source resource / puissance
→ conversion mechanism
→ destination resource / puissance / power relation
```

Examples:

```text
knowledge → evidence of competence → credibility
credibility → ranking / endorsement → visibility
visibility → audience → opportunity
money → purchase / funding → infrastructure
infrastructure → dependency → bargaining leverage
brand → affiliation → revenue
founder / persona → trust → agenda leverage
citation volume → apparent consensus → epistemic authority
reference stewardship → canon visibility → truth-monopoly risk
administrative control → defaults → de facto policy
```

## Cross-domain leverage

Not all conversion is problematic. Many desirable systems depend on it.

The problem begins when:

1. the conversion is hidden;
2. the destination authority exceeds the source mandate;
3. affected actors cannot contest or substitute;
4. the conversion compounds through multiple domains;
5. the resulting leverage becomes durable or terminal.

## Capture

> **Capture occurs when control over one legitimate function is converted into durable influence over functions outside its intended mandate.**

Canonical warning pattern:

> **Functional Control → Cross-Domain Power**

## Power stacking

Power stacking occurs when one actor or node combines several functions whose interactions create leverage not visible when each function is assessed separately.

For example:

```text
identity
+ credibility
+ discovery
+ funding
+ workflow
= cross-domain leverage
```

or:

```text
monetary rail
+ taxation
+ allocation
+ sanctions
+ dispute enforcement
= high terminality risk
```

## Conversion firewalls

A conversion firewall does not prohibit composition. It prevents **automatic authority inheritance**.

Examples:

- expertise → advice, not sovereignty;
- money → funding, not votes;
- brand → attention, not truth;
- credibility → discoverability, not unquestionable authority;
- infrastructure → capability, not ownership of users;
- execution → mandate implementation, not mandate redefinition.

## Analytical procedure

For each important function:

1. Name the source domain.
2. Name the source resource/capability.
3. Identify the conversion mechanism.
4. Name the destination domain.
5. Identify the resulting power relation.
6. Measure dependency and switching cost.
7. Identify returns and compounding.
8. Identify firewall, contestation, substitution, or exit.
9. Test whether the destination authority is within mandate.
10. Test whether capture of the instance would imply capture of the system.

## Relation to the three-axis model

Cross-domain conversion connects the three axes:

- **ontology** — what changes state;
- **dynamic** — conversion / compounding / capture;
- **domain** — where the source and destination operate.

This is why the domain axis must be explicit rather than a loose list of examples.

11. [`10-cross-domain-power-conversion.md`](10-cross-domain-power-conversion.md)

## Core model

> **Resource / Asset → Potential → Capacity → Puissance → Power Relation → Effect → Return**

The broader dynamics study how those states **form, mobilize, compose, convert, return, compound, concentrate, capture, contest, diversify, evolve, decay, transfer, scale, and route**.

The domain axis is detailed in [`../domains/README.md`](../domains/README.md).

# Canonical Glossary

This glossary is normative for repository terminology. It keeps the **kOA Power Show** expressive without allowing broad language such as “powerful” to collapse distinct analytical states. When a term is used differently in a source document, preserve the source meaning and explicitly distinguish it from the repository definition.

## Canonical distinctions

| Do not conflate | Canonical distinction |
|---|---|
| resource / power | a resource supports action; power is relational |
| potential / capacity | potential is unrealized possibility; capacity is organized ability |
| capacity / puissance | capacity can exist without being effectively mobilizable; puissance is effective under real conditions |
| puissance / power | puissance produces effects; power shapes another actor's effective possibilities |
| stock / flow | stock is accumulated; flow is movement or throughput |
| reservoir / power | a reservoir retains resources or options; it does not store relational power as a substance |
| score / competence | a score is a governed reading of evidence, not competence itself |
| credibility / truth | credibility affects trust; it does not establish truth by itself |
| support / evidence | support is social endorsement; evidence supports a claim or evaluation |
| dominance / quality | dominance describes position or adoption; quality is multidimensional and contestable |
| forkability / branch mobility | a fork may be possible while remaining unable to attract users, evidence, interoperability, or institutional support |
| centrality / sovereignty | a hub may be functionally central without possessing total or terminal authority |

### French / English convention

The repository deliberately keeps **puissance** in French even inside English prose when precision matters. English *power* collapses several meanings that this framework separates.

- **potential / potentiel** — unrealized possibility;
- **capacity / capacité** — organized ability;
- **puissance** — effectively mobilizable capacity;
- **power / pouvoir relationnel** — relational shaping of another actor's effective possibilities.

## Power Show terms

**kOA Power Show** — the presentation layer of Power Dynamics that maps the forms of effective capability kOA can generate, compose, preserve, distribute, and evolve before analyzing the power relations created by successful capability.

**Capability spectrum** — the set of distinct forms of potential, capacity, and puissance available to an actor or system across domains. In kOA this includes epistemic, semantic, cognitive, collective, network, credibility, decision, operational, mnemonic, technical, infrastructural, federative, exit, and evolutionary capability.

**Capability loop** — a sequence in which one capability feeds the next. The canonical kOA loop is `Know → Choose → Act → Remember → Know better`.

**Closed capability loop** — a capability loop in which outputs are sufficiently preserved and fed back as inputs that the system can learn from prior action rather than repeatedly restarting from near-zero institutional memory. “Closed” does not mean perfectly self-contained.

**Composition engine** — shorthand for the architecture through which distinct capabilities become interoperable inputs to one another, creating higher-order or generated puissance.

**Composition gain** — additional effective capability created because separate functions interoperate. It is the difference between possessing useful components and possessing a coherent capability system.

**Compounding engine** — shorthand for feedback loops in which action produces reusable returns that lower the cost, increase the quality, or expand the scope of future action.

**Capability infrastructure** — technical, semantic, institutional, or procedural infrastructure whose primary effect is to make future action easier, more reliable, more composable, or more reusable.

**Capability surface** — a function or interface through which a system increases what actors can effectively do. A capability surface can later become a power surface if control over it changes other actors’ effective possibilities.

**Power surface** — a function, interface, registry, role, default, gate, workflow, standard, or infrastructure boundary through which control can shape other actors’ access, standing, visibility, allocation, timing, interpretation, or ability to act.

**Power surface map** — cross-domain inventory of the capability surfaces, relational power surfaces, controllers, conversion paths, counterpowers, and routing mechanisms of a system.

**Cross-domain power conversion** — transformation through which a resource, capability, or relational advantage in one domain becomes leverage in another domain.

**Conversion path** — explicit sequence connecting a source domain to a destination domain through a mechanism such as funding, ranking, certification, standardization, ownership, or dependency.

**Capability-to-power transition** — the point at which successful puissance becomes relationally consequential for other actors. This transition is central to the repository: the same function can be empowering as capability and politically consequential as a controlled gate.

## Ontology

**Resource** — anything available to an actor or system that can support future action: money, time, knowledge, code, rights, relationships, reputation, data, infrastructure, identity, property, attention, memory, or other conditions.

**Asset** — a resource that has been accumulated, stabilized, owned, controlled, recognized, or made reusable enough to support future action.

**Stock** — an accumulated quantity or body of resources/assets at a point in time. A stock is descriptive; it is not automatically puissance or power.

**Potential** — unrealized effective possibility preserved or created by resources and conditions.

**Capacity** — an organized ability to perform a class of actions under specified conditions.

**Puissance** — capacity that is effectively mobilizable under real conditions to produce effects. Puissance is the preferred term for what English often calls *power to*, *power with*, or *power through* when no relational control over others is implied.

**Power** — a relational capacity, grounded in puissance, through which one actor, group, institution, or infrastructure shapes another's effective possibilities.

**Power relation** — a relation in which another actor's access, options, standing, visibility, allocation, authority, incentives, categories, timing, dependencies, or ability to act are materially changed.

**Effect** — a realized change produced by action, mediation, or a power relation.

**Return** — a reusable asset created or preserved by an action or effect and available for future cycles.

**Effective possibility** — what an actor can realistically understand, choose, refuse, obtain, build, coordinate, contest, preserve, or leave under actual material, institutional, temporal, informational, and social conditions.

## Modes of capability and relation

**Puissance d'agir / power to** — effective ability to accomplish, understand, build, decide, refuse, learn, create, or otherwise act.

**Collective puissance / power with** — effective capability generated through coordination among multiple actors.

**Infrastructure-mediated puissance / power through** — effective capability enabled, stored, routed, amplified, or made reusable through infrastructure.

**Power over** — an asymmetric family of power relations in which one actor can condition, constrain, block, allocate, define, authorize, or otherwise shape another's possibilities. It is not the definition of all power.

**Mutual power** — a reciprocal power relation in which actors alter one another's possibilities in ways that can expand joint capacity.

**Generated puissance** — new effective capacity created by composition that did not exist in any participant separately.

**Latent puissance** — capacity that could be mobilized but is currently inactive because of timing, coordination, information, access, or other constraints.

## Dynamics

**Formation** — the process through which resources become organized capacity and then effective puissance.

**Mobilization** — the activation of available resources or puissance into action, exposure, coordination, or effects.

**Reservoir** — a configuration in which accumulated resources or reusable assets are retained for future activation. A reservoir is not stored relational power.

**Retention** — preserving a resource, asset, option, or capability rather than immediately committing or releasing it.

**Release** — activation, disclosure, deployment, spending, publication, delegation, or other transition from retained resource to active use.

**Release puissance** — effective ability to control the timing, sequencing, form, and channels through which a retained resource is activated.

**Flow** — movement of mobilized resources, signals, actions, or effects through a system.

**Throughput** — the practical rate at which a system can process, deploy, distribute, or convert resources and puissance into effects.

**Mediation** — selection, framing, routing, translation, ranking, interpretation, packaging, contextualization, or transformation between source and affected actors.

**Amplification** — an increase in scale or intensity of an effect relative to the initiating input because of a network, platform, institution, technology, or channel.

**Bottleneck** — a constrained passage that limits throughput or access to a necessary transformation.

**Bottleneck power** — relational power created when an actor controls a passage that others must use to convert resources or puissance into effects.

**Composition** — combining distinct resources, capacities, or forms of puissance to create a higher-order capability.

**Conversion** — transformation of a resource, asset, capacity, puissance, or relational position into another form.

**Conversion pathway** — an identifiable sequence through which one form becomes another, e.g. `knowledge → credibility → visibility → opportunity`.

**Conversion firewall** — a rule or architecture that permits legitimate composition while blocking automatic conversion into unrelated authority.

**Reinvestment** — using returns to create or amplify future capacity and puissance.

**Compounding puissance** — growth in future effective capacity caused by reinvestment and reuse across cycles.

**Power yield** — the set of returns, effects, and relational positions generated by a given exercise of puissance or power. It should not be reduced to a single scalar metric.

**Concentration** — increasing share of relevant resources, puissance, or relational control accumulating in one actor, node, branch, or institution.

**Stacking** — accumulation of several distinct functions in the same actor or node, producing cross-domain leverage not visible in any function separately.

**Capture** — durable conversion of legitimate functional control into influence or control outside its intended mandate.

**Cross-domain conversion** — movement of advantage from one domain into another, e.g. economic assets becoming political influence or technical administration becoming agenda control.

**Diversification** — distribution of capability or governance across multiple actors, implementations, branches, domains, or institutions.

**Decay** — reduction in usefulness, credibility, adoption, capability, legitimacy, liquidity, network position, or other components of puissance over time.

**Dissipation** — loss of potential returns because knowledge, experience, attention, coordination, or other value is not preserved or reused.

**Transfer** — movement of resources, capacity, authority, relational position, or responsibility between actors or institutions.

**Succession** — governed transfer of roles, stewardship, keys, mandates, or interpretive responsibility across time.

## Dependence, domination, and counterpower

**Dependency** — reliance on an actor, system, resource, or infrastructure whose substitution or exit is materially costly, slow, unavailable, or illegible.

**Dependency gradient** — degree of dependency, determined by alternatives, switching cost, time, portability, recognition, rights, knowledge, network effects, and other factors.

**Domination** — exposure to arbitrary or insufficiently contestable relational power, especially where dependency is high and counterpowers are weak.

**Counterpower** — capacity that can verify, contest, substitute, exit, revoke, reverse, fork, or otherwise alter the terms of a power relation.

**Verification** — ability to independently inspect claims, artifacts, processes, identities, signatures, calculations, or compliance.

**Contestation** — ability to challenge a claim, score, decision, rule, ranking, authority, or implementation through a meaningful process.

**Substitution** — ability to replace a provider, branch, implementation, authority root, workflow, or institution with a viable alternative.

**Exit** — ability to leave a relationship or system while preserving enough assets, rights, data, identity, reputation, and continuity for exit to be meaningful.

**Formal exit** — nominal legal or technical ability to leave.

**Effective exit** — practical ability to leave at acceptable cost without disproportionate loss of essential capability.

**Forkability** — ability to create a divergent implementation, policy, ontology, or branch from an existing lineage.

**Effective forkability** — forkability plus the practical ability to operate, interoperate, attract support, preserve provenance, migrate assets, and become viable.

**Revocability** — ability to withdraw authority or permission.

**Reversibility** — ability to undo or repair the effects of an earlier action or authority. Revocability does not guarantee reversibility.

**Terminal power** — relational power whose capture, failure, or abuse cannot be effectively substituted, reversed, contested, or exited.

**Instance capture** — capture of one deployment, organization, branch, or institution.

**System capture** — capture of the broader architecture such that meaningful alternatives cannot continue, interoperate, or emerge.

## Branch ecology and evolutionary governance

**Branch** — a distinct version, implementation, policy, ontology, or interpretation with enough lineage and provenance to be compared with alternatives.

**Lineage** — traceable relationship among versions, forks, sources, transformations, and derived artifacts.

**Branch mobility** — practical ability of a branch to become discoverable, credible, supported, interoperable, adopted, and capable of influencing or replacing dominant branches.

**Dominance** — a branch's current advantage in adoption, support, visibility, compatibility, institutional recognition, network effects, or infrastructure. Dominance does not imply truth or optimality.

**Complementarity** — additional value produced when distinct branches, perspectives, or implementations cover different evidence, contexts, scales, or functions without requiring a universal winner.

**Redundant plurality** — multiple branches that add little new evidence, context, method, function, or interoperability value.

**Meaningful variation** — branch difference that creates identifiable added value or tests a consequential alternative.

**Evolutionary governance** — governance in which consequential rules, policies, interpretations, and implementations remain open to explicit variation, demonstration, comparison, adoption, recombination, amendment, and replacement.

**Stable kernel** — shared minimal contracts, formats, provenance rules, or interoperability requirements that permit divergence without fragmentation.

**Evolving branch** — a branch whose policies, readings, implementations, or governance may change while preserving lineage and interoperability where feasible.

**Recombination** — integration of useful elements from multiple branches into a new branch or later version.

**Replacement** — transition in which an alternative becomes more adopted, authoritative, useful, or institutionally supported than an earlier dominant branch.

## Epistemic, semantic, and credibility terms

**Epistemic puissance** — effective ability to produce, organize, validate, compare, preserve, or reuse knowledge.

**Semantic puissance** — effective ability to create, maintain, translate, contest, and evolve categories, ontologies, definitions, and relations of meaning.

**Semantic power** — relational power created when control over categories, definitions, ontologies, thresholds, or classifications shapes how others can understand, qualify, or act.

**Semantic sovereignty** — practical capacity of a person or community to govern the terms, categories, ontologies, and knowledge structures through which it generates and shares meaning without arbitrary dependence on external authorities.

**Credibility asset** — accumulated evidence or recognition that makes a person, branch, claim, or institution more likely to be trusted in a defined context.

**Credibility signal** — governed reading of evidence about expertise, contribution, conduct, reliability, or other criteria. It is not the person or competence itself.

**Domain-bounded expertise** — expertise whose relevance and influence are limited to explicit domains rather than generalized into universal authority.

**Discoverability puissance** — effective ability to make relevant content, people, or branches findable.

**Discoverability power** — relational power created when control of discovery materially shapes what others see, reuse, fund, cite, learn, adopt, or build.

**Pre-political power** — power exercised through meaning, knowledge, visibility, credibility, agenda, and framing before a formal decision is made.

**Mnemonic puissance** — effective ability to preserve and reuse knowledge, reasons, decisions, actions, failures, and outcomes across time.

**Mnemonic power** — relational power created when control of collective memory shapes future interpretation, precedent, options, or institutional learning.

## Political and constitutional terms

**Political puissance** — effective ability to participate in, shape, organize, or carry collective judgment, agenda formation, decision, amendment, representation, or public action.

**Political power** — relational power to shape political standing, agenda, mandates, decision rights, representation, legitimacy, veto, amendment, or binding collective outcomes.

**Constitutional power** — power to define, amend, interpret, or enforce the rules by which other powers are authorized, limited, contested, or transferred.

**Decision power** — relational power to determine or materially condition which collective option becomes binding or operative.

## Economic, monetary, and allocation terms

**Economic resource** — money, property, goods, productive assets, contractual rights, or other material means.

**Economic puissance** — effective ability to mobilize economic resources for action.

**Economic power** — relational power created when control or mobilization of scarce economic resources materially changes others’ access, bargaining position, dependency, allocation, or effective options.

**Liquidity** — ease and speed with which an asset can be converted into usable resources without excessive loss.

**Liquidity puissance** — effective ability to make resources rapidly usable.

**Allocation power** — relational power to determine which people, projects, institutions, or goals receive resources.

**Monetary puissance** — effective ability to create, access, move, settle, convert, intermediate, or finance value through monetary and credit infrastructure.

**Monetary power** — relational power associated with control over units, rails, issuance, settlement, reserves, access, or conditions of exchange.

**Custodial power** — relational power arising from control over assets held on behalf of others.

**Market-making puissance** — capacity to enable exchange by providing liquidity, matching, standards, custody, or transaction infrastructure.

**Property power** — relational power arising from enforceable control of scarce assets, land, infrastructure, intellectual property, or access rights.

## Information, media, network, and narrative terms

**Information resource** — data, documents, signals, archives, observations, or other transmissible representations.

**Distribution puissance** — effective ability to deliver information, resources, signals, or instructions to relevant actors at scale.

**Reach** — number and relevance of actors that can practically be reached through available channels.

**Reach puissance** — effective capacity to cross geographic, linguistic, network, or institutional boundaries.

**Attention power** — relational power to influence what receives scarce attention.

**Visibility power** — relational power to affect whether and how actors, claims, branches, or events become publicly visible.

**Agenda power** — relational power to influence what enters collective consideration as a problem, priority, or option.

**Framing power** — relational power to shape interpretation by selecting descriptions, categories, narratives, baselines, or causal frames.

**Media puissance** — effective ability to gather, package, distribute, synchronize, and amplify information to an audience.

**Network resource** — relationships, affiliations, pathways, memberships, trust links, or structural positions that can support future coordination.

**Network puissance** — effective ability to mobilize relationships for access, coordination, diffusion, recruitment, or action.

**Affiliation power** — relational effects produced by membership, association, endorsement, exclusion, or visible identification with a network or symbol.

**Narrative puissance** — effective ability to make complex systems understandable, memorable, transmissible, and emotionally or culturally legible.

**Narrative power** — relational power created when narratives shape interpretation, legitimacy, attention, identity, or mobilization.

**Brand asset** — accumulated recognition, associations, expectations, reputation, symbols, and identity attached to a name or mark.

**Brand puissance** — effective ability to mobilize brand recognition into attention, affiliation, trust, distribution, commerce, or coordination.

**Brand power** — relational effects created when brand-mediated trust, attention, certification, or affiliation changes others' opportunities, choices, or standing.

## Technical, infrastructural, administrative, and cognitive terms

**Technical resource** — code, tools, protocols, hardware, schemas, standards, credentials, deployment knowledge, or other technical assets.

**Technical puissance** — effective ability to build, modify, operate, verify, deploy, repair, or reproduce technical systems.

**Infrastructural puissance** — effective ability created by reliable shared systems, protocols, deployment paths, identity rails, data layers, or other infrastructure.

**Infrastructural power** — relational power arising when control over infrastructure materially conditions others' access, options, interoperability, or continuity.

**Administrative puissance** — effective ability to route, assign, schedule, validate, escalate, record, close, and coordinate work.

**Administrative power** — relational power produced when administrative control changes others' timing, workload, access, status, or ability to complete an action.

**Execution puissance** — effective ability to transform decisions into coordinated, traceable action.

**Cognitive puissance** — effective ability to perceive, reason, synthesize, model, plan, generate, or evaluate.

**AI-mediated cognitive puissance** — cognitive capacity amplified through AI tools under explicit governance and human/institutional constraints.

**Hidden sovereignty** — effective decision or policy control exercised by a system that is formally described as merely advisory, technical, or assistive.

## Constitutional and institutional terms

**Authority** — recognized permission or mandate to perform defined actions within a scope.

**Scoped authority** — authority limited by domain, function, duration, jurisdiction, conditions, and review.

**Mandate** — explicit basis, purpose, and boundary of legitimate authority.

**Separation of functions** — design principle preventing one role or component from silently inheriting unrelated functions.

**Functional centrality** — importance of a node to coordination or operation without necessarily implying ownership or sovereignty over the whole system.

**Sovereignty** — ultimate or non-subordinate authority within a defined domain. In this repo, technical centrality and practical dependence can create quasi-sovereign effects without formal sovereign status.

**Subsidiarity** — placing decisions and authority at the lowest level capable of handling them effectively while preserving necessary coordination.

**Federation** — coordination among independently governed nodes or institutions through shared contracts, protocols, or interoperability arrangements.

**Local sovereignty** — practical ability of a local deployment or community to govern critical operations and meanings without requiring permanent permission from a remote authority.

**Protocol commons** — shared technical or semantic substrate whose reuse and interoperability do not require submission to one commercial or political owner.

**Brand stewardship** — governance of official names, marks, certification, and identity separately from protocol ownership or operation.

## Founder and temporal terms

**Founder power** — relational leverage derived from authorship, code control, brand ownership, infrastructure, credentials, narrative authority, interpretation, relationships, or unique knowledge.

**Founder decentering** — progressive reduction of unique founder dependency through documentation, distributed mastery, independent operation, maintainer pluralism, federation, succession, and reproducibility.

**Optionality** — preserved ability to choose among future actions because resources, rights, identity, timing, or commitments have not been irreversibly fixed.

**Temporal optionality** — optionality specifically created by retaining control over timing or disclosure.

**Identity optionality** — preserved ability to reveal, separate, associate, or withhold public identity under different future conditions.

**Release optionality** — preserved ability to decide when and how a retained resource, corpus, asset, or announcement becomes active.

## kOA-specific terms

**kOA** — the broader conceptual, cultural, strategic, governance, and narrative framework.

**kOA Digital Ecosystem** — the operable digital system-of-systems supporting the cycle `Know → Choose → Act → Remember`.

**Kristal** — structured, portable, provenance-preserving knowledge artifact or family of artifacts used to make knowledge auditable, reusable, versioned, and capable of controlled transformation.

**Konnaxion** — public collaboration, circulation, coordination, and commons-facing platform layer.

**Koali** — credibility/reputation infrastructure envisioned as plural distributions around interoperable structures rather than one universal social-credit authority.

**EkoH** — domain-bounded credibility, expertise, ethics, contribution, or trust signal layer, depending on implementation context.

**SmartVote** — decision/discoverability mechanism preserving baseline and advisory/weighted readings separately rather than silently replacing political equality.

**Orgo** — operational workflow and case-management layer connecting decisions to roles, tasks, routing, escalation, closure, and operational memory.

**Capsule** — signed portable deployment/package unit intended to support local operation, verification, rollback, self-hosting, and federation.

**SemantiK Architect** — semantic-architecture function concerned with concepts, categories, relations, contexts, and multilingual structures.

**SenTient** — semantic reconciliation function concerned with ambiguity, entity linking, uncertain propositions, and structured interpretation.

## Compatibility with earlier Power Dynamics shorthand

Earlier drafts used several useful but broader expressions. They remain meaningful if interpreted through the stricter ontology.

**Power stock** — legacy shorthand for accumulated resources/assets plus durable relational positions. Prefer specifying the actual stock rather than treating power as a substance stored in one pool.

**Power flow** — broad shorthand for movement, mobilization, conversion, mediation, or transfer across the system. Prefer identifying what flows: resources, signals, attention, money, authority, puissance, effects, or returns.

**Power return** — shorthand for reusable returns produced by action or power relations. A return may become a resource, asset, relational advantage, or future puissance; it is not necessarily relational power at the moment it returns.

**Power reinvestment** — shorthand for using returns to increase future resources, capacity, puissance, or relational leverage.

**Power regeneration** — shorthand for recurring cycles in which effects are preserved and reused instead of dissipating, creating renewed or expanded puissance.

**Compounding power** — shorthand for cumulative dynamics in which returns increase later relational leverage. Prefer **compounding puissance** when the effect is increased capability without a specified relation to others.

**Power reservoir** — avoid as a strict term. Use **resource reservoir**, **epistemic reservoir**, **financial reserve**, or another domain-specific reservoir. Relational power is not stored like a physical substance.

## Repository shorthand

**Strong distributed puissance, weak sovereignty** — maximize useful effective capability while limiting non-contestable or terminal control.

**Plurality without fragmentation** — allow meaningful divergence while preserving enough shared lineage, contracts, provenance, and interoperability for comparison and recombination.

**Instance capture ≠ system capture** — failure or capture of one node should not automatically make alternatives non-viable.

**Architecture is governance before governance is written** — schemas, defaults, registries, identity systems, workflows, interfaces, and deployment topology already assign and constrain possibilities before formal policy text is applied.


## Power routing and sharing

**Power routing** — explicit design of where generated capability, authority, returns, optionality, visibility, and interpretive leverage are allowed to accumulate.

**Power redirection** — deliberate change in the default destination or conversion path of leverage, such as expertise → advice rather than sovereignty or infrastructure → local autonomy rather than operator dependency.

**Power sharing** — creation of conditions under which multiple actors can effectively use a capability or participate in a right without requiring exclusive ownership by one actor.

**Power devolution** — durable transfer of effective control from a central locus toward local, plural, or lower-level loci.

**Self-binding** — public, procedural, contractual, or technical constraints adopted by an actor or institution that make later exercises of power more legible, contestable, or limited.

**Narrative self-binding** — use of the same narrative or symbolic channel that creates attention and affiliation to publish ethical limits, anti-domination principles, attribution rules, revision norms, or other constraints on symbolic power.

**Protocolization** — conversion of tacit or founder-centric capability into explicit contracts, schemas, specifications, artifacts, tests, and reproducible behavior that others can implement independently.

**Ownership decoupling** — separation of a useful function from exclusive ownership of the implementation, brand, registry, or operator that first instantiated it.

**Capability commons** — a body of reusable capability that multiple actors can access, reproduce, improve, or instantiate without requiring one terminal owner of the function.

**Architect-confirmed design** — a current design intention confirmed in the working process but not yet supported by a canonical source document or direct implementation evidence. It must be labeled separately from source-derived or deployed claims.


## Adversarial resilience terms

**Rehearsed counterpower** — counterpower whose activation pathway has been practiced sufficiently that participants know how to recognize a problem, organize objection, invoke procedures, and preserve the result as institutional memory.

**Founder opposition test** — resilience test asking whether a system can reject a founder-backed proposal while the founder remains influential. Distinct from the founder absence test.

**Epistemic authority corruption** — process by which weak, dependent, misleading, or fabricated authority signals are converted into apparent credibility, consensus, or truth status.

**Apparent source independence** — surface appearance that several sources independently support a claim.

**Causal source independence** — support that arises from genuinely distinct evidentiary lineages rather than repeated dependence on the same origin.

**Worldview fork** — a Kristal branch that intentionally changes assumptions, interpretive rules, or ontological commitments while preserving lineage to the branch from which it diverged.

**Tattoo Protocol** — informal name for a reusable civic counterpower pattern derived from a proposed founder-opposition drill: isolate a problematic proposal, document it, mobilize evidence and expertise, organize contestation, seek correction, and preserve the postmortem as reusable memory.

# From Central Correction to Non-Domination

Some legacy economic proposals imagine very strong global coordination, automated enforcement, sanctions, and monetary controls.

Later current-core texts emphasize semantic sovereignty, local operation, auditability, bounded expertise, multiple readings, federation, recourse, and non-domination.

The evolution can be summarized as a movement from:

> **find a sufficiently just center to correct the system**

toward:

> **design institutions so that even useful centers remain bounded, contestable, substitutable, and non-terminal**.

This is a major constitutional evolution and should remain explicit in the corpus history.

# From Dependency to Empowerment

`Abuser l'humanité` diagnoses how debt, time capture, bureaucracy, justice cost, desire, work, and procedural complexity can shrink effective possibility.

`Hacking Reality` develops the opposite movement: language, symbols, alliances, learning, narrative, and organization can expand possibility.

kOA later formalizes the transition as infrastructure: transform dispersed knowledge and commitment into governed collective action and reusable memory.

Power Dynamics therefore inherits both halves:

> **How systems shrink effective possibility**

and

> **How systems generate effective possibility**.

# Founder Power Across Phases

A possible maturity trajectory is:

1. founder sovereignty;
2. explicit constitutional constraints;
3. maintainer pluralism;
4. institutional federation;
5. protocol-level continuity.

The goal is not to erase authorship. It is to ensure that independent reproduction, operation, interpretation, and branching do not depend on unique founder permission or inaccessible knowledge.

# Genealogy of the Power Model

The corpus shows an evolution rather than one fixed doctrine.

Broadly:

1. **diagnosis of dependency and structural abuse**;
2. **experiments in strong corrective economic institutions**;
3. **language, narrative, alliance, and empowerment**;
4. **knowledge-to-action architecture**;
5. **non-domination, semantic sovereignty, bounded expertise, auditability, federation**;
6. **Power Dynamics synthesis: capability creation plus constitutionalized evolution**.

The repository preserves tensions instead of retroactively making all documents agree.
